# Shadow debt — channel map

Nine channels, each traced by balance-sheet mechanics to answer one question:
does it create purchasing power, or intermediate existing savings?

*21 August 2026. Evidence, not the deliverable — the handbook is in `Shadow_Debt_Measurement_Handbook.md`.*

---

# Private credit and direct lending: funds, BDCs, interval funds, the bank-to-private-credit lending chain, subscription/capital-call and NAV facilities, and fund-level leverage

**Verdict: `contested`**

## What it is

THE INSTRUMENT. A private credit fund is a pooled vehicle (closed-end LP, BDC, or interval fund) whose asset side is bilaterally negotiated, held-to-maturity, floating-rate senior loans to non-investment-grade borrowers, mostly PE-sponsored. FSB's narrow working definition (Report on Vulnerabilities in Private Credit, 6 May 2026, p.3): nonbank direct lending to medium-sized companies negotiated bilaterally. Typical loan: 4-6y maturity, SOFR+~600bp, first-lien, maintenance-covenanted, unrated or privately rated around single-B.

THE FIVE BALANCE SHEETS THAT MATTER. Stack them in order, because the money question is answered differently at each rung:

(1) BORROWER. Assets: operating assets. Liabilities: term loan from the fund, plus in ~50% of cases a revolving credit facility from a *bank* alongside the fund's term loan (FSB s2.2, citing Haque/Mayer/Stefanescu 2025). Leverage 5-7x EBITDA, PIK-toggle usage rising.

(2) FUND. Assets: the loan book, at fair value, marked quarterly with discretion. Liabilities: LP capital (equity), plus one or more of five bank facility types (FSB Table 2):
 - Subscription / capital-call line. 1-3y, ~SOFR+200bp, secured on *uncalled LP commitments* and the GP's right to call. Most common facility type globally.
 - Fund portfolio financing / borrowing-base. ~+225bp. Fund draws dynamically against a haircut-adjusted eligible loan pool. Legally structured as the bank investing in a senior tranche of a securitisation where a fund SPV is declared "originator" — equity tranche 30-40%, few tranches (AA/equity). Banks book it as a securitisation exposure to get a lower risk weight. Largest facility type in the euro area.
 - NAV facility. 3-5y term/revolver, +200-400bp, LTV generally capped ~30% per FSB member data.
 - Asset-based lending via SPV. 5-6y, LTV up to 65%.
 - Repo against credit-linked notes (the SRT recycling loop).

(3) SECURITISATION. Private credit CLO ("middle market CLO"): ~$155bn outstanding Oct 2025, ~16% of the $977bn US CLO market (FSB Box 2, citing BofA). Issuers typically retain the whole equity tranche; banks buy AAA (~+350bp) and provide warehouse lines.

(4) INVESTOR. Pension funds, insurers, SWFs, endowments; increasingly retail via BDCs, interval funds and (since early 2025) private-credit ETFs.

(5) THE RATED-NOTE FEEDER. A feeder issues a rated senior note plus an equity tranche into the same underlying fund. The rating — often a private letter rating from a smaller NRSRO — lets a life insurer carry it on Schedule D as a bond at a low RBC charge instead of Schedule BA at a punitive one. FSB names this explicitly: private ratings "may be used to attract insurers who rely on ratings for regulatory purposes" (Exec Summary). The insurer funds the purchase from annuity and FABN inflows; PE-linked insurers increasingly cede the liability to Bermuda via asset-intensive/funded reinsurance.

THE CIRCULARITY WORTH NAMING. Banks sell synthetic risk transfers; private credit funds are the largest single buyer group of EU bank SRTs (EBA RAR Spring 2025, reproduced as FSB Graph 5); banks then repo-finance the CLNs those funds bought. FSB calls this "circles of risks" (s2.4) — the fund's SRT investment becomes a bank risk via the bank's own funding of the fund.

## Is it money creation?

Answer by tracing deposits, not narrative. Six cases, and they do not all give the same answer.

CASE 1 — LP-funded drawdown, unlevered fund. GP calls capital; a pension fund's $100 deposit moves to the fund's account; the fund lends; the deposit moves to the borrower. Aggregate bank deposits UNCHANGED. The LP swaps a deposit for an illiquid, non-redeemable LP interest. PURE INTERMEDIATION. This is the modal case: OFR (Brief 26-02, Mar 2026) finds the median leverage ratio (gross/net assets) across ~2,000 Form PF private credit funds is 1.04 — effectively no leverage; 25th percentile exactly 1.00.

CASE 2 — Subscription / capital-call line. The bank books +$100 loan asset and +$100 deposit liability to the fund. That is de novo deposit creation on standard Tobin/BoE mechanics. The fund lends; the borrower now holds a deposit that did not exist. It is extinguished 1-3 years later when the GP calls capital and the fund repays. So: MONEY CREATION, BUT REVOLVING AND SELF-EXTINGUISHING. The stock of money created equals the *drawn balance outstanding*, never the flow of originations. It looks permanent only because the aggregate drawn balance grows with the industry and never returns to zero — and because tenors have been lengthening. This is the closest analogue to what the operator observed under ZIRP: not a new money instrument, but a term-extension of a bridge until the bridge is structurally permanent.

CASE 3 — Fund portfolio financing / NAV / ABL. The fund already holds LP-funded loans, pledges them, draws 30-65% LTV, and re-lends. Bank: +loan asset, +deposit liability. The same LP dollar now supports up to ~1.65 dollars of lending, and the increment is bank-created deposit money that is NOT extinguished until the underlying loans amortise. DURABLE MONEY CREATION, collateralised by model-priced, infrequently-marked assets. Note the capital arbitrage: because the euro-area version is booked as a senior securitisation tranche, the risk weight is lower than a plain corporate loan, so deposit-creation-per-unit-of-bank-capital is higher than for direct C&I lending.

CASE 4 — Private credit CLO. Depends entirely on who buys the paper. Bank warehouse line to the CLO, or a bank buying the AAA out of an expanding balance sheet: creation. A pension fund or insurer buying the AAA out of existing assets: reallocation only. CONTESTED, buyer-dependent.

CASE 5 — Rated-note feeder funded by annuity inflows. THE HONEST ANSWER IS NO, AND THIS IS WHERE THE POPULAR STORY IS WRONG. The annuitant surrenders a deposit and receives a policy liability; the deposit travels annuitant → insurer → feeder → fund → borrower. Aggregate deposits are unchanged at every step. A deferred fixed annuity or MYGA carries surrender charges, market-value adjustments and tax penalties on early withdrawal — it is not a means of payment and not par-on-demand. It is not money and not near-money in the M2 sense (fixed annuities are excluded from M2; small time deposits are included, which is the closest analogue). What this chain manufactures is not purchasing power but WILLINGNESS TO PART WITH IT FOR LONGER: it converts a household's demand for safe, nominally-denominated savings into a 5-7 year illiquid corporate loan while leaving the household feeling liquid. That is maturity and liquidity transformation without money creation, and it should be labelled as such rather than inflated into a money-creation story.

CASE 6 — Rated note bought by a bank. Reverts to Case 4 logic: creation.

WHAT THIS MEANS FOR THE OPERATOR'S PUZZLE. Savings rates are flat and other assets are not being sold, yet capex is funded. For this channel the decomposition is:
(a) MOSTLY a reallocation of a given saving flow out of public bonds and bank deposits into locked-up LP interests and annuities. No new money; a large increase in the duration and credit risk that the same savings support.
(b) A REAL BUT BOUNDED amount of genuine bank-money creation, equal to the *drawn* balance of bank facilities to private credit vehicles: $54bn (Fed Y-14, narrow ID, YE2024), $74bn (OFR Y-14, broader ID, YE2024), $264bn (Call Report business-credit-intermediary drawn, 2024, per OFR Fig 1). The $410-540bn OFR headline is bank AND nonbank lending; the nonbank slice (BDC unsecured notes bought by insurers and funds) is intermediation, not creation, so the creation subset is materially smaller than the headline.
(c) A LEVERAGE MULTIPLIER concentrated in the tail. Median fund gross/net 1.04, 75th pct 1.36, 95th pct 3.58; the 95th-100th percentile cohort (104 funds) holds $100bn gross on $19bn net, i.e. $81bn of borrowing — 23% of all Form PF private-credit borrowing from 5% of funds (OFR). BDC debt/equity has tripled from ~30% to >90% over 15 years and is strongly procyclical (BIS Bulletin 106).
(d) THE LARGEST LEG IS NOT MONETARY AT ALL: the borrower's own 5-7x leverage. That expands claims on future output without expanding the money stock.

WHY IT IS INVISIBLE TO M1-M3 — and the answer is not "a shadow money instrument". M2 measures the LIABILITY side of banks. Private credit moves the ASSET side of credit intermediation off banks. Deposits are unchanged, the loan exists, and no monetary aggregate records that a loan was made. The correct aggregate is a total-credit measure, not a money measure — and the standard total-credit apparatus does not capture it either: the Fed's own FSR sizes private credit from Preqin, LSEG BDC Collateral and PitchBook LCD, and uses Z.1 only for the DENOMINATOR (nonfinancial corporate debt). Z.1 has no private credit, BDC or private fund sector; domestic hedge funds and private equity funds sit inside the household-and-nonprofit residual (Z.1, 11 Jun 2026, S14/S15 footnote 1).

VERDICT: predominantly intermediation, with a genuine, measurable, fast-growing money-creation sleeve at the bank-facility rung — concentrated in subscription lines (revolving, self-extinguishing) and fund portfolio/NAV/ABL financing (durable). The annuity-insurer-rated-note leg, where most commentary places the money creation, does not create money.

## Size

Every figure below is labelled STOCK or FLOW, with vintage, unit and source. Do not add across blocks.

=== STOCK: FUND-LEVEL AUM (COMMITMENTS, INCLUDES DRY POWDER) ===
- Global private credit AUM: >USD 2.2trn, mid-2025 (BIS Bulletin 106, 9 Jul 2025; PitchBook-sourced). Up from ~USD 100bn in 2010.
- US, YE2024: USD 1.217trn private debt funds (Preqin) + USD 420bn BDCs (PitchBook) = ">USD 1.6trn" (OFR Brief 26-02, endnote 2).
- Form PF universe net assets, US private credit private funds, YE2024: USD 997bn (OFR, Form PF Q9/Q16). Excludes BDCs, which do not file Form PF.

=== STOCK: OUTSTANDING DRAWN LOANS (THE CREDIT BASE — NOT THE SAME AS AUM) ===
- Global: USD 1.5-2.0trn at end-2024 (FSB, 6 May 2026, Exec Summary and s6). US ~USD 1trn, then euro area, then UK. Comparable to institutional leveraged loans (USD 1.5-1.7trn) and US HY (~USD 2trn).
- US: ~USD 1.4trn in H2 2025 = 10% of total US nonfinancial corporate debt, ~one-third of below-IG debt excluding bank loans (Federal Reserve FSR, 8 May 2026, Box 4.1 Figure A; sources Preqin, LSEG BDC Collateral, PitchBook LCD, ICE, Z.1).
- Global direct lending only: >USD 1.2trn, 2025 (BIS Bulletin 106).
- EU private debt funds NAV: EUR 337bn (ESRB NBFI Monitor, Jun 2024 — stale; refresh required).
- BEWARE A BASE INCONSISTENCY: BIS Bulletin 120 (Jan 2026) states AI-related private credit loans are >USD 200bn and ~8% of total outstanding private credit loan volumes, implying a ~USD 2.5trn denominator. That is a broader PitchBook base than Bulletin 106's USD 1.2trn direct-lending base. Do not mix.

=== STOCK: DRY POWDER / UNCALLED CAPITAL ===
- NOT PUBLISHED as an official series. Best primary-derived figure: uncalled capital owed to US private credit private funds ~USD 300bn at YE2024, of which pensions ~USD 100bn (OFR Brief 26-02, computed as Form PF regulatory AUM minus balance-sheet assets, Q8/Q14/Q16; chart total USD 312bn). Form PF-only; excludes BDCs.

=== STOCK: BANK CHAIN — the money-creation leg. Five mutually inconsistent measurements ===
From OFR Brief 26-02 Figure 1, plus later vintages:
- Moody's global survey of 32 banks, 2023: USD 525bn COMMITTED, drawn n/a.
- US Call Reports, 2024, business credit intermediaries: USD 400bn COMMITTED / USD 264bn DRAWN.
- Temple/Penn State (Jang & Rosen), 2023, incl. BDCs: USD 372bn COMMITTED.
- Federal Reserve FSR 2021, ex-BDCs: USD 200bn COMMITTED.
- Berrospide et al., FEDS Notes 23 May 2025, Y-14Q Sch H.1, YE2024, incl. BDCs: USD 96bn COMMITTED (USD 40bn PD funds + USD 56bn BDCs) / ~USD 54bn DRAWN (USD 22bn + USD 32bn); utilisation 55% and 56.8%. 201 private debt funds identified.
- OFR Brief 26-02, Y-14, YE2024: USD 123bn COMMITTED incl. USD 30bn to BDCs / USD 74bn DRAWN incl. USD 16bn to BDCs. 551 private credit funds + 147 BDC borrowers identified. Utilisation historically 50-65%. Against USD 1.6trn+ aggregate Tier 1 at the same banks; drawn is <5% of the sample's total C&I.
- OFR headline total bank AND nonbank lending to US private credit: USD 410-540bn at YE2024 = Form PF reported borrowings USD 215bn (upper-bounded at USD 345bn once the gross-minus-net gap is added back) PLUS BDC borrowings from 10-K/10-Q of USD 195bn. Additive only because BDCs do not file Form PF.
- Global, FSB member regulatory data (five jurisdictions, May 2026): ~USD 220bn drawn + undrawn. Commercial data suggest USD 270-500bn. HKMA (Jan 2025), commercial data: ~USD 268bn global at end-2023; median bank 0.2% of assets, max 4.4%. FSB aggregate: <0.5% of total bank assets.
- US private-sector estimate on public filings: largest banks ~USD 300bn drawn, USD 155bn undrawn, plus USD 91bn from US branches of European banks (JPM Oct 2025 / Moody's Oct 2025, cited by FSB).

=== STOCK: THE BROADER NDFI ENVELOPE (upper bound; includes far more than private credit) ===
- H.8 line 26, loans to nondepository financial institutions, all commercial banks, SA: USD 2,005.0bn, July 2026. Domestically chartered USD 1,481.9bn; large domestic USD 1,285.6bn; small domestic USD 196.3bn; foreign-related USD 523.0bn.
- FDIC Call Report basis, Q3 2025: total NDFI loans USD 1.32trn (all banks); USD 1.29trn at banks >USD 10bn, split RC-C M.10.a mortgage credit intermediaries USD 315.8bn (24.5%), M.10.b business credit intermediaries USD 317.3bn (24.6%), M.10.c private equity funds USD 308.9bn (24.0%), M.10.d consumer credit intermediaries USD 105.0bn (8.1%), M.10.e other USD 241.4bn (18.7%). Unfunded commitments to NDFIs USD 987bn = 42.9% of total NDFI commitments. Past-due-and-nonaccrual 0.15% vs 1.32% for C&I. (FDIC, Banking Issues in Focus No. 1, Feb 2026.)
- Q1 2026: NDFI loans +USD 62.3bn (+4.4%) QoQ, implying ~USD 1.48trn (FDIC QBP Q1 2026, released 27 May 2026).
- FR Y-14Q Sch H.1 aggregate: bank credit COMMITMENTS to all NBFIs USD 2.6trn at Q4 2025; the "private equity, BDCs and private credit" category is ~25% of that (~USD 650bn committed) (Fed FSR, 8 May 2026, Figure 3.15 and Box 3.1).
- Bank contingent liquidity provision to all NBFIs: USD 2.3trn, 2024 (BIS Bulletin 106 Graph 4.D).

=== STOCK: SUBSCRIPTION / CAPITAL-CALL FACILITIES ===
- The only official number anywhere: RC-C M.10.c, USD 308.9bn drawn at Q3 2025, US banks >USD 10bn, of which FDIC states "most" are capital-call-secured. BUT this line covers facilities to funds whose assets are >50% EQUITY — i.e. PE and VC funds. A private CREDIT fund's subscription line belongs in M.10.b. There is no published subscription-facility series for private credit funds anywhere, in any jurisdiction.

=== STOCK: SEMI-LIQUID / RETAIL VEHICLES (US, Fed FSR 8 May 2026 Box 4.1, data H2 2025) ===
- Perpetual-life non-traded BDCs: USD 306bn gross assets / USD 161bn net assets.
- Interval funds: USD 119bn gross / USD 80bn net.
- Combined: USD 425bn gross / USD 241bn net ~ 20% of net AUM in US private credit vehicles (implies ~USD 1.2trn total vehicle net AUM).
- IMF GFSR Apr 2026: semiliquid vehicles ~ one-fifth of all direct-lending loans.
- Retail share of global private credit AUM: 13%, ~USD 280bn, 2025, up from <1% in 2010 (BIS Bulletin 106). BDCs total >USD 300bn AUM ~ one quarter of US direct lending.
- Private credit CLOs outstanding: USD 155bn, Oct 2025 (FSB Box 2, citing BofA).

=== STOCK: INSURER LEG ===
- Proxy (private placements + private ratings): ~10% of North American LIFE insurer portfolios, ~3% non-life (S&P Oct 2025, cited FSB Box 3).
- Structured securities: ~27% of PE-linked insurer portfolios at end-2024 vs ~12% for other large insurers (BIS Papers 161, Oct 2025).
- PE-backed US insurers control ~USD 900bn of insurance liabilities, up from USD 67bn in 2012; 35% of new US annuity sales in 2023.
- IAIS GIMAR 2025: most jurisdictions estimate insurer private credit exposure at <5% of insurance sector total assets — definitions still being refined.

=== FLOW ===
- NO OFFICIAL ANNUAL ORIGINATION SERIES EXISTS in any jurisdiction. Preqin/PitchBook/LCD sell deal-count and volume; nothing free and nothing official.
- Only official-source flow figures found: private credit ORIGINATIONS to AI-related companies USD ~40bn in 2025 vs ~USD 3bn in 2010 (BIS Bulletin 120, Jan 2026). Perpetual-BDC net flows turned negative in Q1 2026 — accepted redemptions exceeded inflows for the first time since these vehicles were created (Fed FSR Box 4.1 Figure C, built from SEC Form 8-K).
- AI-related private credit STOCK: >USD 200bn outstanding, ~8% of total outstanding private credit volumes (BIS Bulletin 120); FSB Box 4 reports the AI/tech share of private credit DEALS reached 34% in 2025 vs a 17% five-year average, and cites Morgan Stanley projecting USD 800bn of the USD 1.5trn external AI capex funding 2025-28 coming from private credit. That last is a private-sector projection, not a measurement.

=== VINTAGE / REVISION WARNINGS ===
1. Fed FSR revised committed amounts to "PE, BDCs and private credit" UP BY USD 261bn in Q4 2025 on a reclassification from "other financial vehicles", "SPEs/CLOs/ABS" and "open-end investment funds" using new vendor data (FSR Box 3.1). A prior revision was made in the April 2025 report. The level is a classification artefact as much as a measurement.
2. Call Report NDFI subcategories were reported on a best-efforts basis for Dec-2024 and Mar-2025 and only comprehensively from Jun-2025. Banks reclassified out of M.10.e into the other four through H1 2025. Pre-Jun-2025 subcategory data is not comparable.
3. USD 193.2bn of C&I and USD 140.6bn of consumer loans were reclassified into NDFI-and-not-elsewhere-classified at domestically chartered banks through Sep 2025 (FDIC, citing H.8). NDFI growth rates spanning 2024Q3-2025Q3 are contaminated.
4. Z.1 tables were RENUMBERED effective the 11 Jun 2026 release (2026Q1 data) to SNA alphanumeric codes: old L.133 is now S2.s, F.133 is S2.t; nonfinancial corporate business is S11.1.t/S11.1.s; finance companies S125s2.1; ABS issuers S125s1.3; holding companies S127.1; other financial business S127.2. A mapping CSV is on the Z.1 release page. Any pre-June-2026 code in your pipeline is dead.
5. Form PF: the Feb-2024 amendments that would add a "private credit" strategy category have been deferred three times; current compliance date 1 Oct 2026, and the Apr-2026 SEC/CFTC proposal would shrink Form PF substantially and asks whether to create a separate "private credit fund" definition at all. The free path to an official private credit AUM series does not exist and its arrival date is not fixed.

**Growth.** FUND AUM. Global private credit AUM from ~USD 100bn (2010) to >USD 2.2trn (mid-2025) — ~30% CAGR over 15 years off a near-zero base (BIS Bulletin 106). US private debt fund AUM more than tripled in the decade to YE2024 = 14% CAGR (Preqin, cited OFR Brief 26-02).

FSB member-jurisdiction survey (May 2026), 5-year average annual growth of private credit outstanding: Canada 16%, United Kingdom 17%. Euro area 13% p.a. over the past decade, from a low base. United States: threefold increase since 2019.

BANK CHAIN. FR Y-14Q committed amounts to "PE, BDCs and private credit" grew 17% YoY in 2025, versus 14% for commitments to all NBFI types (Fed FSR 8 May 2026, Box 3.1 — on the revised classification only). Call Report total NDFI loans: 21.0% CAGR since first reported in Q1 2010; +7.4% per quarter on average in 2024; +9.3% in Q1 2025; +4.4% QoQ (+USD 62.3bn) in Q1 2026 (FDIC). Caveat: 2024Q4-2025Q2 growth is inflated by USD 193.2bn of C&I and USD 140.6bn of consumer reclassification.

RETAIL. Retail share of global private credit AUM: <1% (2010) to 13% (2025), ~USD 280bn (BIS Bulletin 106). US semi-liquid vehicles reached USD 241bn net assets ~20% of private credit vehicle net AUM by H2 2025 (Fed FSR).

BDC LEVERAGE. Average BDC debt-to-equity roughly tripled from ~30% to >90% over 15 years to 2025 (BIS Bulletin 106 Graph 4.A).

AI SUB-SEGMENT. Outstanding private credit to AI-related firms from near zero to >USD 200bn (~8% of outstanding volumes); originations USD ~3bn (2010) to USD ~40bn (2025); share of funds lending to AI sectors 5% (2010) to ~20% (2025), though only ~5% of the average fund's volumes (BIS Bulletin 120, Jan 2026). FSB Box 4: AI/tech share of private credit deals 34% in 2025 vs 17% five-year average.

TURNING POINT. Q1 2026 is the first quarter in which accepted redemptions exceeded inflows for perpetual non-traded BDCs since the vehicle type was created (Fed FSR Box 4.1). Several funds received requests well above 5% of NAV; most managers capped at 5%.

## Series

| Series | Publisher | Freq | Cost | Gives | Limitations |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| FFIEC 031/041 Call Report, Schedule RC-C Part I item 9.a and Memoranda 10.a-10.e; Schedule RC-L item 1.e.(3)(a)-(e); Schedule RC-N NDFI past-due/nonaccrual | FFIEC / FDIC / Federal Reserve / OCC | Quarterly | free | THE single best free primary series for the bank leg. RC-C 9.a = total drawn loans to nondepository financial institutions, all banks. Memoranda 10.a-10.e split that into mortgage credit intermediaries / business credit intermediaries / private equity funds / consumer credit intermediaries / other, for banks with >$10bn assets. RC-L 1.e.(3)(a)-(e) gives the matching UNDRAWN commitments per subcategory - this is what lets you compute utilisation and contingent money-creation capacity. RC-N gives asset quality. | CRITICAL MAPPING TRAP: private credit funds and BDCs sit in M.10.b 'business credit intermediaries', NOT in M.10.c 'private equity funds'. M.10.c is capital-call/subscription facilities to funds whose assets are >50% EQUITY. A private credit fund's subscription line belongs in 10.b. Worse, the official instruction text for M.10.e's illustrative list ALSO names 'loans to private capital funds, including private equity and private debt funds' - a genuine internal inconsistency in the FFIEC instructions that guarantees noisy 10.b/10.e allocation. M.10.b is also polluted by finance companies, leasing companies, marketplace lenders and GAAP-loan CLO tranche holdings. Subcategories collected only from banks >$10bn (FFIEC 051 filers do not report them). Best-efforts reporting Dec-2024 and Mar-2025; comprehensive only from Jun-2025, so no usable time series before Q2 2025. Reclassification out of 10.e into the other four occurred through H1 2025. Filings unaudited. |
| H.8 Assets and Liabilities of Commercial Banks in the United States, line 26 'Loans to nondepository financial institutions' (Tables 2, 4, 6, 8, 10) | Federal Reserve Board | Weekly | free | Highest-frequency read on the bank-to-nonbank credit chain, split all commercial banks / domestically chartered / large domestic / small domestic / foreign-related institutions. USD 2,005.0bn all commercial banks, July 2026 SA. The foreign-related institutions line (Table 10) is the only free weekly proxy for foreign bank participation, relevant to Japanese and European bank lending into US private credit lenders. | NOT DECOMPOSABLE. The definition of line 26 bundles mortgage/business/consumer credit intermediaries with PE funds, insurers, GSEs, hedge funds, pension funds, securitisation vehicles, publicly listed investment funds and banks' own trust departments. Private credit is an unknown minority. Estimated from a survey of ~850 institutions and benchmarked to Call Reports, so it inherits every Call Report reclassification: USD 193.2bn of C&I and USD 140.6bn of consumer loans moved into NDFI-and-NEC through Sep 2025 alone. Excludes thrifts. Growth rates across 2024Q4-2025Q3 are meaningless without stripping reclassification. |
| FR Y-14Q Schedule H.1 (Corporate Loan Data Schedule), Capital Assessments and Stress Testing | Federal Reserve Board | Quarterly | confidential/regulatory | Loan-level: every corporate loan >$1mn at large BHCs/IHCs/SLHCs, with obligor name, committed and utilised amount, lien position, syndication flag, maturity, interest rate and spread, internal PD and LGD, and leveraged-loan flag. The only dataset that lets you see the bank-to-private-credit-fund facility one loan at a time. | Confidential. Foreign banks under-represented, which matters because FSB records rising Asia-Pacific (notably Japanese) bank lending to North American private credit lenders. Contains no explicit private credit identifier - every published estimate relies on name-matching against Preqin/PitchBook plus regex on 'private credit', 'direct lending', 'mezzanine'. Two official teams using the same data got materially different answers. Omits borrowings routed through BDC SPVs, of which a given BDC may run several, one per bank lender. No loan-to-value observable: OFR states plainly that LTVs cannot be seen in the confidential data. |
| Berrospide et al., 'Bank Lending to Private Credit: Size, Characteristics, and Financial Stability Implications', FEDS Notes | Federal Reserve Board | One-off, 23 May 2025 | free | Replicable Y-14 methodology. Identified 201 private debt funds by string-matching bank-provided borrower names against a Preqin manager list plus regex on 'private credit'/'direct lending', manually verified. YE2024: committed USD 96bn (USD 40bn PD funds, USD 56bn BDCs); drawn USD 22bn and USD 32bn; utilisation 55% and 56.8%. Y-14 banks are ~75% of US bank assets and ~60% of total credit commitments to BDCs. | Narrow identification produces the lowest published Y-14 estimate. Does not distinguish subscription lines from NAV lines. |
| OFR Brief 26-02, Berg & Lee, 'Measuring Counterparty Exposures to Private Credit' | Office of Financial Research, US Treasury | One-off, 12 March 2026 | free | The most complete published reconciliation. Novel identification: commercial DBs PLUS keyword search in Form PF and Y-14 PLUS manual reading of US public pension annual comprehensive financial reports (which disclose individual fund investments WITH strategy labels) PLUS insurer statutory Schedule BA Part 1 'private equity - mezzanine financing' entries, cross-matched by fund name. Found >2,000 Form PF private credit funds and 551 PC funds + 147 BDC borrowers in Y-14 for YE2024. Results: Y-14 committed USD 123bn (USD 30bn BDCs) / drawn USD 74bn (USD 16bn BDCs); Form PF reported borrowings USD 215bn, up to USD 345bn on the gross-minus-net adjustment; BDC borrowings from 10-K/10-Q USD 195bn; total bank+nonbank USD 410-540bn; uncalled LP capital ~USD 300bn (pensions ~USD 100bn). Leverage distribution: mean 1.91, 25th 1.00, median 1.04, 75th 1.36, 95th 3.58; the top 5% (104 funds) = USD 100bn gross on USD 19bn net, USD 81bn of borrowing, 23% of all Form PF private credit borrowing. Creditor mix: 80% US financial institutions; by type G-SIBs and other banks dominate. Bank loan characteristics: 86% first/second lien, 41% five-year term loans, 52% syndicated, only 21% classified as leveraged loans, all floating rate; equal-weighted 12-month PD 1.3%, LGD ~32%. | YE2024 data, now 20 months stale. Authors flag conflicting classifications between regulatory and commercial sources for the same fund. The gross-minus-net adjustment may capture non-interest-bearing liabilities and some advisers report undrawn borrowings inside gross assets, so the USD 345bn upper bound is soft. Excludes BDC SPV borrowings from the Y-14 figure. Counterparty-type breakdown covers only 109 Qualifying Hedge Funds - most private credit funds do not file Form PF question 47. |
| Federal Reserve Financial Stability Report, Box 4.1 'Developments in Private Credit', Figures 3.15/3.16, Box 3.1 | Federal Reserve Board | Semiannual (April/May and November) | free | The only regularly-updated official US private credit size series: ~USD 1.4trn outstanding, H2 2025, 10% of nonfinancial corporate debt, ~one third of below-IG debt ex bank loans. Semi-liquid vehicle detail: perpetual BDCs USD 306bn gross / USD 161bn net; interval funds USD 119bn gross / USD 80bn net. Y-14 aggregate: bank credit commitments to NBFIs USD 2.6trn at Q4 2025, 'PE, BDCs and private credit' ~25% of it, +17% YoY. Names its own sources: Preqin, LSEG BDC Collateral, PitchBook LCD, ICE, Morningstar Direct, SEC Form 8-K, FR Y-14Q Sch H.1, Z.1. | Box 4.1 Figure A is annual, chart-only, no published data file. The Y-14 NBFI category was revised UP BY USD 261bn in Q4 2025 on a vendor-driven reclassification, and had already been revised once in April 2025 - the level is partly a classification artefact and the series is not backward-consistent. 'PE, BDCs and private credit' bundles private equity in, so it is an upper bound for the private credit bank chain. |
| SEC Form PF (Sections 1a/1b/1c/4), specifically Questions 8, 9, 12, 14, 16, 43, 47 | SEC / CFTC | Quarterly for large advisers, annual for others | confidential/regulatory | Per-fund gross assets (Q8), net assets (Q9), borrowings (Q12), regulatory AUM (Q14), investor-type breakdown (Q16), creditor location (Q43) and, for Qualifying Hedge Funds only, named creditor identities and amounts (Q47). The only source for fund-level leverage distribution and uncalled capital by LP type. | THERE IS NO 'PRIVATE CREDIT FUND' CATEGORY. Section 1c Question 20 forces allocation across 22 investment strategies and private credit is not one; some funds skip the question entirely. Identification is therefore manual keyword matching. The Feb-2024 amendments that would add private credit have been deferred three times, currently to 1 Oct 2026; the Apr-2026 SEC/CFTC proposal would shrink Form PF materially and merely ASKS whether to define 'private credit fund' at all. BDCs are entirely out of scope. Form PF collects no full balance sheet, so reported borrowings cannot be reconciled to gross-minus-net. |
| SEC Form ADV Part 1A, Schedule D Section 7.B.(1) private fund reporting; adviser bulk data files | SEC (IAPD) | Annual amendment plus other-than-annual amendments | free | The free substitute for Form PF at the fund level: for every private fund an adviser manages, the fund name, fund type, gross asset value, minimum investment, number of beneficial owners, percentage owned by specified investor categories, whether a feeder/master, and the identity of the master. Downloadable as bulk CSVs. Use it to build a fund-name universe, a gross-asset-value panel, and to spot feeder structures. | Fund type options do not include private credit - most direct lending funds self-select 'other private fund' or 'private equity fund'. Gross asset value only, no net assets and no borrowings, so leverage is not computable from ADV alone. Reported at the adviser's own definition; double-counting across master-feeder is easy to introduce. |
| BDC annual and quarterly reports: Form 10-K / 10-Q, Consolidated Schedule of Investments and debt footnote; Form N-54A BDC election notification | SEC EDGAR | Quarterly | free | The single most under-used free primary dataset in this channel and the ONLY place you can build a bottom-up, loan-by-loan private credit stock without paying a vendor. The Consolidated Schedule of Investments lists every portfolio company by name, industry, investment type, reference rate and spread, cash and PIK coupon components, maturity date, principal, cost, fair value, fair-value hierarchy level, and non-accrual flag. The debt footnote gives every credit facility by lender, commitment, drawn amount, rate and maturity - this is how OFR reconstructed USD 195bn of BDC borrowings that Y-14 misses. N-54A filings give the definitive BDC universe (BDC election is a filed notification, not a guess). | BDCs do NOT file N-PORT, so there is no structured XBRL holdings feed; the Schedule of Investments must be parsed from the filing and financial-statement XBRL tagging of investment schedules is inconsistent across filers. BDC portfolios include broadly syndicated loans and equity, so the sum of fair values overstates private credit. Non-traded and private BDCs file on the same forms but with different fiscal calendars. SPV-level borrowings may be on- or off-balance-sheet depending on structure. |
| Perpetual non-traded BDC Form 8-K (share issuance, repurchase offers and results) | SEC EDGAR | Monthly to quarterly | free | The Fed builds its perpetual-BDC net flow chart (FSR Box 4.1 Figure C) from exactly this. Gives gross subscriptions, redemption REQUESTS, redemption requests ACCEPTED, and the proration factor when the 5%-of-NAV cap binds. The gap between requested and accepted is the cleanest available real-time stress indicator for the retail leg. | Filer-by-filer; no consolidated feed. Disclosure format is not standardised - some report in shares, some in dollars, some only the proration percentage. Only covers vehicles that voluntarily disclose this way. |
| Form N-PORT and Form N-CEN (interval funds and other registered closed-end funds holding private credit) | SEC | N-PORT monthly filed; N-CEN annual | free | Structured, machine-readable position-level holdings with identifiers, fair value, fair-value level and liquidity classification for the USD 119bn interval fund segment. N-CEN gives borrowing, credit-facility and securities-lending detail. | Covers registered funds only - the vast majority of private credit AUM is outside it. Does not cover BDCs. Many private loan positions lack ISIN/CUSIP, so cross-fund aggregation to a single borrower is not reliable. |
| FSB, 'Report on Vulnerabilities in Private Credit' (P060526) | Financial Stability Board | One-off, 6 May 2026 | free | The definitive current cross-jurisdiction primary source. Global size USD 1.5-2.0trn end-2024. Bank exposure USD ~220bn drawn+undrawn from member regulatory data vs USD 270-500bn from commercial data, <0.5% of bank assets. Graph 3 gives the facility-type mix of DRAWN bank lending by jurisdiction (euro area, HK, UK, Canada, Japan). Table 2 gives tenor, spread and credit protection by facility type as of Q3 2025: subscription line 1-3y ~200bp; fund portfolio financing ~225bp; NAV 3-5y 200-400bp; ABL 5-6y; BDC revolver ~230bp; PC CLO senior ~350bp. LTV caps: NAV generally <=30%, ABL up to 65%. Graph 13 is a heatmap of exactly which data each authority holds. Table 3 and Annex 2 are the FSB's own proposed core and additional metric set - use it as your specification. Annex 1 is a jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction inventory of data sources and their defects. | Point-in-time, mostly end-2024. Not comparable across jurisdictions: euro area figure is 12 banks and drawn only; UK is an ad hoc collection with manual staff classification and the FSB calls it likely an underestimate; HK is a representative sample; Canada and Japan figures INCLUDE private equity lending; the US could not share facility-type detail due to sharing restrictions. Not a repeating series - there is no commitment to update it. |
| FSB Global Monitoring Report on Nonbank Financial Intermediation 2025 (P161225), Box 1 | Financial Stability Board | Annual, December | free | The single most important negative result in this channel: FSB member jurisdictions, using their own official statistical and regulatory reporting, could identify only ~USD 0.5trn of private credit, against USD 1.5-2.0trn from commercial data. Also establishes that private credit funds are NOT in the FSB narrow measure (USD 76.3trn, +12.7% in 2024) at all, because they are closed-end, low-leverage and not short-term-wholesale-funded, so they fall into no economic function EF1-EF5. Total NBFI USD 256.8trn, +9.4% in 2024, 51% of global financial assets. Underlying data is downloadable. | Only eight jurisdictions could disaggregate a private credit estimate at all, and for different entity types each (Table A1): Canada/Italy/Luxembourg funds only; Japan finance companies only; Hong Kong insurance corporations only. The USD 0.5trn is not a market estimate, it is a coverage measurement. |
| IMF Global Financial Stability Report, Chapter 1 private credit section and Online Annex 1.4 | International Monetary Fund | Semiannual (April, October) | free | April 2026: semiliquid vehicles ~one-fifth of all direct-lending loans; ~one-fifth of direct-lending loans are to the software sector, and software exposure may exceed 50% of NAV for LEVERAGED private credit vehicles. Stress-test framework showing distressed-borrower shares 2-3x baseline under stressed-rates and stressed-earnings scenarios. Figure 1.15 tracks S&P direct-lending selective vs payment default rates monthly. April 2024 Chapter 2 remains the canonical methodological reference for the asset class. | Chart-only; underlying series not released. Relies on S&P and PitchBook underneath. The 'one-fifth semiliquid' and 'one-fifth software' figures are not accompanied by a published denominator. |
| FDIC, 'Bank Lending to Nondepository Financial Institutions', Banking Issues in Focus No. 1 (Feb 2026), and the Quarterly Banking Profile | Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation | QBP quarterly; Banking Issues in Focus ad hoc | free | The authoritative walkthrough of the new NDFI Call Report items with the full official subcategory definitions reproduced in the appendix, the Q3 2025 subcategory dollar split, unfunded commitments (USD 987bn, 42.9% of NDFI commitments), asset quality (0.15% PDNA vs 1.32% C&I), and the size-cohort breakdown showing banks >USD 100bn spread evenly across PE/business credit/mortgage credit while USD 10-100bn banks skew ~40% to mortgage credit intermediation. Also documents, with dollar amounts, every reclassification that corrupts the series. | Explicitly states that private equity, private credit and real estate funds are NOT available as a category in Z.1 and cites a separate source for their ~USD 7trn 2023 asset figure. Chart-only for the size-cohort split. |
| Federal Reserve Z.1 Financial Accounts of the United States (S-code tables) | Federal Reserve Board | Quarterly | free | DENOMINATORS ONLY. Nonfinancial corporate business liabilities (S11.1.s) for the 'private credit as % of NFC debt' ratio the Fed itself computes. Finance companies S125s2.1; ABS issuers S125s1.3; holding companies S127.1; other financial business S127.2. | THERE IS NO PRIVATE CREDIT, BDC OR PRIVATE FUND SECTOR IN Z.1. Domestic hedge funds and private equity funds are folded into the household-and-nonprofit sector residual (see S14/S15 footnote 1). 'Other financial business' is explicitly a residual derived from other sectors. TABLES WERE RENUMBERED effective the 11 June 2026 release (2026Q1) to SNA alphanumeric codes - F.133 is now S2.t, L.133 is S2.s. A mapping CSV is on the release page. Any pipeline built on L./F. numbering is broken. |
| NAIC statutory annual and quarterly statements: Schedule D Part 1 Section 1 (issuer credit obligations, D-1-1), Section 2 (asset-backed securities, D-1-2), and Schedule BA Part 1 | NAIC / state insurance departments | Quarterly and annual | paid | The insurer leg at CUSIP level. From 1 Jan 2025 the principles-based bond definition (SSAP 26R/43R, Issue Paper No. 169) splits the old Schedule D-1 into D-1-1 issuer credit obligations and D-1-2 asset-backed securities, and pushes anything failing the bond definition to Schedule BA. Rated-note feeders are the target of this reform: whether one lands in D-1-2 or BA turns on the substantive credit-enhancement test. OFR found insurers commonly file private credit fund holdings under the Schedule BA Part 1 'private equity - mezzanine financing' category, and used exactly that to build a fund-name list. Combine with the NAIC designation and the private-letter-rating flag. | NAIC sells the data; free access is state-by-state and partial. There is no 'private credit' box on any schedule - you must proxy with private placements plus private ratings, which is what S&P did to reach ~10% of North American life portfolios. 2025 is a transition year: D-1-1/D-1-2/BA time series are NOT comparable to pre-2025 Schedule D. Offshore reinsurance (Bermuda) removes liabilities and their backing assets from US statutory visibility entirely. |
| AIFMD Annex IV reporting; ESRB EU Non-bank Financial Intermediation Risk Monitor; ESMA market reports; FCA AIFMD returns | ESRB / ESMA / ECB / FCA | AIFMD returns annual, half-yearly or quarterly by fund size; ESRB monitor annual | free | Aggregates are free; fund-level Annex IV data is confidential to authorities. The only harmonised EU/UK fund-level framework: investment strategy, investor concentration, instruments held, aggregate exposures, market risk, leverage on both gross and commitment methods, and investor and portfolio liquidity profiles. Yields the euro-area open-ended share (~20% of private credit funds, ~75% of those monthly-or-more-frequent redemptions, Dec 2024) and the EUR 337bn private debt fund NAV figure. | FSB Box 5 is a devastating list of defects: no private credit fund category at entity level (they fall in a residual); at activity level loans are only 'leveraged loans' or 'other loans'; only the TOP FIVE instruments per fund are reported; most private loan positions and many ABS carry NO ISIN or LEI, so you cannot aggregate a single borrower's leverage across lenders; NO LOOK-THROUGH - reporting stops at the first layer, so multi-layer fund/SPV structures and LBO-embedded leverage are invisible; and the legal definition of 'exposure' excludes several leverage types. The ECB does not have access to AIFMD data and uses PitchBook instead. The FCA identifies private credit funds in its own returns using keyword searches, PitchBook and machine learning. |
| BIS Locational Banking Statistics (cross-border claims on non-bank financial institutions) and Consolidated Banking Statistics | Bank for International Settlements | Quarterly | free | The only free multilateral view of the cross-border bank-to-NBFI leg by reporting country and counterparty country, on the NBFI counterparty sector split introduced with the LBS enhancements. Use it to size Japanese and euro-area bank claims on US-resident NBFIs - the corridor FSB flags as growing. | NBFI counterparty sector is far broader than private credit; no fund-type decomposition. Residency-based, so a Cayman or Luxembourg fund vehicle lending into the US shows up in the wrong place. Cannot be reconciled to Y-14 or Call Report figures. |
| BIS Bulletins 106 (retail investors in private credit, Jul 2025), 120 (financing the AI boom, Jan 2026), 128 (AI disruption in private credit); BIS Quarterly Review Mar 2025 and Mar 2026; BIS Papers 161 (life insurance transformation, Oct 2025) | Bank for International Settlements | Irregular | free | Global AUM >USD 2.2trn; retail share 13% ~ USD 280bn; direct lending outstanding >USD 1.2trn; BDC balance-sheet composition (drawn bank lines ~40% of BDC debt, unsecured notes ~50%); BDC debt/equity tripled 30% to >90% and is strongly procyclical, with change in assets driven by change in debt while equity is sticky; bank contingent liquidity provision to NBFIs USD 2.3trn. Bulletin 120: AI-related private credit >USD 200bn outstanding, ~8% of volumes, originations USD 40bn in 2025 vs USD 3bn in 2010, loans to AI firms average USD 169mn vs USD 90mn elsewhere at essentially identical spreads (6.2 vs 6.1pp) and maturities (4.7 vs 4.8y). BIS Papers 161: PE-linked insurers 27% structured securities vs 12% for other large insurers. | All PitchBook/Capital IQ underneath - not independent of the vendor data. Bulletin 120's implied ~USD 2.5trn total-outstanding denominator is inconsistent with Bulletin 106's USD 1.2trn direct-lending base; the two bulletins use different perimeters. |
| HKMA, 'Assessing the Role of Banks in the Private Credit Sector: Evidence from Bank Loans Provided to Private Credit Lenders' | Hong Kong Monetary Authority | One-off, January 2025 | free | The only published GLOBAL bank-lending-to-private-credit estimate built from commercial loan-level data rather than one jurisdiction's regulatory returns: ~USD 268bn at end-2023, with the distribution across banking groups showing a median of 0.2% and a maximum of 4.4% of total bank assets, and a time series back to 2014. | End-2023, now stale. Commercial-database coverage, so it misses bilateral facilities not captured by loan-deal vendors. Distribution is by banking group, not by facility type. |
| Preqin (private debt AUM, dry powder, fundraising); PitchBook and PitchBook LCD (deal volume, spreads, BDC AUM); LSEG BDC Collateral; Morningstar Direct; KBRA DLD; Lincoln International valuation indices; Moody's and S&P private credit research | Various commercial | Monthly to quarterly | paid | Everything the official sector cannot produce: AUM by strategy, dry powder, fundraising flow, deal-level origination volume, spread and leverage series, private credit CLO issuance, and borrower-level EBITDA/leverage/ICR. Every official estimate in this note ultimately leans on one of these. The Fed's own FSR private credit size chart is sourced from Preqin, LSEG BDC Collateral and PitchBook LCD. | Voluntary reporting creates selection bias in both directions. OFR found that some of the largest and most reputable private credit funds are ABSENT from commercial databases entirely, because they do not need to list themselves for fund discovery - the vendors systematically under-cover the top of the market. Conflicting fund classifications between vendors and between vendors and regulatory filings. FSB Graph 1.1 shows the range of vendor AUM estimates spanning roughly USD 1trn to USD 40trn+ depending on definition. Licences typically forbid redistribution, which is why nothing free reproduces them. |

## Build recipe

Build FOUR separate panels. They measure different things on different bases and must never be summed. Then reconcile them explicitly.

===============================================================
STEP 0 - FIX THE PERIMETER BEFORE TOUCHING ANY DATA
===============================================================
Nothing reconciles until you choose. FSB Graph 1.1 shows vendor AUM estimates for "private credit" ranging from roughly USD 1trn (direct lending only) to USD 40trn+ (adding agricultural lending, residential mortgages and supply-chain finance). Pick one and write it down:
 (a) FSB NARROW: nonbank direct lending to medium-sized companies, bilaterally negotiated, mostly PE-sponsored. Recommended default; comparable to IMF (2024) and every official estimate quoted here.
 (b) ECOSYSTEM: adds BDCs, private credit CLOs, and bank SRTs bought by credit funds.
 (c) ORIGINATION-CENTRIC: adds privately placed investment-grade debt originated by asset managers and held by insurers - this is the one that captures the rated-note-feeder/annuity chain, and it roughly doubles the number.
Tag every series with its perimeter.

===============================================================
PANEL A - COMMITMENT BASE (AUM AND DRY POWDER)
===============================================================
A1. Paid path. Preqin private debt AUM by strategy and vintage; PitchBook for BDCs. Dry powder = AUM minus NAV. Anchor: USD 1.217trn (Preqin, US private debt funds) + USD 420bn (PitchBook, BDCs) = >USD 1.6trn US at YE2024; global >USD 2.2trn mid-2025 (BIS).

A2. Free path, and it works. Download the SEC Form ADV bulk data files. Parse Schedule D Section 7.B.(1): one row per private fund per adviser, with fund name, fund type, gross asset value, minimum investment, number of beneficial owners, and master/feeder linkage. Then:
 - Build the fund universe by keyword on fund name. Use OFR's exact list: alternative credit, asset-based, direct lend, illiquid credit, middle-market, private credit, private debt, private loan, private lend, mezzanine, senior debt, senior credit, senior loan, venture debt.
 - Augment the universe from two free sources OFR proved out and nobody else uses: (i) US public pension annual comprehensive financial reports, which list individual private fund investments WITH strategy labels - these catch the large, reputable managers absent from Preqin and PitchBook; (ii) insurer statutory Schedule BA Part 1 entries filed under "private equity - mezzanine financing", which is where insurers habitually park private credit fund holdings.
 - De-duplicate master/feeder using the Schedule D master-fund identifier, or you will double count the rated-note feeder against its master.
 - Sum gross asset value. This gives a gross-asset panel, not AUM and not dry powder - ADV carries no net assets and no borrowings.

A3. Uncalled capital. There is no series. Reproduce OFR's construction: uncalled capital = regulatory AUM (Form PF Q14, or Form ADV Item 5.F for the free version) minus balance-sheet assets (Form PF Q8). OFR got ~USD 300bn for US private credit private funds at YE2024, pensions ~USD 100bn. Form-PF-only; excludes BDCs.

===============================================================
PANEL B - CREDIT BASE (OUTSTANDING DRAWN LOANS)
===============================================================
B1. Headline, free, official: Fed FSR Box 4.1 Figure A. ~USD 1.4trn US, H2 2025, with the denominators (10% of NFC debt, one third of below-IG debt ex bank loans) already computed. Annual, chart-only.

B2. Bottom-up BDC leg - the highest-value free build here.
 - Get the BDC universe from EDGAR Form N-54A filings (the BDC election notification). Definitive; do not use SIC codes.
 - For each CIK, pull every 10-K and 10-Q.
 - Parse the Consolidated Schedule of Investments: portfolio company name, industry, investment type (first lien / second lien / mezzanine / equity), reference rate and spread, cash coupon, PIK coupon, maturity, principal, cost, fair value, fair-value hierarchy level, non-accrual flag.
 - From this you can compute, quarterly and free: outstanding first-lien direct lending stock; PIK share of interest income and of loans by count; non-accrual rate; Level 3 share; sector concentration (compute the software share and compare with IMF's ~one-fifth of direct lending); weighted-average spread.
 - Parse the debt footnote for every credit facility: lender name, commitment, drawn, rate, maturity, and whether the borrower is the BDC or an SPV. This is how OFR reconstructed USD 195bn of BDC borrowings that Y-14 misses, because BDCs borrow through SPVs, often one SPV per bank lender.
 - Anchor: aggregate BDC AUM >USD 300bn ~ one quarter of US direct lending (BIS).
 - Do NOT treat the sum of BDC fair values as private credit - strip out broadly syndicated loans and equity using the investment-type field.

B3. Interval fund leg. N-PORT structured data for holdings, N-CEN for borrowings. Anchor: USD 119bn gross / USD 80bn net (Fed FSR). Verify the current N-PORT public-availability schedule before building a live pipeline.

B4. EU/UK leg. ESRB NBFI Monitor and ESMA reports for the AIFMD aggregate (EUR 337bn private debt fund NAV as of the Jun-2024 monitor - refresh). Accept that entity-level identification is a residual category and only the top five instruments per fund are reported.

===============================================================
PANEL C - THE BANK CHAIN (THE MONEY-CREATION LEG)
===============================================================
Four layers of narrowing.

C1. OUTER ENVELOPE - H.8 line 26. Pull the H.8 CSV weekly. Take line 26 from Table 2 (all commercial banks), Table 4 (domestically chartered), Table 6 (large domestic), Table 8 (small domestic), Table 10 (foreign-related institutions). Latest: USD 2,005.0bn all commercial banks, July 2026 SA. Upper bound only - it bundles mortgage/business/consumer credit intermediaries with PE funds, insurers, GSEs, hedge funds, pension funds, securitisation vehicles, publicly listed funds and banks' own trust departments. FRED carries these (candidate IDs LNFDCBW027SBOG, LNFACBW027NBOG, LNFFRIW027NBOG - verify which panel each maps to against the H.8 tables before use). Table 10 is your only free weekly read on foreign bank participation.

C2. SUBCATEGORY SPLIT - Call Report. Pull FFIEC 031/041 via the FFIEC CDR bulk download or the FDIC BankFind Financial Data API:
 - RC-C Part I item 9.a: total drawn NDFI loans, all banks.
 - RC-C Part I Memoranda 10.a / 10.b / 10.c / 10.d / 10.e: mortgage credit intermediaries / business credit intermediaries / private equity funds / consumer credit intermediaries / other. Banks >USD 10bn only.
 - RC-L item 1.e.(3)(a) through (e): the matching UNDRAWN commitments, subcategory by subcategory. This is the item everyone forgets and the one that measures contingent money-creation capacity.
 - RC-N: 30-89 day, 90+ day and nonaccrual NDFI loans, all banks.
 THE MAPPING RULE, and get this wrong and the whole panel is wrong: your private credit line is M.10.b, NOT M.10.c. M.10.c "loans to private equity funds" is capital-call and subscription facilities to funds whose underlying assets are >50% EQUITY - PE and VC. A private CREDIT fund's subscription line belongs in M.10.b along with BDCs, private debt funds, direct lenders, business CLOs and business ABCP conduits. The instructions are internally inconsistent: M.10.e's illustrative list also names "loans to private capital funds, including private equity and private debt funds", which guarantees 10.b/10.e leakage.
 Q3 2025 benchmark (banks >USD 10bn): 10.a USD 315.8bn, 10.b USD 317.3bn, 10.c USD 308.9bn, 10.d USD 105.0bn, 10.e USD 241.4bn; total NDFI USD 1.32trn all banks; unfunded commitments USD 987bn (42.9% of total NDFI commitments); PDNA 0.15%. Q1 2026: NDFI +USD 62.3bn (+4.4%) QoQ ~ USD 1.48trn.
 Start the time series at 2025Q2. Everything before is best-efforts reporting.
 Compute utilisation = drawn / (drawn + undrawn) per subcategory. Use M.10.b + M.10.c as an upper bound for the whole private-capital bank chain and M.10.b alone as the private credit proxy.

C3. NAMED-COUNTERPARTY LAYER - FR Y-14Q Schedule H.1. Confidential, but carry all published aggregates side by side rather than picking one:
 - Fed FSR Figure 3.15: bank credit commitments to NBFIs USD 2.6trn at Q4 2025; "PE, BDCs and private credit" ~25% (~USD 650bn committed), +17% YoY. UPPER BOUND - bundles private equity.
 - Berrospide et al. FEDS Note (May 2025), 201 funds: YE2024 committed USD 96bn, drawn ~USD 54bn. LOWER BOUND - narrow name-matching.
 - OFR Brief 26-02 (Mar 2026), 551 PC funds + 147 BDC borrowers: YE2024 committed USD 123bn (USD 30bn BDCs), drawn USD 74bn (USD 16bn BDCs). MIDDLE, and methodologically the best documented.
 The spread between USD 96bn and USD 650bn is not measurement error, it is definitional: Y-14 has no private credit flag, so the answer is a function of how you name-match. Report the range, never a point.

C4. TOTAL LENDING INCLUDING NONBANK - OFR construction:
 Form PF reported borrowings (Q12) USD 215bn, upper-bounded at USD 345bn by adding back the gross-minus-net gap (Q8 minus Q9), PLUS BDC borrowings from 10-K/10-Q of USD 195bn. Additive only because BDCs do not file Form PF. Total USD 410-540bn at YE2024.
 THE MONEY-CREATION SUBSET IS SMALLER. Strip out BDC unsecured notes and bonds held by insurers, pension funds and mutual funds - those are intermediation. BIS gives the split: for the average BDC, drawn bank lines are ~40% of debt and unsecured notes ~50%. So roughly 40-50% of the BDC leg is bank-created, the rest is not.

C5. FACILITY-TYPE SPLIT - the only place the creation question is actually answered.
 No free series exists. Use FSB Graph 3 (drawn commitments by facility type, by jurisdiction: euro area, HK, UK, Canada, Japan) as your allocation prior, with FSB Table 2 parameters:
 - Subscription line: 1-3y, ~200bp, secured on uncalled LP commitments. Most common globally. Creates deposits that self-extinguish at the capital call. Model as a revolving stock.
 - Fund portfolio financing / borrowing base: ~225bp, structured as a senior securitisation tranche with a 30-40% equity tranche, fund SPV declared originator, bank gets securitisation risk weights. Largest euro-area type. Creates durable deposits. Euro-area data suggests ~70% of it funds non-EU (mostly US-exposed) borrowers.
 - NAV facility: 3-5y, 200-400bp, LTV generally <=30%.
 - Asset-based lending via SPV: 5-6y term, LTV up to 65%.
 - Repo against credit-linked notes: the SRT recycling loop, high haircuts.
 - BDC revolvers: ~230bp; up to 90% of bank lending to BDCs is credit lines; the average BDC maintains relationships with 5.5 banks; top 5 banks hold 63% and top 10 hold 84% of committed BDC lending.
 Compute a creation-weighted total: (drawn subscription lines, weighted for the fraction of the year outstanding) + (drawn fund-portfolio-financing + NAV + ABL, at full weight) + (bank purchases of private credit CLO tranches and warehouse lines, at full weight). Everything else in Panel C is either intermediation or double counting.

C6. CROSS-BORDER. BIS Locational Banking Statistics, cross-border claims on non-bank financial institutions, by reporting and counterparty country. Size the Japanese and euro-area corridor into US-resident NBFIs, which FSB flags as growing. Cannot be reconciled to C2 or C3; treat as directional.

===============================================================
PANEL D - INVESTOR CLAIMS (THE LIABILITY SIDE)
===============================================================
D1. LP composition. Form PF Q9 and Q16 give net assets by investor type - OFR reports USD 997bn net assets for US private credit private funds at YE2024. Free substitute: Form ADV Schedule D Section 7.B.(1) percentage-owned-by-investor-category fields.

D2. Insurer leg. NAIC statutory statements. From 1 Jan 2025 pull Schedule D-1-1 (issuer credit obligations), D-1-2 (asset-backed securities) and Schedule BA Part 1. No private credit box; proxy with private placements plus privately-rated securities, the S&P method that yielded ~10% of North American life portfolios vs ~3% non-life. For rated-note feeders specifically, screen D-1-2 and BA for securities carrying a private letter rating from a smaller NRSRO whose issuer name matches your Panel A fund universe. Do NOT build a pre-2025 time series across the bond-definition change - the schedules are not comparable.

D3. Retail / semi-liquid leg. Free and high-frequency:
 - Perpetual non-traded BDC net flows from SEC Form 8-K: gross subscriptions, redemption requests, redemptions accepted, proration factor. Exactly how the Fed builds FSR Figure C. The requested-minus-accepted gap is your stress indicator.
 - Interval funds from N-PORT/N-CEN.
 - Anchors: perpetual BDCs USD 306bn gross / USD 161bn net; interval funds USD 119bn gross / USD 80bn net; combined USD 425bn gross / USD 241bn net ~ 20% of US private credit vehicle net AUM (Fed FSR, H2 2025). Q1 2026 was the first quarter in which accepted redemptions exceeded inflows.

===============================================================
STEP 5 - LEVERAGE STACK
===============================================================
Compute four separate leverage numbers and label each with its rung. Do not multiply them without stating the collateral overlap.
 1. Borrower: debt/EBITDA, ICR, DSCR. BDC Schedule of Investments gives coupon and spread but not EBITDA - borrower financials require KBRA DLD, Lincoln International or Preqin. There is NO free borrower-leverage series. FSB records ~75% of borrowers with EBITDA below USD 100mn and a rating distribution clustered at single-B.
 2. Fund: gross assets / net assets. Form PF Q8/Q9. YE2024 distribution: mean 1.91, 25th 1.00, median 1.04, 75th 1.36, 95th 3.58. The 95th-100th cohort (104 funds) holds USD 100bn gross on USD 19bn net = USD 81bn borrowing = 23% of all Form PF private credit borrowing. Free substitute for the BDC subset only: 10-Q balance sheet debt/equity - sector average tripled from ~30% to >90% over 15 years.
 3. Facility: LTV. NAV <=30%, ABL up to 65%, fund portfolio financing equity tranche 30-40% implying ~60-70% advance rate. OFR states LTV is NOT observable even in confidential data - these are FSB member survey ranges, not measurements.
 4. Investor: LP-level leverage against fund commitments. Not measured anywhere.

===============================================================
STEP 6 - REVISION AND VINTAGE DISCIPLINE
===============================================================
Hard-code these breaks into any pipeline:
 - Z.1 renumbered to SNA S-codes effective the 11 Jun 2026 release. Old L./F. codes are dead. Mapping CSV on the release page.
 - Fed FSR Y-14 NBFI classification revised UP USD 261bn in Q4 2025, after a prior revision in Apr 2025. Not backward-consistent.
 - Call Report NDFI subcategories: best-efforts Dec-2024 and Mar-2025, comprehensive only from Jun-2025. Start at 2025Q2.
 - USD 193.2bn C&I plus USD 140.6bn consumer reclassified into NDFI-and-NEC through Sep 2025. Strip before computing any growth rate spanning 2024Q4-2025Q3.
 - Form PF: private credit strategy category compliance date currently 1 Oct 2026, deferred three times; the Apr-2026 proposal may remove it. Do not architect around it.

===============================================================
STEP 7 - THE ONE-PAGE RECONCILIATION
===============================================================
US, latest available vintage, four numbers on four bases:
 A. Commitments (AUM incl. dry powder): >USD 1.6trn, YE2024, Preqin+PitchBook.
 B. Outstanding loan stock: ~USD 1.4trn, H2 2025, Fed FSR.
 C. Bank+nonbank lending TO the vehicles: USD 410-540bn, YE2024, OFR; of which the Y-14 bank slice is USD 74-123bn drawn-to-committed and the money-creating subset is smaller still.
 D. Investor claims: USD 997bn net assets in Form PF funds + USD 241bn net in semi-liquid vehicles + ~USD 300bn uncalled.
A minus B is dry powder plus non-loan assets. B is the credit. C is the sliver of B that is bank-financed and therefore partly monetary. D is the liability side of B. A+B+C+D is meaningless.

## Double-counting risk

SEVEN OVERLAPS, in descending order of how often they are botched.

1. AUM vs OUTSTANDING LOANS. USD 2.2trn global AUM and USD 1.5-2.0trn global outstanding are not two markets. AUM includes uncalled commitments (~USD 300bn in the US alone) and non-loan assets. Adding them roughly double counts the drawn book.

2. BANK LENDING TO FUNDS vs FUND LENDING TO BORROWERS. The USD 410-540bn of lending to private credit vehicles is a claim on the SAME loan pool that constitutes the USD 1.4trn outstanding stock. It is a second lien on identical collateral, not incremental credit to the real economy. Adding Panel B to Panel C double counts the entire bank chain.

3. THE FIVE PUBLISHED BANK-EXPOSURE ESTIMATES OVERLAP ALMOST COMPLETELY. Moody's USD 525bn (32-bank global survey, committed, 2023), Call Reports USD 400bn/USD 264bn (2024), Temple/Penn State USD 372bn (2023), Fed FSR USD 200bn (2021, ex-BDCs), Fed study USD 96bn/USD 54bn (2024), OFR USD 123bn/USD 74bn (2024). Five measurements of one thing with different perimeters, different committed-vs-drawn conventions, different BDC treatment and different foreign-bank coverage. Summing or averaging them is the single worst error available here.

4. COMMITTED vs DRAWN. Utilisation on bank facilities to private credit funds and BDCs has run 50-65% historically (OFR, Y-14). Undrawn commitments to all NDFIs were USD 987bn = 42.9% of total NDFI commitments at Q3 2025. Undrawn is contingent money creation, not realised money creation. Stating a committed figure alongside a drawn figure from a different source and treating both as stock is a factor-of-two error.

5. CALL REPORT M.10.b vs M.10.c vs M.10.e. BDCs and private debt funds are in 10.b. Subscription facilities to PE/VC funds are in 10.c. The official instructions for 10.e ALSO list "private capital funds, including private equity and private debt funds", and the FDIC documents banks reclassifying between these lines through June 2025. Adding 10.b + 10.c and calling it "private credit" double counts private equity subscription lines; using 10.c alone as "subscription lines to private credit funds" is simply the wrong line.

6. THE FUND / BDC / CLO / FEEDER STACK ON ONE LOAN. A single middle-market loan can appear simultaneously as: an asset in a private credit fund's Form PF gross assets; collateral in a fund portfolio financing facility booked by the bank as a securitisation exposure; an asset in a private credit CLO (USD 155bn outstanding, ~16% of the USD 977bn US CLO market); an indirect exposure in a rated note held by an insurer on Schedule D-1-2; and an underlying in a semi-liquid BDC's Schedule of Investments. Each is a legitimate measurement of a different balance sheet. There is no look-through in AIFMD reporting (ESRB Box 5: reporting stops at the first layer) and none in Form PF, so you cannot net them.

7. CROSS-CHANNEL OVERLAPS WITH OTHER RESEARCHERS' PATCHES.
 - SRT / synthetic securitisation: private credit funds are the largest single investor group in EU bank SRTs, and banks then repo-finance the CLNs those funds bought. That exposure sits in both the SRT channel's numbers and mine. FSB calls it "circles of risks".
 - Insurance / annuity / funded reinsurance: the rated-note feeder is simultaneously an insurer asset (their channel) and a private credit fund liability (mine). Count it once, on the insurer's side, since that is where the funding originates.
 - Private credit CLOs overlap the securitisation channel entirely.
 - Bank NDFI lending overlaps whichever channel covers hedge fund and broker-dealer financing - H.8 line 26 contains all of it and cannot be split.
 - AI-linked private credit (>USD 200bn outstanding, USD 40bn 2025 originations) is a SUBSET of my USD 1.4trn, not an addition to it; whoever covers AI financing and I are describing the same loans.

THE ONE THING THAT IS GENUINELY ADDITIVE: Form PF borrowings (USD 215-345bn) plus BDC borrowings from 10-K/10-Q (USD 195bn), because BDCs are statutorily outside Form PF. OFR makes this addition explicitly and it is the only sum in this note I would defend.

## What cannot be measured

Stated plainly, without hedging.

NO SERIES EXISTS AT ALL FOR:
1. The size of the NAV lending market. Not in any jurisdiction, from any official body. FSB can only say it is "present in Canada and Japan, growing in the UK", with LTVs generally under 30% and more commonly used for private equity and CRE funds than credit funds. No stock, no flow, no issuance.
2. The size of the subscription / capital-call facility market for PRIVATE CREDIT funds. The only official number anywhere is Call Report M.10.c (USD 308.9bn drawn, Q3 2025, US banks >USD 10bn), and that line covers funds whose assets are >50% EQUITY - PE and VC, not credit funds. Private credit subscription lines are buried inside M.10.b with BDCs, finance companies and CLO tranche holdings, unseparated.
3. Fund portfolio financing / borrowing-base facilities. FSB identifies this as the LARGEST facility type in the euro area and gives no total for it, anywhere, in any jurisdiction.
4. Rated-note feeder issuance. No series. Not in NAIC filings (no such category), not in SEC filings, not from any regulator. The Jan-2025 NAIC principles-based bond definition changes WHERE these sit (D-1-1 vs D-1-2 vs Schedule BA) but creates no identifier for them.
5. Annual private credit ORIGINATION volume, from any official source, in any jurisdiction. Only vendor data. The single official-source origination figure I found in this whole exercise is BIS's USD ~40bn of 2025 originations to AI-related firms.
6. A single borrower's total leverage across multiple private lenders. ESRB states the reason bluntly: a significant proportion of private loan positions and many ABS carry no ISIN, LEI or any standard identifier, so cross-lender aggregation is impossible even inside the supervisory perimeter.
7. Indirect and multi-layer exposures. AIFMD reporting has no look-through requirement; reporting stops at the first layer of investment. Stakes in multiple layers of funds and SPVs, and LBO-embedded leverage, are excluded from the legal definition of "exposure". Form PF has no look-through either.
8. Loan-to-value ratios on bank facilities to private credit funds. OFR states outright that LTVs cannot be observed even in confidential regulatory data. The 30% and 65% figures in circulation are FSB member survey ranges, not measurements.
9. Valuation dispersion. FCA's March 2025 private market valuation review is qualitative. There is no published series on marking frequency, mark dispersion across managers holding the same credit, or the gap between a fund's mark and a transaction price.
10. Foreign banks' bank-to-private-credit exposure into the US. Y-14 under-represents them; H.8 Table 10 shows foreign-related institutions in aggregate (USD 523.0bn NDFI, July 2026) with no decomposition; BIS LBS is residency-based and cannot identify private credit. FSB notes Japanese banks increasing lending to North American private credit lenders and gives no number.

THE COVERAGE GAP, QUANTIFIED:
FSB member jurisdictions, using their own official statistical and regulatory reporting, could identify approximately USD 0.5trn of private credit for the 2025 Global Monitoring Report. Commercial estimates for the same period are USD 1.5-2.0trn. The official sector can see roughly a quarter to a third of the market it is trying to supervise. That is the finding and it should not be smoothed over.

STRUCTURAL BLIND SPOTS IN THE MAJOR FRAMEWORKS:
- Fed Z.1 has NO private credit, BDC or private fund sector. Domestic hedge funds and private equity funds sit inside the household-and-nonprofit residual. "Other financial business" is itself a residual derived from other sectors' data. The FDIC states explicitly that private equity, private credit and real estate funds are not available as a category in the Fed's data. The Fed's own FSR sizes private credit from Preqin, LSEG and PitchBook and uses Z.1 only for the denominator.
- FSB narrow measure (USD 76.3trn) EXCLUDES private credit funds entirely - they are closed-end, low-leverage and not short-term-wholesale-funded, so they map to no economic function EF1-EF5. Anyone using the narrow measure to track private credit is tracking nothing.
- Form PF has no "private credit fund" strategy category. The Feb-2024 amendments that would create one have been deferred three times, currently to 1 Oct 2026, and the April 2026 SEC/CFTC proposal would shrink Form PF substantially while merely ASKING whether to define private credit funds. Do not build a pipeline that assumes this category will exist.
- AIFMD has no private credit fund entity category (residual) and classifies loans only as "leveraged" or "other". The ECB does not have access to AIFMD data and uses PitchBook. The FCA identifies private credit funds in its OWN regulatory returns using keyword searches and machine learning.
- Every official estimate ultimately rests on Preqin or PitchBook, and OFR documents that the largest and most reputable private credit managers are systematically ABSENT from those databases because they do not need to list themselves for fund discovery. The vendor data under-covers precisely the top of the market. There is no way to correct for this from outside.

WHAT AN ANALYST SHOULD REFUSE TO PRODUCE:
A single "total shadow money created by private credit" number. The measurement bases do not permit it. What can be defended is a bounded statement: the money-creating subset of this channel is the DRAWN balance of bank facilities to private credit vehicles, which at YE2024 was somewhere between USD 54bn (Fed, narrow name-match, Y-14 banks only) and USD 264bn (Call Report business credit intermediaries, all US banks), against a USD 1.4trn outstanding loan book. The rest of the channel is intermediation and borrower leverage, and the rated-note-feeder-annuity chain that gets cited as shadow money creation does not create money at all.

---

# Insurance, annuities and the offshore reinsurance layer: funding agreements (FABN/FABCP/FABR), FHLB funding-agreement advances, PE-affiliated life insurers, and asset-intensive reinsurance ceded to Bermuda and Cayman

**Verdict: `creates_near_money`**

## What it is

Three mechanically distinct structures that regulators and commentators routinely conflate. Keep them separate or every number double-counts.

(1) FUNDING AGREEMENT-BACKED SECURITIES (FABS). A life insurer establishes a bankruptcy-remote SPV. The insurer issues a funding agreement — a deposit-type contract under SSAP No. 52, NOT debt — to the SPV; the funding agreement is the SPV's only asset. The SPV issues notes (FABN), commercial paper (FABCP), or enters a repo (FABR) against it. Buyers are money market funds, pension funds and asset managers. Balance-sheet mechanics: cash goes from the noteholder to the SPV to the insurer's general account; the insurer books an invested asset (Schedule D corporate bonds, mortgages, private credit) and a deposit-type-contract liability. The noteholder has recourse only to the SPV, but the SPV assigns the funding agreement to a trustee, so noteholders rank pari passu with policyholders in insolvency. Terms mirror exactly: currency, coupon basis, maturity, principal. Variants confirmed by NAIC: FXFABN (non-USD, hedged on the insurer's Schedule DB, not at the SPV), FABCP (master funding agreement, ≤360-day paper), FABR (SPV repos the funding agreement to a bank, which may rehypothecate the pledged collateral — this reduces the bank counterparty's regulatory capital charge), FABL (bank-established SPV lends rather than issues), and FABMB (municipal gas/electric prepayment bonds).

(2) FHLB FUNDING AGREEMENTS. The insurer joins a Federal Home Loan Bank, buys activity-based capital stock, pledges collateral, and issues a funding agreement to the FHLB in exchange for cash. Accounted for under SSAP No. 52 as a deposit-type contract, NOT under SSAP No. 15 as borrowed money — the distinction turns entirely on stated use of proceeds. ACLI's position, on the record to the NAIC Macroprudential Working Group: proceeds must be invested in the general account, so this is "operating leverage" and not debt; FHLB advances taken for general corporate purposes ARE debt and go in Note 11. Athene AAIA reports zero SSAP No. 15 FHLB borrowings and $23.27bn of SSAP No. 52 FHLB funding agreements at YE2025.

(3) ASSET-INTENSIVE REINSURANCE. A US-domiciled cedant transfers annuity/deposit liabilities and the matching asset portfolio to an offshore reinsurer, overwhelmingly a Bermuda Class E affiliate. Three legal forms with completely different visibility consequences, and this is the crux:
 - Straight coinsurance: reserve leaves Page 3, assets leave Schedule D, cedant books a reserve credit. This genuinely disappears from NAIC visibility.
 - Modified coinsurance (modco): cedant RETAINS both the assets and the reserve on its own balance sheet; the reinsurer's economics are settled through a modco adjustment. Nothing leaves Schedule D.
 - Funds-withheld coinsurance (COFW): cedant retains the assets as a withheld balance; reinsurer holds a receivable.
The offshore reinsurer files a Capital and Solvency Return with the BMA on an Economic Balance Sheet basis (best-estimate liability plus risk margin, BSCR calibrated to 99% TVaR one-year). The CSR is confidential. What is public is the unconsolidated Form 1SFS statutory financial statement, which is close to useless for sizing because subsidiaries are equity-method.

The linkage that makes this one channel rather than three: the same operating company does all of it simultaneously. Athene Annuity and Life Company at YE2025 held $290.4bn of general-account admitted assets against $287.4bn of general-account liabilities on $4.12bn of capital and surplus, while running $63.0bn of institutional funding agreements, $23.3bn of FHLB funding agreements, and ceding $65.3bn of reserve credit plus $168.3bn of modco reserve plus $65.5bn of funds withheld to Bermuda affiliates.

## Is it money creation?

Split verdict. The two halves of this channel do genuinely different things and must not be argued as one.

FABN/FABCP/FHLB FUNDING AGREEMENTS: near-money creation, not deposit creation. Trace the balance sheet. An institutional prime money market fund buys a $1bn FABN. Before: the MMF holds T-bills or bank CP; a bank deposit sits somewhere. After: the MMF holds a 3-5 year FABN; the SPV holds a funding agreement; the insurer holds newly purchased corporate bonds and private credit; the bond seller holds the deposit that the MMF's cash used to represent. No bank balance sheet expanded. Aggregate deposits are unchanged — the deposit merely changed hands. So on a strict Tobin/Borio test this is intermediation, and anyone claiming FABNs "create money" in the M-aggregate sense is wrong.

But that is the wrong test for the operator's puzzle. What changed is the LIQUIDITY of the claim structure. The ultimate saver's claim is an MMF share: redeemable at par, daily, treated as cash by its holder and reported as cash-equivalent on their balance sheet. The asset ultimately financed is 5-10 year illiquid private credit. The insurer's balance sheet performs the transformation, and it does so outside both bank regulation and the monetary aggregates — institutional MMF shares are not in M2, and the funding agreement is booked as an insurance liability, not debt. So one dollar of pre-existing saving is doing two jobs at once: it remains money to its holder AND it is term funding to a borrower. That is exactly why aggregate saving does not rise and no assets need to be sold while capex gets funded. This is Gurley-Shaw near-money creation by non-banks, and the NAIC's own Macroprudential Working Group calls it, quoting the Fed's framing, hidden leverage or "nontraditional liabilities." The tell that this is a funding channel and not a savings channel: the Z.1 puts the liability inside "Miscellaneous liabilities" (FL543190005), not inside "Life insurance reserves" or "Pension entitlements," so any measure of household saving intermediated through insurers misses it entirely.

Scale check on the near-money claim: FABS outstanding $291.4bn (2026-03-31) plus FHLB funding agreements ~$153bn (2025). The NAIC's own aggregation put FHLB + FABN at $436bn as of 30 June 2025, equal to 10% of the invested assets and 106% of the capital and surplus of the life companies that use them, and 3.4x the industry's $129bn of cash and cash equivalents. That last ratio is the liquidity-transformation measure and it is the one worth watching.

THE OFFSHORE REINSURANCE LAYER: not money creation at all, and I will not inflate it. Ceding an annuity block to Bermuda does not create a new claim on anyone. It is capital relief. What it does is change the QUANTITY OF BALANCE SHEET that can be run against a given amount of capital, because the Bermuda EBS gives credit for illiquidity premia and asset spreads that US statutory reserving and RBC do not. The economic effect is a permissioning effect on leverage, not a monetary one: it lets the same equity support more spread-lending capacity, which in turn lets more FABN and FHLB funding be raised and more private credit be bought. So the reinsurance layer is the enabling condition for the near-money channel rather than a money channel itself. Treat it as a capital-efficiency multiplier, and measure it as such.
