A caution on the strongest version of the argument. FABNs are not puttable. The Fed EFA data shows zero outstanding in the putable and extendible categories across the entire history, and NAIC and ACLI both confirm no embedded optionality in the current market. So the run-risk analogy to ABCP in 2007 does not hold on the liability side as currently structured. The real fragility is roll risk: bullet maturities of 3-5 years against longer assets, with the insurer relying on refinancing. Regulators concede they cannot verify the duration match because they receive no statutory reporting on the notes.

## Size

Every figure below is labelled STOCK or FLOW with vintage and source. Do not add across the groups — see double_counting_risk.

STOCKS
- FABS total outstanding: $291.38bn at 2026-03-31 (Fed EFA, daily). Composition: FABN medium-term $244.42bn; FABN short-term $4.10bn; FABCP $20.87bn; FABR $22.00bn; putable $0.00; extendible $0.00. Non-US-issued only $0.36bn.
- Same measured in Z.1: $291.0bn at 2026Q1, $280.5bn at YE2025 (series FL673090543). Independent cross-check agrees to 0.1%.
- FABS history (Fed EFA, year-end): 2019 $94.22bn; 2020 $116.69bn; 2021 $158.79bn; 2022 $175.76bn; 2023 $188.29bn; 2024 $221.52bn; 2025 $280.83bn.
- FHLB funding-agreement advances to life/annuity insurers: $153bn in 2025, $136bn in 2024 (A.M. Best, "Funding Agreements Drive FHLB Borrowings for the L/A Industry in 2025," 17 June 2026, Note 11 basis).
- All US insurer FHLB advances: $160.6bn BACV at YE2024, +13% on $142.5bn at YE2023, +75.5% since 2019; 84% funding-agreement-backed; collateral pledged $321bn; FHLB capital stock $8.6bn; maximum advances during 2024 $173.5bn (NAIC Capital Markets Bureau, "Capital Stock and Advances Rise for U.S. Insurer FHLB Members in 2024," July 2025).
- Exhibit 7 deposit-type contracts, US life general accounts: $684.50bn at YE2025, of which $130.63bn FHLB funding agreements ($121.68bn at YE2024). Vendor computation over NAIC filings (S&P Global Market Intelligence / A.M. Best), not a primary published series — rebuild it yourself per the recipe.
- NAIC's own combined leverage aggregate: FHLB + FABN = $436.0bn at 30 June 2025 ($175.156bn FHLB + $260.869bn FABN). Context given by NAIC staff: 10% of invested assets, 106% of capital and surplus, 3x cash for the life companies that use these. Sibling categories they list: debt issuance $80bn, surplus notes $51bn, repo $45bn, securities lending $44bn, revolvers/LOC unquantified.
- US life reserves ceded to non-US reinsurers: $1,111.3bn at YE2025; $1,125.3bn at 2026Q1; $1,129.8bn YE2024; $993.9bn YE2023 (Z.1 FL543076035). Companion: reinsured to US reinsurers $1,042.2bn YE2025; direct reserve $8,675.8bn YE2025.
- Bermuda commercial long-term sector: total assets $1,518.2bn at YE2024; total liabilities $1,362.0bn; insurance reserves $1,294.0bn; capital and surplus $156.2bn; C&S/assets 10.3%; reserve leverage 828.7%; median ECR ratio 286%, 10th percentile 158% (BMA, Long-term Insurance Market Analysis and Stress Testing Report, December 2025 edition, YE2024 data). Reserves by line: deferred annuities $453.8bn, mortality $314.3bn, longevity-immediate $131.5bn, annuities certain $77.1bn, longevity-deferred $24.8bn, critical illness $14.5bn, other $30.6bn. US share of reserves 72.5% (2024), 71.8% (2023), 67.0% (2022).
- Cayman international insurance sector: total assets $189.63bn across 721 licences at Q2 2026; reinsurance class 115 licensees, $113.09bn assets, $39.78bn premium; life 43 licensees, $83.79bn assets; deferred variable annuities 7 licensees, $10.52bn assets (CIMA quarterly statistics). Reinsurance assets $101bn at YE2025 vs $23bn at YE2020; licensed reinsurers 113 vs 58.

FLOWS
- FABN net issuance: +$59.3bn in 2025; +$30.9bn in 2024; +$12.3bn in 2023; 2026Q1 at $42.2bn SAAR (Z.1 FA673090543). These are NET of maturities. Gross issuance is not published free — Fitch and S&P have it, both paid.
- Bermuda long-term GWP: $200.1bn in 2024, +17.8%; NWP $152.4bn, +21.2% (BMA). WARNING: this flow is dominated by single-premium block transfers of liabilities that already existed. It is not new saving and must never be read as such.
- Bermuda policyholder claims and benefits paid: $69.0bn in 2024; $301.0bn cumulative over five years (BMA).
- Z.1 flow of reserves reinsured to non-US reinsurers: +$182.0bn (2023), +$133.5bn (2024), −$23.0bn (2025), +$60.9bn SAAR (2026Q1). The 2025 negative print directly contradicts every commercial source reporting record deal volume in 2025. Unresolved — see known_limitations.

ATHENE DATAPOINT — VERIFIED, WITH ONE CORRECTION
Athene Annuity and Life Company (Iowa, NAIC-filed annual statement, YE2025):
- Total admitted assets $340.75bn including separate accounts; $290.35bn excluding. At 2026-03-31 total admitted assets $350.17bn, total liabilities $345.55bn, total capital and surplus $4.62bn. The project's "$350bn / $4.6bn" is the Q1 2026 vintage and is correct.
- YE2025 capital $10.0m + surplus $4,111.8m = $4,121.8m against $287,359.1m of general-account liabilities = 1.43% surplus-to-liability. The project's 1.3% is the Q1 2026 figure (4.62/345.55 = 1.34%). Both correct; state which vintage.
- IMPORTANT CORRECTION: that ratio understates regulatory capital. Total adjusted capital is $9,503.5m because the asset valuation reserve ($5,362.5m) counts as capital for RBC. Surplus + capital + AVR = 3.30% of liabilities, and the RBC ratio is 436% of company action level (TAC $9,503.5m / 2 × ACL $1,090.95m). The 1.3% figure is arithmetically right but will be attacked as unfair if presented without the AVR adjustment. Present both.
- Affiliated investments $52,014.8m at YE2025 (Five-Year Historical line 49), versus $40,452.6m (2024), $22,538.8m (2023), $14,431.6m (2022), $10,357.3m (2021). 5.0x in four years. Confirms ">$50bn."
- COULD NOT VERIFY the ">7% national average" surplus-to-liability benchmark. No primary NAIC aggregate was reachable within this session's search budget. Recipe to build it is in build_recipe step 9. Do not quote 7% until you have computed it.

GENERALISATION BEYOND ATHENE — supported by the data
- PE-owned insurers: 28 of the 541 US insurer FHLB members at YE2024 were PE-owned, 20 of them life companies. They held $25.9bn of outstanding advances (16% of the industry total) and $26.9bn of maximum advances (15%) — roughly 5x their share of member count (NAIC FHLB report). The concentration is real and quantified.
- Offshore concentration: Bermuda is 39.9% of ceded US annuity reserves in 2025 (40.9% in 2024) and roughly 85% of everything ceded to non-US jurisdictions; offshore reinsurers in aggregate take ~56% of ceded annuity reserves. Cayman is 2.3% (2.0% in 2024), ~$75bn of US life liabilities, growing via sidecars. Both figures A.M. Best, August 2026.

**Growth.** FABS outstanding: $94.22bn (2019-12-31) to $291.38bn (2026-03-31) = 3.09x, 19.8% CAGR over 6.25 years. Most recent full year 2024 to 2025: +26.8% ($221.52bn to $280.83bn). Net flow accelerating hard: +$12.3bn (2023), +$30.9bn (2024), +$59.3bn (2025) — the annual net flow nearly quintupled in two years. Fastest-growing sub-component is FABR (funding-agreement-backed repo): $4.0bn (YE2023) to $12.5bn (YE2024) to $22.0bn (2026Q1), 5.5x in just over two years; FABCP is comparatively flat at $17.7bn to $20.9bn.

FHLB funding agreements: $136bn (2024) to $153bn (2025), +12.5%; all US insurer FHLB advances +75.5% from 2019 to 2024; borrowing capacity +18% in 2025 with two-thirds of companies still using under half of it, so the constraint is not capacity.

Bermuda long-term sector: total assets $862.8bn (2020) to $1,518.2bn (2024) = +76%, 15.2% CAGR; 2024 alone +19.1%. Insurance reserves +25.0% in 2024. Deferred annuity reserves specifically +33.0% in 2024 to $453.8bn — that line is the asset-intensive block business and it is growing faster than the sector.

Cayman reinsurance assets: $23bn (2020) to $101bn (YE2025) = 4.4x, ~34% CAGR; licensee count 58 to 113. Small base, fastest growth rate in the channel.

Athene AAIA as the sponsor-owned archetype: deposit-type contract funds $12.74bn (2021) to $64.26bn (2025) = 5.0x, with $27.9bn of that added in 2025 alone; affiliated investments $10.36bn (2021) to $52.01bn (2025) = 5.0x; total admitted assets $106.30bn (2021) to $340.75bn (2025) = 3.2x. Surplus over the same period went $1.27bn to $4.12bn = 3.2x, so leverage is roughly constant — the growth is balance-sheet scaling, not a deterioration in the ratio.

Divergence worth flagging: on the Z.1 basis, reserves ceded to non-US reinsurers went slightly NEGATIVE in 2025 (−$23.0bn) after +$182.0bn and +$133.5bn in 2023-24, while A.M. Best and Alirt both report 2025 as a record deal year ($73bn of new transactions, ten largest unaffiliated deals $106.9bn). Do not reconcile these by averaging. They are different measurement bases and the contradiction is itself the finding.

## Series

| Series | Publisher | Freq | Cost | Gives | Limitations |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Funding Agreement-Backed Securities (FABS), Enhanced Financial Accounts project | Federal Reserve Board | Daily for FABS by maturity type; quarterly for FABCP and FABR. History from 1994-11-14. | free | The only comprehensive FABN measure that exists. Direct CSV at https://www.federalreserve.gov/releases/efa/fabs-chart-data-historical.csv (24 columns, ~11,461 rows, ~2.7MB). Columns: FABS US/Non-US/Total; FABN Medium-Term; FABN Short-Term; FABN Putable; FABN Extendibles; FABN Spinoffs; FABCP; FABR — each split US/Non-US/Total. STOCK only. | Built from Bloomberg Finance LP, Moody's ABCP Program Index, DTCC Solutions LLC and SEC 10-Q/10-K — a vendor-security-master roll-up, not a regulatory return, so an unlisted or privately placed note can be missed. FABCP source switched from Moody's to DTCC in June 2023, breaking that sub-series. Revised without notice: NAIC's February 2026 primer quoted YE2024 at $216.949bn from this exact source; the current file gives $221.52bn. Re-download every time. Coverage of non-USD notes is the known weak point — the file shows only $0.36bn non-US against Bloomberg's $48bn of FX FABNs at YE2024, a discrepancy the NAIC flagged and nobody has resolved. |
| Z.1 Financial Accounts, table S1281.s line 39 / S1281.t line 41 — 'Funding agreements backing securities', series FL673090543 (level) and FA673090543 (flow) | Federal Reserve Board | Quarterly | free | FABN as a life-insurer liability, with a matching quarterly FLOW series — the flow is what the EFA file cannot give you. Level $280.5bn YE2025, $291.0bn 2026Q1. Flow +$12.3bn (2023), +$30.9bn (2024), +$59.3bn (2025). The mirror asset is table S125s1.3.s line 13, same series ID, where the SPV is classified as an Issuer of Asset-Backed Securities. | Z.1 TABLE NUMBERING HAS CHANGED to SNA-style codes — the life insurer tables are now S1281.t/.s (with S1281.1 general accounts and S1281.2 separate accounts), NOT the old L.116/F.116. Anyone searching for L.116 will find nothing. Footnote 3 states the series is set equal to the EFA FABS figure, so this is NOT an independent measurement — it is the same number re-tabled, and agreement between them proves nothing. The general/separate account sub-tables do not break funding agreements out; they sit inside Miscellaneous liabilities (FL543190005, $2,009.2bn GA at YE2025). NJ-domiciled firms are extrapolated. |
| Z.1 table S1281.t/.s memo lines 51-54 — Direct reserve (FA/FL543176015), Reinsured to U.S. reinsurers (543176025), Reinsured to non-U.S. reinsurers (FL543076035 / FA543076035), Reinsured from non-U.S. reinsurers (543176035) | Federal Reserve Board | Published quarterly; effectively annual (see limitations) | free | The only free, no-registration, long-history national series for US life reserves ceded offshore. $1,111.3bn YE2025, $1,125.3bn 2026Q1, $1,129.8bn YE2024, $993.9bn YE2023. This is the single best starting point for the offshore layer. | The quarterly values are straight-line interpolations of an annual figure — the 2025 quarterly flows are literally identical at −23.0, −23.0, −23.0, −23.0. Never read a quarterly turning point off this series. It is a reserve-credit-basis measure, so it counts only the coinsurance slice and structurally MISSES modco and funds-withheld cessions entirely; since those are the fast-growing forms, the series understates economic transfer and can fall while activity rises. That is the likely (unconfirmed) explanation for the negative 2025 print against a record deal year. |
| NAIC Annual Statement, Life/Fraternal blank — Exhibit 7, Deposit-Type Contracts, including footnote (a) FHLB Funding Agreements | NAIC (filed by each insurer; obtain via NAIC Financial Data Repository, S&P Capital IQ Pro, or free from insurer IR pages) | Annual, with quarterly balances | confidential/regulatory | Both STOCK and FLOW for deposit-type contracts at entity level. Line 1 opening balance; line 2 deposits received during the year (GROSS INFLOW — this is the genuine flow measure and it is rare); line 3 investment earnings credited; line 7 net surrender/withdrawal payments; line 9 closing balance before reinsurance; line 14 net after reinsurance, which ties to Page 3 line 3. Six columns: Total, Guaranteed Interest Contracts, Annuities Certain, Supplemental Contracts, Dividend Accumulations, Premium and Other Deposit Funds. Footnote (a) splits out FHLB funding agreements by column. | THE CENTRAL GAP. NAIC states plainly that statutory reporting of FABNs does not exist and that funding agreements backing FABNs cannot be distinguished from funding agreements issued for other purposes or from ordinary GICs. Insurers are also inconsistent about which Exhibit 7 column they use — some put FABN-backing agreements in deposit funds, others in guaranteed investment contracts. Footnote (a) captures ONLY the general account: Athene AAIA shows $568,791,839 there against $23,271,200,000 of actual FHLB funding agreements. Fixes are coming for YE2026 — see the Blanks proposal. |
| NAIC Annual Statement, Notes to Financial Statements, Note 11 (Debt), part B — FHLB Agreements | NAIC (per-insurer filing) | Annual | confidential/regulatory | The CORRECT FHLB measure, and the one the NAIC Capital Markets Bureau itself uses. Sub-note (2) FHLB capital stock by class (membership Class A/B, activity, excess) plus insurer-estimated borrowing capacity. Sub-note (3) collateral pledged, fair value and carrying value, split general vs separate accounts, with maximum pledged during the period. Sub-note (4)(a) borrowing from FHLB in a four-column table — Total \| General Account \| Separate Accounts \| Funding Agreements Reserves Established — across rows (a) Debt, (b) Funding agreements, (c) Other, (d) Aggregate total. This is where the SSAP 15 versus SSAP 52 split is made visible. | Borrowing capacity in (2)(f) is 'as determined by the insurer' — self-reported and not comparable across filers. The narrative text and the tables can disagree in presentation; Athene's narrative gives $22,976.9m (the reserve established) while table (4)(a) gives $23,271.2m (the principal borrowed). Use the table, and state which of the two you took. |
| NAIC Annual Statement, Schedule S Part 3 Section 1 — Reinsurance Ceded Life Insurance, Annuities, Deposit Funds and Other Liabilities Without Life or Disability Contingencies | NAIC (per-insurer filing) | Annual | confidential/regulatory | The transaction-level ground truth for the offshore layer, and the only place the modco and funds-withheld amounts appear. Fifteen columns: 1 NAIC company code; 2 ID number; 3 effective date; 4 name of company; 5 domiciliary jurisdiction; 6 type of reinsurance ceded; 7 type of business ceded; 8 amount in force; 9/10 reserve credit taken current/prior year; 11 premiums; 12/13 outstanding surplus relief current/prior; 14 modified coinsurance reserve; 15 funds withheld under coinsurance. Subtotalled by authorisation status (authorized / unauthorized / certified / reciprocal jurisdiction) crossed with US vs non-US and affiliate vs non-affiliate. Key roll-ups: 9199999 Total U.S., 9299999 Total non-U.S., 9999999 Totals. Part 3 Section 2 is the A&H equivalent; Parts 1-2 cover assumed; Part 6 is the reinsurance recoverable ageing. | TRAP: one reinsurer appears under MULTIPLE identifiers in different subtotal blocks. Athene Annuity Re Ltd appears as AA-3191361 (older treaties, unauthorized alien) AND as RJ-3191540 (2024 treaties, reciprocal jurisdiction). Filter on either alone and you halve the answer. Match on name and domicile, not ID prefix. Type-of-reinsurance codes must be parsed: CO/I and CO/G coinsurance individual/group, MCO/I and MCO/G modified coinsurance, COFW/I coinsurance funds withheld, YRT/I yearly renewable term, OTH/I other. Only YRT and mortality business should be excluded when isolating asset-intensive deals. There is no machine-readable public download; you are parsing filed PDFs or paying a vendor. |
| NAIC Capital Markets Bureau Special Report — U.S. insurer FHLB exposure (annual series) | NAIC Capital Markets Bureau | Annual | free | The pre-aggregated industry FHLB numbers so you do not have to parse 541 filings: advances outstanding, maximum advances, capital stock by class, collateral pledged, all split by statement type and by assets-under-management band, plus an explicit private-equity-owned insurer cut. YE2024 edition published July 2025. | The YE2024 edition contains a transposed sentence in the executive summary (membership 'decreased by 3% from year-end 2024 to year-end 2023'); the tables are right, the prose is not. Table 3 maximum advances totals $175,325.1m while the narrative says $173.5bn — an internal inconsistency, so cite the table. The PE-owned cut depends on an NAIC-internal ownership mapping that is not published, so it cannot be reproduced or audited externally. |
| NAIC Capital Markets Bureau Primer — Funding Agreement-Backed Notes (February 2026), and Macroprudential (E) Working Group FABN materials (July 2025, 7 November 2025) | NAIC | One-off primer plus ongoing working-group call materials | free | Mechanics of every FABN variant, and — more valuable — the regulators' own written admission of what they cannot see, plus the ACLI's on-the-record counter-position. The 7 November 2025 materials contain the $436bn FHLB+FABN aggregate at 30/6/2025, the Bloomberg-versus-Fed discrepancy, and the draft Exhibit 7 disclosure format. Call materials are at content.naic.org/sites/default/files/call_materials/. | The primer's appendix chart uses the pre-revision Fed vintage (YE2024 $216,949m). Working-group materials are slide decks, not audited data; the aggregates in them are staff calculations. |
| Bermuda Long-term Insurance Market Analysis and Stress Testing Report | Bermuda Monetary Authority | Annual (third edition, December 2025, published April 2026) | free | The only public aggregate for the Bermuda long-term sector, drawn from Class C/D/E Capital and Solvency Returns. Table 4.1.1 sectoral highlights; 4.1.3 balance-sheet indicators; 4.1.5 income statement; 4.1.6 capital structure; 4.2.1 RoA/RoE/RoI; 4.3.1 reserves by line of business; 4.3.3 geographic distribution of reserves; section 5 stress results by scenario R1-R9 from Schedule V(e). | Aggregate only; no entity detail, no counterparty detail, no security-level asset data. The BMA warns explicitly that IFRS amendments limit year-over-year aggregation and comparability, that the firm cohort changes between editions, and that resubmissions change prior aggregates. Valuation is EBS best-estimate-liability plus risk margin — NOT comparable to US statutory reserves, so you cannot difference it against NAIC numbers. Mixes GAAP and IFRS filers. Class A/B (captive) business is excluded. |
| Bermuda statutory financial statements, Form 1SFS (unconsolidated) — e.g. Athene Life Re Ltd. | Filed under the Insurance Act 1978 / Insurance Account Rules 2016; obtainable from group investor-relations pages | Annual, some quarterly | free | Entity-level Bermuda balance sheet: cash, quoted/unquoted investments, investments in and advances to affiliates (equity method), insurance reserves, other liabilities, total statutory capital and surplus. | UNCONSOLIDATED, therefore close to useless for sizing a block. Athene Life Re Ltd shows YE2025 total assets of $45.65bn and statutory capital and surplus of $13.70bn — a fraction of the economic footprint — because subsidiaries sit at equity method ($8.90bn of investments in and advances to affiliates). The consolidated Economic Balance Sheet inside the Capital and Solvency Return is confidential and never published. Do not build a size estimate on Form 1SFS. |
| Cayman Islands insurance statistics | Cayman Islands Monetary Authority (CIMA) | Quarterly (international sector), annual (domestic audited) | free | International Insurance Company by Primary Class of Business — licensee counts, total premium, total asset value across 17 business classes including Life, Deferred Variable Annuities and Reinsurance. Plus Insurance Company by Risk Location, and the Licensing Activity Report back to 1997. | Aggregate class tables only. No entity filings, no CSR equivalent, no reserve data, no counterparty data, no asset composition, no US-cedant linkage. Classes overlap so the columns do not sum cleanly to a sector total. Sidecars — the actual growth driver — are inside the Reinsurance class with no separate identification. Cayman is the genuine blind spot in this channel and the fastest-growing part of it. |
| A.M. Best special reports on FHLB borrowings and on offshore life/annuity reinsurance domiciles | A.M. Best | Annual | paid | Industry FHLB funding-agreement totals on the Note 11 basis ($153bn in 2025 vs $136bn in 2024; capacity +18%; utilisation distribution), and the ceded-reserve market-share series by domicile (Bermuda 39.9% of ceded US annuity reserves in 2025 vs 40.9% in 2024; Cayman 2.3% vs 2.0%; offshore in aggregate ~56%; Cayman managing ~$75bn of US life liabilities; ten largest unaffiliated 2025 deals $106.9bn). | Paid, and the underlying company-level workings are not disclosed, so shares cannot be independently reproduced. Best and Alirt use different denominators — Best quotes shares of ceded ANNUITY reserves, Alirt of ALL ceded life and annuity liabilities — which is why Bermuda appears as 39.9% in one and 40.7% in the other for the same year. Always state the denominator. |
| Actuarial Guideline 55 asset adequacy testing reports for reinsurance | NAIC / state insurance departments | Annual | confidential/regulatory | Cash-flow-testing-based asset adequacy analysis for in-scope asset-intensive reinsurance, requiring the cedant to demonstrate that ceded liabilities remain supported under moderately adverse conditions. Scope targets large affiliated funded reinsurance and certain third-party deals. Adopted at the 2025 Summer National Meeting; FIRST REPORTS DUE 1 APRIL 2026. | NOT PUBLIC. Filed to the domiciliary regulator. Whether any aggregate will ever be published is undecided. Flag it as a future unlock, not a current source — but it is the first instrument that will let any regulator see through a modco or funds-withheld cession to the supporting assets. |
| FHLB Office of Finance Combined Financial Report | Federal Home Loan Banks Office of Finance | Quarterly and annual | free | The lender-side control total: system-wide advances outstanding, member counts by type (insurers were 6% of ~6,500 members at YE2024; commercial banks 60% by principal amount of advances), and consolidated obligation issuance — which is the funding leg that connects this channel to money market funds. | Does not break advances out by insurer member, and does not distinguish funding-agreement-evidenced advances from ordinary advances. Use it only as a top-down sanity check on the NAIC Note 11 aggregate, never as a substitute. |

## Build recipe

Nine steps. Steps 1-4 are free and reproducible today. Steps 5-8 need NAIC filings. Step 9 is the benchmark I could not close.

STEP 1 — FABN stock, free, ten minutes.
curl -sL -o fabs.csv https://www.federalreserve.gov/releases/efa/fabs-chart-data-historical.csv
Daily, 1994-11-14 to 2026-03-31, ~11,461 rows. Take column "FABS (Total)" as the headline STOCK. Take "FABN - Medium-Term (Total)", "FABN - Short-Term (Total)", "FABCP (Total)", "FABR (Total)" for composition. Verify "FABN - Putable (Total)" and "FABN - Extendibles (Total)" are zero — they are, across the entire history, and that is the single most important structural fact about the liability side. Compute year-end values by taking the last observation on or before 31 December. You should reproduce: 2019 94.22, 2020 116.69, 2021 158.79, 2022 175.76, 2023 188.29, 2024 221.52, 2025 280.83, and 291.38 at 2026-03-31. If you get 216.949 for 2024 you are looking at the pre-revision vintage quoted in NAIC's February 2026 primer — re-download.

STEP 2 — FABN flow, free.
Take the Z.1 CSV bundle at https://www.federalreserve.gov/releases/z1/20260611/z1_csv_files.zip, or read the release PDF at .../z1.pdf. Pull FA673090543 from table S1281.t line 41 (identically S125s1.3.t line 15). Annual: +12.3 (2023), +30.9 (2024), +59.3 (2025); 2026Q1 SAAR 42.2. Confirm the flow reconciles to the first difference of the level FL673090543 (190.3 → 221.2 → 280.5): it does, exactly. Do NOT search for tables L.116 or F.116 — the Z.1 renumbered to SNA codes and life insurers are now S1281.t/.s, with S1281.1 general accounts and S1281.2 separate accounts. Note that the FABN liability sits inside Miscellaneous liabilities (FL543190005), not inside reserves, so it is invisible to any reserve-based measure of insurer liabilities.

STEP 3 — offshore ceded reserves, free, the fastest useful cut.
From the same Z.1, take memo line 50 of S1281.s / line 53 of S1281.t: FL543076035 and FA543076035, "Reinsured to non-U.S. reinsurers." Levels 993.9 (2023), 1129.8 (2024), 1111.3 (2025), 1125.3 (2026Q1). Pair with FL543176025 (reinsured to US reinsurers, 1042.2 at YE2025) and FL543176015 (direct reserve, 8675.8). Before using the quarterlies, check whether the four flow values within a year are identical — in 2025 they are all −23.0, which means the Fed interpolated an annual figure. Treat the series as annual. And record that this is a RESERVE-CREDIT basis, so it structurally omits modco and funds-withheld; that omission is why it can print negative in a record deal year.

STEP 4 — Bermuda and Cayman aggregates, free.
Bermuda: pull the BMA Long-term Insurance Market Analysis and Stress Testing Report and lift tables 4.1.1, 4.1.3, 4.1.5, 4.1.6, 4.2.1, 4.3.1 and figure 4.3.3. Apply the US reserve share (72.5% for 2024) to insurance reserves ($1,294.0bn) to get a US-attributable Bermuda reserve of roughly $938bn. Carry the 16-month lag as an explicit column in your output; do not silently align YE2024 Bermuda data against YE2025 US data.
Cayman: take the quarterly "International Insurance Company by Primary Class of Business" table from cima.ky/insurance-statistics. Q2 2026: Reinsurance 115 licensees / $39.78bn premium / $113.09bn assets; Life 43 / $21.56bn / $83.79bn; Deferred Variable Annuities 7 / $0.69bn / $10.52bn. Do not sum the classes — they overlap.

STEP 5 — FHLB, the correct way. Note 11, not Exhibit 7.
For each life filer, go to Notes to Financial Statements, Note 11 (Debt), part B, sub-note (4)(a) "Borrowing from FHLB — Amount as of the reporting date." Take row (b) Funding agreements, column (1) Total. Then take columns (2) General Account and (3) Separate Accounts separately, because that split is the whole point. Worked example, Athene AAIA YE2025: Total $23,271,200,000 = GA $570,000,000 + SA $22,701,200,000, with funding-agreement reserves established of $22,976,877,526 in column (4). Now compare against Exhibit 7 footnote (a), which reports $568,791,839 — the general-account slice only, understating by 97.6%.
Industry reconciliation, and it closes almost exactly: A.M. Best (Note 11 basis) gives $153bn of FA-backed FHLB advances for 2025; the Exhibit 7 general-account footnote aggregate is $130.63bn; the $22.37bn difference is separate-account FHLB funding agreements, and Athene alone contributes $22.70bn of it. One company is essentially the entire gap. Anyone building this series off Exhibit 7 has a Athene-shaped hole in it.

STEP 6 — the institutional funding agreement residual, which is where FABN actually hides.
Per filer: Exhibit 7 line 9 column 2 (Guaranteed Interest Contracts, balance end of year before reinsurance) MINUS Note 11(4)(a) row (b) column (2) (general-account FHLB funding agreements). Athene AAIA YE2025: $63,592,705,381 − $570,000,000 = $63.02bn. That residual is institutional funding agreements — FABN-backing plus ordinary GICs — with no line item anywhere separating the two. NAIC states this outright: funding agreements backing FABNs cannot be distinguished from funding agreements issued for other purposes or from GICs. Also grab the genuine FLOW from the same exhibit: line 2 deposits received during the year ($26,534,997,698 for Athene in 2025), line 3 investment earnings credited ($2,649,832,829), line 7 net surrender and withdrawal payments ($6,236,134,893). Exhibit 7 is one of very few places in the whole map where a gross inflow is published rather than a net stock change. Cross-check the closing balance against Page 3 line 3 and against Five-Year Historical line 25 — for Athene, $64,259,784,362 on the Page 3 basis after reinsurance, up from $36,359,016,400 at YE2024.
Sanity-check your bottom-up total against the vendor aggregate of $684.50bn of Exhibit 7 deposit-type contracts at YE2025.

STEP 7 — the offshore cession, at transaction level. Schedule S Part 3 Section 1.
Parse the schedule and take the roll-up lines: 9199999 Total U.S., 9299999 Total non-U.S., 9999999 Totals. Read columns 9 (reserve credit taken, current year), 11 (premiums), 14 (modified coinsurance reserve) and 15 (funds withheld under coinsurance) as FOUR SEPARATE MEASURES. They are not additive and they mean different things.
Athene AAIA YE2025, line 9299999 (non-US): reserve credit $65,263,421,108 (prior year $54,210,562,940); premiums $35,191,584,869; modco reserve $168,291,336,691; funds withheld under coinsurance $65,543,636,517. The modco figure decomposes as general account $116,092,703,380 (line 4599999) plus separate accounts $52,198,633,311 (line 9099999) — verify that it sums, as a parse check.
THE TRAP: filter on name and domicile, never on ID prefix. Athene Annuity Re Ltd (Bermuda) appears twice under two identifiers in two different subtotal blocks — AA-3191361 for pre-2024 treaties in the "unauthorized non-U.S. affiliates" block, and RJ-3191540 for 2024 treaties in the "reciprocal jurisdiction non-U.S. affiliates" block. Same counterparty, and taking one block halves your answer.
To isolate asset-intensive business, keep type-of-reinsurance codes CO, MCO and COFW and drop YRT; keep type-of-business codes FA, IA, OA and VA (fixed, indexed, other and variable annuity) and drop OL, ADB, DIS, XXXL and AXXX, which are mortality and morbidity.

STEP 8 — what actually leaves NAIC visibility, stated precisely. This is where most commentary is wrong.
Only the straight-coinsurance slice disappears: the reserve leaves Page 3, the assets leave Schedule D, and a reserve credit appears. For Athene that is $65.3bn.
Under modco ($168.3bn) and funds-withheld coinsurance ($65.5bn), the assets STAY on the cedant's Schedule D and the reserve STAYS on Page 3. Nothing vanishes from the securities detail. What moves offshore is the economics: the investment discretion, the spread, and the capital benefit. So the correct statement is not "assets disappear" but "for two-thirds of the transferred book, the assets remain visible while the risk and the return do not." For Athene the general-account modco reserve alone is 40% of general-account liabilities and funds withheld another 23%.
Build the ratio (col 9 + col 14 + col 15) / general-account liabilities as an "economic cession intensity" measure, and report the three components separately underneath it. Never report the sum as a single number without the decomposition.

STEP 9 — the benchmark I could not close, and how to close it.
The ">7% national average surplus-to-liability" comparator in the project brief is UNVERIFIED. I could not reach a primary NAIC aggregate within this session's search budget, and I will not supply a plausible-looking substitute. To build it: from the NAIC Financial Data Repository (or S&P Capital IQ Pro), pull Page 3 line 26 (total liabilities excluding separate accounts) and Page 3 line 38 (total capital and surplus) for every life and fraternal filer, and take the ratio of sums, not the mean of ratios.
When you do, present Athene on two bases, because the one-basis version will be attacked. YE2025: capital + surplus of $4,121,783,748 over general-account liabilities of $287,359,053,515 = 1.43%. But total adjusted capital is $9,503,503,398 because the asset valuation reserve of $5,362,457,556 counts toward RBC, giving 3.30% and an RBC ratio of 436% of company action level (TAC / 2 × authorized control level of $1,090,950,938). Both numbers are true. The 1.43% is the right one for a leverage discussion and the 436% is the right one for a solvency discussion, and conflating them in either direction is the mistake to avoid.
Whatever industry denominator you build, apply the identical AVR treatment on both sides.

FORWARD WATCH. The NAIC Macroprudential (E) Working Group exposed a Blanks proposal in November 2025 adding FABN disclosures to SSAP No. 52, targeted at year-end 2026: funding agreements backing FABNs grouped by remaining years to maturity crossed with fixed versus floating rate, a currency-denomination table, and two yes/no interrogatories (whether any funding agreements backing FABNs have embedded options, and whether all funding agreement terms mirror the FABN terms). If adopted, the YE2026 statements filed 1 March 2027 will be the first time the FABN stock is measurable from regulatory filings rather than from a Bloomberg security master. Separately, first Actuarial Guideline 55 reports were due 1 April 2026, though they are confidential.

## Double-counting risk

Six specific overlaps. Several of these will bite an analyst who is being careful in every other respect.

1. FABN AND FHLB AND EXHIBIT 7 ARE NESTED, NOT PARALLEL. FABN-backing funding agreements ($291.4bn) and FHLB funding agreements ($153bn) are BOTH funding agreements and BOTH sit inside the $684.50bn of Exhibit 7 general-account deposit-type contracts — except for the separate-account FHLB portion, which sits outside Exhibit 7 entirely. So FABN + FHLB + Exhibit 7 triple-counts, while Exhibit 7 alone under-counts by the separate-account slice. There is no clean total. The defensible presentation is Exhibit 7 as the general-account base, plus the separate-account FHLB residual, with FABN and FHLB shown as overlapping subsets underneath rather than as additions.

2. THE NAIC'S OWN $436BN AGGREGATE IS ITSELF SLIGHTLY DOUBLE-COUNTED. NAIC staff added FHLB $175.156bn + FABN $260.869bn at 30/6/2025. Both legs are funding agreements issued by the same companies and both are inside Exhibit 7's general-account total for the general-account portion. It is a useful leverage indicator and I have quoted it as one, but it is a sum across two measures of the same instrument class and should not be treated as a distinct quantum of liability.

3. FABR OVERLAPS THE REPO CHANNEL. Funding-agreement-backed repo is $22.0bn at 2026Q1 and it is inside the FABS total. But it is also a repo: the SPV repos the funding agreement to a bank, and the bank may rehypothecate the pledged collateral. Whoever is mapping repo and collateral reuse will see the same transaction from the other side. Coordinate on this one explicitly or it gets counted twice, once as insurer near-money and once as dealer collateral velocity.

4. FHLB COLLATERAL OVERLAPS THE SECURITIES-FINANCING CHANNEL. Insurers pledged $321bn of collateral to the FHLB system at YE2024 against $160.6bn of advances. Those pledged securities are the same Schedule D bonds that also appear in securities lending ($58.6bn industry-wide per Z.1 at YE2025) and repo ($36.0bn). Encumbrance is being counted against a single stock of collateral. Also note the FHLB itself funds by issuing consolidated obligations largely into money market funds, so the FHLB leg and any GSE-debt or MMF channel in the wider map are the same flow observed at two points.

5. THE BERMUDA BALANCE SHEET AND THE US BALANCE SHEET CONTAIN THE SAME LIABILITIES, AND FOR MODCO THE SAME ASSETS TWICE. Never add BMA total assets ($1,518.2bn at YE2024) to US life general-account assets. The Bermuda reserves are US-originated liabilities, 72.5% of them, and under modified coinsurance and funds-withheld coinsurance the SUPPORTING ASSETS ARE STILL ON THE US CEDANT'S SCHEDULE D while simultaneously appearing on the Bermuda reinsurer's Economic Balance Sheet. For Athene that is $168.3bn of modco plus $65.5bn of funds withheld sitting on both sides of the same transaction. This is the single largest double-count available in this channel and it is very easy to make.

6. THE THREE MEASURES OF OFFSHORE CESSION DISAGREE BY CONSTRUCTION AND MUST NOT BE AVERAGED. Z.1 FL543076035 gives $1,111.3bn at YE2025 on a reserve-credit basis, which omits modco and funds-withheld. Summing Schedule S line 9299999 column 9 across all filers gives a statutory reserve-credit figure on a different valuation basis. BMA insurance reserves × US share gives roughly $938bn at YE2024 on an EBS best-estimate-liability-plus-risk-margin basis. Three numbers, three valuation regimes, three vintages. Report them side by side with their bases named. Do not average them, do not difference them to derive a "gap," and do not sum them.

Also, per the project rule: nothing in this channel is a market capitalisation and I have not used one. But note the inverse trap specific to insurance — Bermuda GWP of $200.1bn in 2024 looks like a flow of new business and is mostly the single-premium transfer of liabilities that already existed on a US balance sheet. It is a change of custodian, not a creation of anything. Treating it as new funding would be the insurance-sector equivalent of treating market cap as money.

## What cannot be measured

Stated plainly, because the gaps are the finding.

1. FABN BY ISSUER. Does not exist. NAIC's own words: statutory reporting of FABNs by insurers does not exist. Regulators wrote "We have to rely on the Fed for this data" in their own November 2025 slide deck. You cannot answer "which insurer has how much FABN outstanding" from any regulatory source today. The proposed Blanks change targets YE2026, so the earliest possible answer is March 2027.

2. THE FX FABN DISCREPANCY IS UNRESOLVED AND LARGE. At YE2024 the Fed EFA series showed essentially nil non-US FABS ($0.357bn) while Bloomberg showed $48bn of FX FABNs. Total FABN at the same date: Fed $217bn, Bloomberg $135bn. Two vendor-sourced measures of the same market differ by 60% in one direction on the total and by two orders of magnitude on the FX component. NAIC flagged this and nobody has reconciled it. My own analysis uses the Fed series because it is free, primary-published and internally consistent, but I cannot demonstrate it is right. Any FX-denominated funding agreement analysis is currently unfounded.

3. WHETHER FUNDING AGREEMENTS HAVE EMBEDDED PUTS. Regulators do not know. The NAIC wrote that they do not know whether extendible FABNs with embedded put options are issued or outstanding, and do not know whether FX FABNs and FX funding agreements are issued or outstanding. The Fed series shows zero putable and zero extendible, and ACLI asserts none exist. That assertion is unaudited. Since put risk is the entire liquidity-run case, the most important single parameter in this channel rests on an industry body's say-so.

4. DURATION MATCHING BETWEEN THE FUNDING AGREEMENT, THE NOTE AND THE ASSETS. Regulators state directly that they receive no statutory reporting allowing them to determine whether the maturity of the funding agreement and the investment agrees with the FABN issued. Theoretically they mirror. It cannot be verified from outside, and it cannot be verified from inside either.

5. HEDGE-TO-LIABILITY MAPPING. ACLI told the NAIC it is not feasible to map hedges to funding agreements, because hedges are not one-to-one and some insurers currency-match with assets rather than derivatives. So Schedule DB derivatives cannot be tied to the FX funding agreements they cover. Basis risk in this channel is unmeasurable.

6. BERMUDA ENTITY-LEVEL ECONOMICS. The consolidated Economic Balance Sheet inside the Capital and Solvency Return is confidential. The public Form 1SFS is unconsolidated and therefore misleading by an order of magnitude — Athene Life Re Ltd shows $45.65bn of total assets against a group footprint several times that, because subsidiaries are equity-method. There is no public Bermuda equivalent of Schedule D. Asset composition, counterparty concentration and affiliated-asset percentages at entity level are simply unavailable.

7. CAYMAN, ALMOST ENTIRELY. No entity filings, no reserve data, no CSR equivalent, no cedant linkage, no asset detail. CIMA publishes aggregate class tables and nothing else. Sidecars — the actual growth mechanism, per A.M. Best — sit inside the Reinsurance class with no separate identification. Cayman is the fastest-growing part of the channel (4.4x since 2020) and the least visible. If the measurement question is "where would you go to hide from this map," the answer is Cayman, and I can demonstrate that from the absence of sources rather than the presence of any.

8. LOOK-THROUGH ON AFFILIATED AND RELATED-PARTY INVESTMENTS. Five-Year Historical lines 44-49 give the total ($52.01bn for Athene AAIA at YE2025) and Schedule D names the security, but the schedule does not consistently reveal where the ultimate obligor sits relative to the sponsor. Sponsor-originated private credit held through an unaffiliated-looking wrapper is not reliably identifiable. The affiliation flag is a legal-entity test, not an economic one.

9. GROSS ISSUANCE VERSUS NET. Every free FABN series is a stock or a net flow. Gross issuance and gross maturities are only in Fitch and S&P, both paid. This matters because a 3-5 year bullet market with $291bn outstanding is rolling roughly $60-90bn a year, and rollover volume is not new funding. If you see a large "issuance" number quoted anywhere, establish whether it is gross before using it.

10. THE 2025 CESSION CONTRADICTION. The Z.1 says reserves ceded to non-US reinsurers FELL by $23.0bn in 2025. A.M. Best and Alirt both say 2025 was a record year, with $73bn of new transactions and the ten largest unaffiliated deals totalling $106.9bn. I can construct a plausible explanation — the Z.1 line is reserve-credit basis and therefore blind to modco and funds-withheld structures, which are exactly the growing forms, and it also nets recaptures — but I have not verified that explanation and I am not going to assert it. Record it as an open contradiction between two primary-adjacent sources.

11. THE ">7% NATIONAL AVERAGE" SURPLUS-TO-LIABILITY BENCHMARK IS UNVERIFIED. It appears in the project brief. I could not reach a primary NAIC industry aggregate in this session. It is plausible on its face and I still will not carry it forward as established. Recipe to build it is step 9.

12. PENSION RISK TRANSFER VOLUMES ARE NOT IN THIS OUTPUT. LIMRA is the standard source; both attempted fetches returned 404 and the session's web search budget was exhausted before I could route around it. Rather than substitute a remembered figure, I am recording it as not retrieved. What I can say from primary data: BMA Table 4.3.1 shows Bermuda longevity-immediate reserves of $131.5bn (+9.1% in 2024) and longevity-deferred of $24.8bn (+76.3%), which bounds the offshore-reinsured portion of the longevity book but is not a PRT sales figure and must not be presented as one.

---

# Securitisation and risk transfer: CLOs (BSL, middle-market, private-credit), esoteric/whole-business/data-centre ABS, GPU-and-compute ABF, equipment ABS, and Significant Risk Transfer / synthetic securitisation (SRT)

**Verdict: `creates_credit_not_money`**

## What it is

Two mechanically different things are routinely lumped together under "securitisation", and they have opposite balance-sheet consequences. Keep them apart or the whole exercise fails.

(1) TRUE-SALE SECURITISATION (BSL CLOs, middle-market/private-credit CLOs, auto/card/equipment ABS, whole-business ABS, data-centre and fibre ABS/CMBS, GPU-backed ABF). An originator sells a defined pool into a bankruptcy-remote SPV. The SPV funds the purchase by issuing tranched notes to third parties. The loans LEAVE the originator's balance sheet (sale accounting). The originator receives cash and typically retires the warehouse borrowing that funded pool accumulation. Note the lifecycle: the collateral is almost never created by the securitisation. SFA (23 Jul 2026) is explicit for data centres — construction loans, project finance, private credit and corporate bonds carry development risk, and ABS/CMBS refinance stabilised, leased, cash-flowing assets afterwards. Securitisation is a TAKEOUT, not a primary funding event. Barclays estimates average deal size ~$600m for data-centre ABS and ~$1.2bn for CMBS, below the largest AI campuses, which is why corporate bonds and private credit remain the front end.

(2) SYNTHETIC SECURITISATION / SRT. Nothing is sold. The bank retains legal and economic ownership of the loans and keeps them on balance sheet. It buys credit protection on a mezzanine (EU/UK) or first-loss (US) tranche of a reference portfolio via a guarantee, a CDS, or credit-linked notes issued directly by the bank or by an SSPE. The protection seller — overwhelmingly an NBFI: specialist credit funds, hedge funds, pension funds, insurers, and in the euro area development banks including the EIF — posts cash collateral upfront in the funded structures that dominate the market (BCBS: 85–90% of US transactions funded; all UK transactions to date funded). The bank retains the senior tranche at a floored risk weight (15% under Basel; the EU cut the STS on-balance-sheet floor to 10% in April 2021). The consequence is a fall in RWA and a rise in the CET1 ratio with no change in assets, no change in deposits, and no security necessarily issued at all.

BCBS d607 (Feb 2026) Annex worked example: a €650m RWA portfolio tranched 1% junior / 7% mezzanine / 92% retained senior falls to €263m RWA once the mezzanine is hedged — roughly 60% RWA reduction against 8% of the pool being protected.

Structural detail that matters for measurement: EU/UK funded SRTs increasingly bypass the SPV. BCBS reports the SPV share of funded euro-area/UK/Canadian SRTs fell from about two thirds in 2019 to 15% in 2024, with CLNs increasingly issued directly by the originating bank. A directly-issued CLN is a bank debt security, not a securitisation note, so it disappears from securitisation statistics entirely. US SRTs are more often CDS-structured, partly because CDS satisfies US capital-relief criteria more cleanly.

Motive split (the analytically load-bearing distinction). True-sale is predominantly a FUNDING and capital-recycling transaction: it converts an illiquid loan into cash and shifts the funding claim from the originator's balance sheet to end-investors. SRT is almost purely a CAPITAL RELIEF transaction: it raises no funding for the bank (the collateral is not a bank funding source in economic substance; the bank was already funded) and exists to relax the regulatory capital constraint. ESRB (May 2025) finds virtually all EU synthetic securitisations achieve significant risk transfer, versus about one quarter of traditional securitisations — confirming capital relief is the point of the synthetic segment.

## Is it money creation?

Short answer: true-sale securitisation is intermediation and destroys, on net, a small amount of bank-created money; SRT creates no money but manufactures the bank capital that permits money creation elsewhere. A third, currently small, leg — banks repo-financing the investors who sell them protection — creates regulatory capital out of nothing and is the closest genuine analogue to what the operator is describing. Argue it from the balance sheet.

TRUE SALE — INTERMEDIATION, with a monetary unwind.
Before: bank holds loan (asset) and, from origination, a deposit (liability) it created. An asset manager or insurer holds a deposit.
After: SPV holds the loan; the investor holds an ABS/CLO note; the investor's deposit has been transferred to the bank as sale proceeds; the bank retires funding.
Net deposits in the system: unchanged at the moment of sale, and reduced to the extent the originator uses the proceeds to repay its own borrowing. The investor's purchasing power is converted from a monetary claim into a non-monetary one. This is textbook intermediation: existing savings are moved, not created. The operator's premise — savings rates flat, other assets not being sold — is a real constraint here, and it binds. Data-centre ABS cannot be the source of the capex boom; SFA's own lifecycle description says it arrives after the asset is built and leased.

The two caveats that make true-sale non-trivial:
(a) The WAREHOUSE. A CLO or ABS pool is accumulated on a bank-provided revolving warehouse facility. Bank lending to a warehouse SPV creates a deposit exactly as any bank loan does. That deposit is destroyed when the CLO prices and the warehouse is repaid. Because the pipeline is continuous, the aggregate stock of warehouse lines outstanding is a persistent, revolving quantum of bank-created money supporting the securitisation machine. It is not published anywhere (see gaps).
(b) BALANCE-SHEET VELOCITY. The same warehouse line and the same origination capital finance several pools per year. What multiplies is originating capacity, not the money stock. This is the honest form of the "invisible funding" intuition for the true-sale segment, and it is a velocity story, not a stock story.

SRT — CAPITAL CREATION, WHICH IS THE BINDING CONSTRAINT ON MONEY CREATION.
Before: bank holds €1bn of corporate loans, RWA €650m, deposits created at origination sit on the liability side. Fund holds €70m of deposits.
After: fund's €70m moves to a collateral account (funded CLN) — still a deposit somewhere in the system, frequently at the issuing bank itself. Bank's RWA falls to ~€263m. Bank's CET1 amount is unchanged; its CET1 RATIO rises. No asset moved. No deposit was created or destroyed. M1–M3: literally unchanged.
Then the second step, which is the whole point: the bank redeploys the freed capital into new lending, and NEW BANK LENDING CREATES NEW DEPOSITS.

This second step is not conjecture — it is measured. Osberghaus and Schepens (17 Oct 2025, using ECB AnaCredit + COREP + SHSS, presented at BIS) regress bank outcomes on SRT intensity and find: a 1 s.d. rise in quarterly SRT intensity raises loan growth by ~0.9% relative to mean; a 1 s.d. rise in annual SRT intensity cuts the regulatory leverage ratio by ~1% relative to mean; and the Tier 1 capital RATIO shows no significant change. Their own arithmetic: a €525m SRT is associated with roughly €570m of additional assets. Banks hold their capital ratio constant and grow the balance sheet. That is the mechanism the operator is looking for, and it is invisible to monetary aggregates because the resulting deposits look like ordinary bank lending — the causal step upstream (a capital constraint being relaxed by an NBFI) leaves no trace in M1–M3, in securities issuance statistics, or in the flow of funds.

Scale of the capital effect: BIS Quarterly (16 Mar 2026, Babu/Chui/Stephanou) puts average capital relief across 44 SRT-issuing banks at ~43bp of CET1, exceeding 100bp in a few cases, against sector-wide CET1 of 14–16%. Autonomous Research (via BCBS) estimates 47bp for the average listed EU/UK/Swiss bank at Q4 2024, with ~12% of the average corporate loan book covered and several banks above a quarter. That is a 3% relative increase in usable capital for the average bank, and materially more for the heavy users; Osberghaus/Schepens find 35 euro-area banks with more than 10% of the corporate book synthetically transferred, 15 above 25%.

THE CIRCULAR LEG — SYNTHETIC CAPITAL, EX NIHILO.
If the bank (or another bank) lends against the CLN the protection seller bought, the protection absorbing the bank's credit losses is itself funded by bank debt. Osberghaus and Schepens quantify this precisely: a loan to an SRT investor binds on average about €230,000 of capital (weighted average risk weight on such loans ~29%) but frees around €5,000,000. The banking system as a whole is stripped of roughly €4.77m of capital per average debt-financed SRT. That is capital created out of nothing, and it is the exact structural cousin of ZIRP-era money creation the operator is describing.
Current scale is modest and should be stated as such: the UK PRA's pre-April-2025 supervisory engagement with large internationally active banks associated SRT financing with roughly 10% of the outstanding global SRT market; BIS estimates total leverage outstanding in the SRT market at around €10bn (haircuts 26–55% per BCBS, 40–60% per BIS); BCBS says supervisors are not aware of banks financing their own-originated SRTs, with one possible exception reported in a Moody's survey. Osberghaus/Schepens find investor leverage "modest" but show total bank credit to protection providers rises in the months before the SRT investment, and that thicker sold tranches correlate with more leveraged investors — which has a direct implication for the US, where the Collins Amendment forces banks to sell thicker tranches for equivalent relief.

VERDICT. Nothing in this channel prints money. But SRT manufactures the scarce input — regulatory capital — that governs how much money the banking system can print, and it does so by importing risk-bearing capacity from NBFIs. The correct framing for the operator is a capital multiplier, not a money multiplier. It shows up in credit and deposit growth with the causal step erased.

## Size

Every figure below is labelled STOCK or FLOW and carries its vintage and measurement basis. Do not add across bases.

=== SRT / SYNTHETIC SECURITISATION ===
STOCK, protected loan pool, end-2024: almost €800bn (BIS Quarterly Review, 16 Mar 2026, estimated from Pillar 3 disclosures of 44 banks in Canada, Europe, Japan, UK). Cross-check on a different basis: over €700bn (IACPM 2025 Global SRT Bank Survey, derived from issuance and an assumed 3–5 year average duration).
STOCK, protected assets, Canada + euro area + US + UK: ~EUR 750bn = 1.1% of total bank assets, jurisdictional range 0.9–1.8% (BCBS d607, 18 Feb 2026). This is a partly overlapping estimate of the same thing on a supervisory basis — pick one, do not average.
FLOW, tranche issuance: <€5bn (2016) → €21bn (2024) (IACPM via BIS).
FLOW, underlying pool at inception: €55bn (2016) → €260bn (2024) (IACPM via BIS). Note the ratio: the protected tranche is ~8% of the referenced pool. Journalism and some sell-side notes quote the pool as though it were the transaction size.
STOCK, EU synthetic securitisation (securitised exposures), end-2023: ~€300bn, ~50% of the world market; US ~€120bn, ~20% (ESRB, May 2025, from COREP C14.00 supplied by EBA).
STOCK, synthetic STS subset: zero (Q1 2021) → €145bn (Q2 2024), 40% of EU synthetic outstanding; non-STS synthetic fell over the same window to about €200bn (ESRB, May 2025). Average synthetic STS deal size >€2bn; 98 transactions notified to ESMA 2021–Q2 2024.
STOCK, euro-area synthetically transferred CORPORATE loans: ~€60bn (Q4 2018) → >€300bn (Q3 2024) (Osberghaus/Schepens from AnaCredit). By country at June 2024: Germany €120bn, Italy €75bn, Spain €25bn, France €15bn, Portugal €15bn, Netherlands €6bn, Austria €4bn. As a share of national corporate credit: Portugal 8%, Italy 7%, Germany 5%, Spain 3%, others ≤1%.
FLOW, European "SRT" on AFME's basis (synthetic AND true-sale designated for significant risk transfer): €34.4bn in Q1 2026, −68.3% QoQ from €108.6bn in Q4 2025, +36.0% YoY from €25.3bn in Q1 2025 (AFME European Securitisation Data Report Q1 2026). THIS IS A THIRD, INCOMPATIBLE BASIS. AFME's Q4 2025 €108.6bn cannot be compared with IACPM's €21bn of 2024 tranches nor with ESRB's €300bn stock.
STOCK, SRT leverage: ~€10bn total outstanding, BIS estimate built from Seer Capital data on the assumption that half of asset-manager/credit-fund/hedge-fund investors lever and borrow ~50% of purchases at 40–60% haircuts. SRT financing associated with ~10% of the outstanding global SRT market (UK PRA supervisory sample, pre-April-2025 Dear CFO letter). BCBS reports observed haircuts on CLN collateral of 26–55% and financing tenor of 3–12 months against multi-year SRT assets.
Concentration, STOCK: top 10 issuers = 64% of total outstanding; cumulative issuers above 100; ~8 new issuers per year since 2016; EU-domiciled banks ~half of the global total; Japanese banks infrequent issuers (BIS Mar 2026).
Capital relief: ~43bp of CET1 average across 44 SRT-issuing banks, >100bp in a few (BIS). 47bp for the average listed EU/UK/Swiss bank at Q4 2024, ~12% of the corporate book covered (Autonomous Research, June 2025, cited in BCBS d607).
Composition: corporate and SME loans ~80% of 2024 issuance (BIS); ~75% of euro-area SRT volumes reference corporate loans (ESRB); US mix more diverse — corporate loans largest, then capital call facilities, then auto and residential mortgage.
Privacy: 92% of the outstanding amount of EU synthetic securitisation was PRIVATE as at Q2 2024 (ESRB), versus roughly one third for traditional.

=== CLOs ===
STOCK, US, Fed Z.1, vintage 19 Mar 2026, data to 2025:Q4:
 • L.127 line 6, FL673069005 "Other loans and advances" (includes loans backing domestically-issued CLOs): $361.6bn (2025:Q4), from $305.6bn (2023).
 • L.133 line 15, FL263069563 "Of which: loans held by CLOs" (offshore, overwhelmingly Cayman): $647.0bn (2025:Q4), DOWN from $667.8bn (2023).
 • L.133 line 35, LM263163063 "Of which: CLO securities" — US residents' holdings of foreign-issued CLO notes, i.e. the LIABILITY side of the same vehicles: $583.2bn (2025:Q4), down from $633.0bn (2023).
The Z.1 CLO loan stock is flat-to-falling while market reports record issuance. That divergence is itself the finding — see limitations.
FLOW, US CLO issuance: H1 2026 combined BSL + middle-market ~$230bn across 521 deals INCLUDING refinancings and resets (Trepp/KopenTech quarterly snapshots, reported by Dechert, 4 Aug 2026); Q2 2026 $126.5bn/285 deals of which BSL $111.7bn and MM $14.9bn. NEW-ISSUE only through mid-2026: ~$59.9bn BSL + ~$17.3bn MM (PitchBook LCD). Implication: roughly two thirds of headline CLO "issuance" is liability repricing against unchanged collateral — rollover, not new credit.
FLOW, middle-market/private-credit CLOs: record 2025 at ~68 transactions / ~$35.7bn; 30 deals / ~$15.0bn through May 2026, ~21% of total CLO issuance YTD (vendor-derived, law-firm compilation — treat as indicative).
Q2 2026 new-issue was the weakest quarter since Q3 2023 at $30.2bn across 65 US CLOs (PitchBook), on compressed CLO arbitrage. 2026 sell-side forecasts cluster $190–220bn with JPMorgan at $140–150bn and Morgan Stanley at $180bn — the dispersion tells you the forecasts are not data.
STOCK, Europe, all securitisation including CLOs: €1,294.6bn at end-Q1 2026, +6.3% YoY, +0.03% QoQ (AFME). FLOW: €59.6bn issued in Q1 2026, −6.3% QoQ, −3.1% YoY; €43.3bn placed (72.8% placement rate, up sharply from 59.4% in Q4 2025); €24.9bn STS (41.8%).

=== EURO-AREA TRUE-SALE VEHICLES (verified live from the ECB API, 2026-Q2) ===
STOCK, ECB FVC statistics, dataflow ECB:FVC:
 • Total FVC assets: €2,733.7bn. Series key FVC.Q.U2.N.F.T00.A.1.A1.0000.ZZ.Z01.E
 • Securitised loans: €1,401.6bn. Key FVC.Q.U2.N.F.A20.A.1.A1.0000.00.Z01.E
 • of which originated by euro-area MFIs: €1,044.1bn (…A1.0000.E1…)
 • of which owed by euro-area NFCs: €455.6bn (…U2.2240.00…); by households: €787.2bn (…U2.2250.00…)
 • Debt securities issued by FVCs: €2,072.7bn (…L40…)
Time path of securitised loans, Q4/Q2: 2019Q4 €1,289bn → 2021Q4 €1,297bn → 2023Q4 €1,324bn → 2025Q4 €1,430bn → 2026Q2 €1,402bn. That is +8.8% over six and a half years — essentially flat in real terms. Total FVC assets over the same window rose from €2,085bn to €2,734bn (+31%), so the growth is in securities-holding vehicles (CLOs) and in issued liabilities, not in loans pulled off bank balance sheets. Scale reference: euro-area MFI total loans €15,303.3bn (BSI, 2026-06).

=== ABS AGGREGATES AND ESOTERIC ===
FLOW, US long-term ABS issuance: $517.4bn in 2025, +33.3% YoY from $388.1bn in 2024 (SIFMA Capital Markets Fact Book 2026 Edition, published Aug 2026; underlying data Bloomberg/Refinitiv). SIFMA's ABS aggregate INCLUDES CDOs/CLOs and EXCLUDES CMBS. Long-term only (≥13 months).
FLOW, SIFMA YTD 2026 through July: $322.9bn, +5.9% YoY (SIFMA ABS statistics page, updated 4 Aug 2026).
FLOW, KBRA 2026 forecast: $385.2bn, ~+5% YoY. KBRA's ABS universe excludes CLOs — this is NOT comparable to SIFMA's $517.4bn. Sector direction: auto, student loan, equipment, aircraft and data-centre up; credit card, solar and whole-business down; consumer loan flat.
STOCK, US ABS outstanding: NOT PUBLISHED by SIFMA since 2021. Last value $1,585.3bn at end-2021; the Fact Book prints "n/a" for 2022–2025. Use Fed Z.1 L.127 line 1 instead: total ABS-issuer financial assets $1,834.5bn (2025:Q4), from $1,514.0bn (2023) — +11.7% YoY in 2025.
STOCK, data-centre securitisation (ABS + CMBS combined): $4bn (2020) → $61bn YTD 2026, ~12% of the esoteric ABS market (up from 3% in 2020) and ~6% of the SASB CMBS market. Barclays projects $180bn by end-2028 (Barclays Research via SFA Research Corner, 23 Jul 2026).
Cumulative through May 2025: $48.69bn across 88 transactions since 2018 — 75 ABS deals (70.8% of volume, average $459.7m) and 13 CMBS deals (29.2%, average $1.09bn) (KBRA, 30 May 2025).
FLOW, data-centre new issuance ~$27bn in 2025; brokerage forecasts ~$50bn for 2026 (sell-side, not primary — flag as such).
Context for the AI capex question: Morgan Stanley estimates ~$2.9tn global data-centre capex through 2028, of which ~$1.4tn from hyperscaler cash flow and ~$1.5tn external, with ABS and CMBS ultimately financing approximately $150bn — roughly 10% of the external gap (via SFA, 23 Jul 2026). Securitisation is not where the AI capex boom is being funded.
STOCK, US agency CRT (a distinct form of synthetic risk transfer, do not merge with bank SRT): Fannie Mae CAS and Freddie Mac STACR have transferred credit risk on roughly $6.7tn of unpaid principal balance since 2013 through end-2023 — that is CUMULATIVE TRANSFERRED UPB, not an outstanding stock and not comparable to anything else here (BCBS d607 Box B). GSEs typically retain a 5% vertical slice of each mezzanine tranche. CRT investors fund from capital allocations, not leverage.

=== GPU / COMPUTE-BACKED ABF ===
No published aggregate series exists. Individual transactions are documented (DataBank's ~$1.1bn hyperscale securitisation, Sept 2025, first data-centre ABS dual-rated by S&P and Moody's), and the structure is established — GPUs true-sold into a bankruptcy-remote SPE with the offtake contracts and receivables assigned in — but there is no publisher aggregating outstanding or issuance for chip-collateralised paper as a distinct sector. Rating agency methodologies now exist (Moody's data-centre securitisation methodology Feb 2025; KBRA Data Center ABS Global Rating Methodology 9 Jan 2026), which is usually the leading indicator that a sector is about to become measurable. It is not measurable yet.

**Growth.** SRT, the fastest-growing and least-measured segment. Issuance has risen fivefold since 2016 on the IACPM basis: protected tranche volume from under €5bn (2016) to €21bn (2024), and the underlying pool at inception from €55bn to €260bn — roughly 21% CAGR over eight years. On the ESRB/COREP basis, global synthetic securitisation issuance went from €55bn (2016) to €207bn (2023), with the EU from €36bn to €102bn and the rest of the world from €7bn to €79bn, so the rest of the world grew far faster off a small base. On the AnaCredit basis, euro-area synthetically transferred corporate loans quintupled from about €60bn (Q4 2018) to over €300bn (Q3 2024). Issuer count in the euro area rose from 20 banks (end-2018) to more than 60 (2024), with the HHI of outstanding amounts falling from 0.17 to 0.07 — broadening, not just deepening. Globally, about eight new banks have issued each year since 2016, cumulative issuers now above 100. Synthetic STS in the EU went from zero at the introduction of the framework in Q1 2021 to €145bn by Q2 2024. Market intelligence cited by BIS suggests continued growth in 2025; the 2025 IACPM survey number was not published as at March 2026. AFME's European SRT measure was €34.4bn in Q1 2026, up 36.0% year on year from €25.3bn but down 68.3% from an exceptional €108.6bn in Q4 2025 — quarterly SRT figures are lumpy and year-on-year is the only defensible comparison.

Data-centre securitisation, the fastest-growing ABS sector. Outstanding from $4bn (2020) to $61bn YTD 2026 on the Barclays basis — roughly a 15-fold rise in six years — taking it from 3% to about 12% of the esoteric ABS market and from negligible to about 6% of SASB CMBS. New issuance ~$27bn in 2025, with sell-side forecasts around $50bn for 2026. Barclays projects $180bn outstanding by end-2028, which would be ~20% of esoteric ABS and ~15% of SASB CMBS. Cumulative deal census through May 2025: $48.69bn across 88 transactions since 2018.

US ABS in aggregate. Issuance $517.4bn in 2025, +33.3% year on year from $388.1bn (SIFMA basis, includes CLOs, excludes CMBS); YTD 2026 through July $322.9bn, +5.9% year on year — a sharp deceleration from the 2025 surge. KBRA forecasts $385.2bn for 2026 on its narrower, CLO-excluding basis, about +5%. Fed Z.1 ABS-issuer total financial assets grew 11.7% in 2025 to $1,834.5bn.

CLOs, decelerating. Q2 2026 new issue of $30.2bn across 65 US CLOs was the weakest quarter since Q3 2023, with new-issue volume down more than 20% on the prior year as tight loan spreads relative to CLO liabilities compressed the equity arbitrage. Middle-market/private-credit CLOs are the exception: a record ~$35.7bn across 68 deals in 2025, and ~$15.0bn across 30 deals through May 2026, taking them to about 21% of total CLO issuance. Note that the official Z.1 CLO collateral stock DECLINED over the same period ($667.8bn end-2023 to $647.0bn 2025:Q4) — measured growth and reported growth are moving in opposite directions, which is itself the most important growth fact in this channel.

Euro-area true-sale securitisation, not growing. FVC securitised loans €1,289bn (2019-Q4) to €1,402bn (2026-Q2), +8.8% over six and a half years, versus +31% for total FVC assets and +32% for FVC debt securities issued. European securitisation outstanding including CLOs was €1,294.6bn at Q1 2026, +6.3% year on year, on €59.6bn of quarterly issuance that was down 3.1% year on year. The placed share, however, jumped from 59.4% (Q4 2025) to 72.8% (Q1 2026) — more genuine risk transfer per euro issued, even as the euro total stagnates.

## Series

| Series | Publisher | Freq | Cost | Gives | Limitations |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Z.1 Financial Accounts, Table L.127 Issuers of Asset-Backed Securities — line 1 FL674090005 (total financial assets), line 6 FL673069005 (other loans and advances, includes domestically-issued CLO collateral), line 13 FL673090543 (funding agreements), line 18 FL673163005 (corporate bonds, net) | Federal Reserve Board | Quarterly | free | The only official US stock series for ABS issuers. 2025:Q4 total financial assets $1,834.5bn; other loans and advances $361.6bn; funding agreements $280.5bn. Vintage 19 Mar 2026. FL codes are stable and pullable via the Fed DDP or FRED (BOGZ1FL673069005Q etc.). | Covers only DOMESTIC (Delaware) ABS/CLO issuers. Roughly two thirds of US CLO collateral sits in Cayman vehicles captured in L.133, not here — an analyst reading only L.127 misses most of the CLO market. The HTML preview page frequently renders blank; use the guide PDF at /apps/FOF/guide/L127.pdf or the DDP/FRED series codes instead. Line 13 funding agreements overlaps the insurance/FABN channel. |
| Z.1 Financial Accounts, Table L.133 Rest of the World — line 15 FL263069563 'Of which: loans held by CLOs' (asset side); line 35 LM263163063 'Of which: CLO securities' (liability side) | Federal Reserve Board | Quarterly | free | Offshore CLO collateral and US-resident holdings of offshore CLO notes. 2025:Q4: loans held by CLOs $647.0bn (from $667.8bn at end-2023); CLO securities $583.2bn (from $633.0bn). Vintage 19 Mar 2026. | Line 15 is built from the TIC BL2 questionnaire (foreign customers' USD liabilities, from 2018:Q2) and line 30/35 from TIC SHC(A) (from 2014:Q4); domestic CLO holdings in L.127 line 6 are estimated from the Shared National Credit database. SNC covers syndicated credits above a size threshold, so DIRECT-LENDING collateral in private-credit CLOs is largely outside the source data. This is almost certainly why the Z.1 CLO stock is flat while private-credit CLO issuance sets records. Lines 15 and 35 are the two sides of the same vehicles — never add them. |
| ECB Financial Vehicle Corporation (FVC) statistics, dataflow ECB:FVC (DSD ECB_FVC1) | European Central Bank | Quarterly | free | The best free, current, primary stock series for euro-area TRUE-SALE securitisation. 12-dimension key: FREQ.REF_AREA.ADJUSTMENT.FVC_REP_SECTOR.FVC_ITEM.MATURITY_ORIG.DATA_TYPE.COUNT_AREA.BS_COUNT_SECTOR.FVC_ORI_SECTOR.CURRENCY_TRANS.BS_SUFFIX. FVC_ITEM codes: T00 total assets, A10 deposits and loan claims, A20 securitised loans, A30 debt securities held, A50 equity, L40 debt securities issued, L20 loans and deposits received. FVC_ORI_SECTOR: 00 all, E1 euro-area MFI, R0 non-euro-area. 2026-Q2: total assets €2,733.7bn, securitised loans €1,401.6bn, of which EA MFI-originated €1,044.1bn, debt securities issued €2,072.7bn. | Covers only vehicles that actually hold the assets. SYNTHETIC SECURITISATION IS STRUCTURALLY ABSENT — the loans never leave the bank, so nothing enters an FVC balance sheet. Guarantee- and CDS-structured SRT and directly-issued bank CLNs are invisible here by construction. Also does not distinguish retained-for-repo securitisations (bank buys its own notes for central bank collateral) from genuinely placed paper; that split requires the MFI BSI securitisation-adjustment series. |
| COREP template C 14.00 (and C 14.01), 'Detailed information on securitisations' | European Banking Authority / national competent authorities / ECB Banking Supervision | Quarterly (semi-annual for some fields) | confidential/regulatory | THE definitive EU SRT dataset. Transaction-level: originator identity, traditional vs synthetic, origination date, underlying exposure type, outstanding securitised amount, amount at origination, exposures in default, LGD, expected loss, unexpected loss, tranche attachment points, and explicit SRT and STS flags. This is what ESRB used for its May 2025 report and what Osberghaus/Schepens used for tranche structure. | Not public at transaction level and not published in aggregate. Access requires an NCA/ECB relationship or an ESCB research data agreement. Osberghaus/Schepens report that collateralisation fields are typically missing and that COREP is difficult to match to AnaCredit. Aggregates reach the public only when a supervisor chooses to publish a report. |
| AnaCredit (Analytical Credit Datasets), ECB credit register — SRT loan flag, SRT issue identifier, amount of risk transferred, investor name | European Central Bank | Monthly | confidential/regulatory | Loan-level euro-area coverage from Sept 2018 of every bank exposure ≥€25,000, with an explicit SRT flag, an issue identifier that pins each loan to a transaction, the amount of risk transferred (= junior tranche in a two-tranche deal), and in most cases the named investor. Because AnaCredit also records bank loans to financial firms, it simultaneously measures bank credit TO the protection providers — the only dataset in the world that closes that loop. | Confidential; access via ESCB research programmes only. Non-financial corporate loans only. Investor field sometimes reports the SSPE rather than the ultimate CLN holder, requiring an SHSS merge. Osberghaus/Schepens had to discard a large share of flagged loans for misreported issue identifiers, ending with €187bn across 351 issues from a >€300bn universe. |
| ECB Securities Holdings Statistics by Sector (SHSS) and Centralised Securities Database (CSDB) | European Central Bank | Quarterly, ISIN-level | confidential/regulatory | Identifies who holds SRT credit-linked notes by investor sector and country when the ESMA notification names only the SSPE. ESRB used it to establish that CLN outstandings under synthetic STS were €8.3bn at Q2 2024 and that non-euro-area entities hold roughly three quarters of the total. | Does not identify individual investors, and gives no sector or country detail for non-euro-area holders — which is precisely where three quarters of the holdings sit. A restricted aggregate subset is public; the useful granularity is not. |
| ESMA synthetic STS notification template and the ESMA public register of STS securitisations | European Securities and Markets Authority | Continuous on notification | free | Deal-level: issue and notification dates, originator and protection seller identity, funded vs unfunded, protection type (credit derivative / financial guarantee / CLN), underlying exposure type, and the ISIN of any CLNs issued. Linkable to COREP via origination date plus originator identity. | STS-labelled deals only — 40% of EU synthetic outstanding at Q2 2024, so it misses the majority. Where an SSPE issues the CLN, the SSPE is named as protection seller rather than the actual risk-taker; ESRB had to substitute CLN holders from SHSS. And note the structural gap: PRIVATE securitisations need not be reported to a securitisation repository at all, only to the NCA, and 92% of EU synthetic securitisation outstanding was private at Q2 2024. |
| IACPM Global SRT Bank Survey (also styled Synthetic Securitisation Market Volume Survey) | International Association of Credit Portfolio Managers | Annual | paid | The only cross-border time series of SRT issuance, running 2016–2024, reported on TWO distinct bases: underlying pool size at inception (€55bn 2016 → €260bn 2024) and protected tranche volume excluding placed senior tranches (<€5bn 2016 → €21bn 2024), split by region and asset class. This is the series BIS, BCBS and ESRB all lean on. | Member survey — self-reported, self-selected, no audit, no revision policy published. Excludes US agency CRT. Third-party reproduction requires IACPM permission. Coverage of non-member issuers is unknown, and the number of issuers has risen above 100, so survey coverage may be deteriorating even as the level rises. |
| BCBS d607, 'Synthetic risk transfers' | Basel Committee on Banking Supervision / BIS | One-off | free | The most authoritative cross-jurisdictional description in existence: ~EUR 750bn protected assets across Canada/euro area/US/UK = 1.1% of total bank assets, range 0.9–1.8%; 12% of the average large-and-medium European bank's corporate book protected; jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction risk-transfer thresholds (EU/UK 50% of mezzanine RWA or 80% for first-loss two-tranche; Canada 60% RWA reduction; US no explicit threshold, in practice first-loss covering the first 12.5% of losses); funded share by jurisdiction; SPV usage falling from two thirds (2019) to 15% (2024); haircuts 26–55%; SRT financing ~10% of the global market per the UK PRA sample. | Point-in-time, no series, no revision. States plainly that there is no data repository and no consistent regulatory reporting, and that data on the scale of SRT financing are not available. Figures are supervisory estimates aggregated from member jurisdictions with unstated methodology. |
| BIS Quarterly Review, 'The rise and risks of synthetic risk transfers' (Babu, Chui, Stephanou) | Bank for International Settlements | One-off feature | free | Protected pool ~€800bn end-2024 from Pillar 3 disclosures of 44 banks; average capital relief 43bp CET1 (>100bp in a few cases); average ~5% of loans protected, >10% for a few non-G-SIBs; top 10 issuers 64% of outstanding; ~80% of 2024 issuance corporate/SME; investor mix with supranationals ~10% and insurers <10%; UK top-10 protection sellers ~60% of volumes; estimated €10bn of SRT leverage. Also gives the euro-area AT1 comparison: €30bn of AT1 issued in 2024, 2.5x the protected SRT tranche volume. | Explicitly flags that monitoring is challenged by the scarcity of public information. The €800bn Pillar 3 estimate and IACPM's €700bn+ duration-derived estimate measure the same thing differently and should not be averaged. |
| ESRB, 'Unveiling the impact of STS on-balance-sheet securitisation on EU financial stability' | European Systemic Risk Board | One-off | free | The most granular public EU numbers: global synthetic issuance €55bn (2016) → €207bn (2023), EU €36bn → €102bn, rest of world €7bn → €79bn; EU outstanding ~€300bn at end-2023 (~50% of world), US ~€120bn (~20%); synthetic STS 0 → €145bn Q1 2021–Q2 2024, average deal >€2bn, 98 transactions; 87% of EU synthetic outstanding funded; 92% private; CLN outstanding under synthetic STS €8.3bn with ~75% held outside the euro area; virtually all synthetic deals achieve SRT versus ~one quarter of traditional. Also documents the full data-linkage method (COREP C14.00 + ESMA notification + CSDB + SHSS + IACPM + AFME). | STS-focused; the non-STS synthetic segment is covered only as a residual. Data stop at Q2 2024. Excludes ABCP. |
| Osberghaus & Schepens, 'Synthetic, but how much risk transfer?' (17 Oct 2025 version) | University of Zurich / Swiss Finance Institute and ECB (BIS conference paper) | One-off | free | The only causal, transaction-level evidence on the mechanism. Euro-area synthetically transferred corporate loans €60bn (Q4 2018) → >€300bn (Q3 2024), by country and as a share of national corporate credit. Number of SRT banks 20 (2018) → >60 (2024), HHI 0.17 → 0.07. SRT banks are larger (median balance sheet €373bn vs €24bn) and LESS capitalised (median Tier 1 14.9% vs 18.2%). Regression results: SRT intensity raises loan growth and cuts the regulatory leverage ratio while leaving the Tier 1 ratio unchanged — banks redeploy, they do not de-risk. And the capital arithmetic on the circular leg: a loan to an SRT investor binds ~€230k of capital but frees ~€5m, stripping the banking system of ~€4.77m per average debt-financed deal. | Working paper, not peer-reviewed at this version; views not those of the ECB. Non-financial corporate loans only. Heavy sample cleaning (351 issues, €187bn) versus the >€300bn full universe. AnaCredit does not record loan purpose, so the debt-financing result is inferred from pre-investment debt dynamics rather than observed directly. |
