| AFME European Securitisation Data Report (quarterly) | Association for Financial Markets in Europe | Quarterly | free | European issuance by asset class with the placed-versus-retained split (Q1 2026: €59.6bn issued, €43.3bn placed = 72.8%, versus 59.4% in Q4 2025), outstanding including CLOs (€1,294.6bn at Q1 2026, +6.3% YoY), STS share (41.8%), rating actions (88% upgrades), and a headline SRT figure covering synthetic AND true-sale (Q1 2026 €34.4bn; Q4 2025 €108.6bn; Q1 2025 €25.3bn). The placed/retained split is the single most useful free European number: retained deals are central-bank-collateral manufacturing, not risk transfer. | Trade association compilation, not a statutory return. Underlying inputs are CRA rating actions, ESMA/FCA notifications and commercial vendors (SCI, RTRA). Its SRT definition is broader than IACPM's and than the ESRB's and is not reconcilable to either. Quarter-to-quarter SRT swings of ±68% reflect deal lumpiness, not trend. |
| SIFMA US Asset-Backed Securities Statistics and Capital Markets Fact Book (2026 Edition) | SIFMA | Monthly / quarterly / annual | free | US long-term ABS issuance: $517.4bn in 2025 (+33.3%), $388.1bn in 2024; YTD 2026 through July $322.9bn (+5.9% YoY); ADV trading $2,648.4m (+17.8% YoY). Fact Book Table 29 gives the annual series back to 2011. | Three serious ones. (i) SIFMA's ABS aggregate INCLUDES CDOs and CLOs and EXCLUDES CMBS, so it is not comparable with KBRA's $385.2bn 2026 forecast, which excludes CLOs. (ii) Long-term only, ≥13 months. (iii) THE OUTSTANDING SERIES WAS DISCONTINUED AFTER 2021 — the Fact Book prints 'n/a' for ABS and MBS outstanding from 2022 onward; the last published value is $1,585.3bn at end-2021. Underlying data are Bloomberg/Refinitiv, so SIFMA is a compiler, not the source. The detailed XLS is now behind a HubSpot email-capture form rather than a direct download. |
| FR Y-9C Schedule HC-L item 7 (credit derivatives), column B 'Purchased Protection'; and Schedule HC-R Part II items 9 and 10 (on- and off-balance-sheet securitisation exposures) | Federal Reserve Board / FFIEC (mirrored in Call Report RC-L and RC-R for banks) | Quarterly | free | The only entity-level US regulatory route to a synthetic-risk-transfer proxy. HC-L 7(a)(1)–(4) reports credit derivative notional by instrument, split sold versus purchased protection; 7(c) splits notional by regulatory capital treatment; 7(d) by remaining maturity. Reported by holding companies with $5bn+ total assets. HC-R Part II item 10 captures off-balance-sheet securitisation exposures with the SSFA / Gross-Up / 1,250% treatment columns. Latest instructions revision March 2026. | Fundamental: HC-L 7 mixes trading-book CDS with capital-relief SRT and cannot separate them. Worse, a DIRECTLY ISSUED CLN is not a derivative and never appears in HC-L at all — and BCBS reports direct issuance is displacing SPV structures. So the proxy misses a growing share of exactly the thing you are trying to count. |
| FR Y-9C Schedule HC-S / Call Report Schedule RC-S, 'Servicing, Securitization, and Asset Sale Activities' | Federal Reserve Board / FFIEC | Quarterly | free | US bank true-sale securitisation outstandings by collateral type: column A 1-4 family, B home equity lines, C credit card, D auto, E other consumer, F C&I, G all other loans/leases/assets. Item 1 is outstanding principal of assets sold and securitised with servicing retained or with recourse; items 2 and 9 and Memo 3(a) capture retained credit enhancements; item 6 seller's interests. Filed by holding companies with $5bn+ total assets, fully consolidated. | THE DECISIVE LIMITATION, verified in the March 2026 instructions: 'A holding company should report information in Schedule HC-S, items 1 through 6, only for those securitizations for which the transferred assets qualify for sale accounting or are otherwise not carried as assets on the holding company's consolidated balance sheet.' SRT never qualifies for sale accounting — the loans stay on balance sheet by design. US bank synthetic risk transfer is therefore STRUCTURALLY INVISIBLE in the primary US securitisation return. This is not an oversight in the data; it is a definitional exclusion. BCBS d607 confirms the policy position: in the United States and Canada there are no specific regulatory reporting requirements applicable to SRTs. |
| EBA Pillar 3 Data Hub (successor to the EU-wide Transparency Exercise) | European Banking Authority | Semi-annual/quarterly by institution | free | Centralised machine-readable Pillar 3 disclosures for EU institutions, including the securitisation templates (EU SEC1 banking book exposures, SEC3 originator/sponsor exposures with capital requirements, SEC4 investor exposures, SEC5 exposures in default and specific credit risk adjustments). | The EU-wide Transparency Exercise it replaces was DISCONTINUED from June 2025 and will not be updated — the last exercise covered 119 banks at reference dates 30 Sep 2024 to 30 Jun 2025 and did not carry a securitisation template in any case. Pillar 3 securitisation templates disclose aggregate securitisation RWA, not SRT specifically; BCBS notes that in Europe only aggregate securitisation-position RWA is mandatory and further detail (asset type, transaction count, capital saving) is voluntary. Assembling an SRT panel from Pillar 3 requires hand-reading bank reports — exactly what BIS did for 44 banks. |
| SFA Research Corner and Structured Finance Association research | Structured Finance Association | Ad hoc | free | The 23 Jul 2026 note gives the cleanest public framing of where data-centre securitisation sits in the AI capex stack, with Barclays-sourced numbers: outstanding $4bn (2020) → $61bn YTD 2026, ~12% of esoteric ABS, ~6% of SASB CMBS, projected $180bn by end-2028; average deal sizes $600m ABS / $1.2bn CMBS; and the Morgan Stanley $2.9tn / $1.5tn external / ~$150bn securitisable framing. | SFA is a trade association republishing sell-side research (Barclays, Morgan Stanley, Academy Securities). The underlying Barclays series is not independently obtainable. Treat the levels as indicative and the trend as sound. |
| KBRA, Moody's, Fitch and S&P sector outlooks and rating methodologies (data-centre ABS, CLO, esoteric ABS) | Rating agencies | Annual outlooks plus ad hoc | paid | Press releases and many sector notes are free; full reports are subscription. KBRA 2026 US ABS outlook: $385.2bn forecast, ~+5% YoY, with directional sector calls (auto, student loan, equipment, aircraft, data centre up; credit card, solar, WBS down; consumer loan flat). KBRA 30 May 2025 data-centre note: $48.69bn across 88 deals since 2018, 75 ABS (70.8%, avg $459.7m) versus 13 CMBS (29.2%, avg $1.09bn), plus first European data-centre ABS on UK and German collateral in 2024–25. Methodologies: Moody's data-centre securitisation Feb 2025; KBRA Data Center ABS Global Rating Methodology 9 Jan 2026. | Each agency's ABS universe is defined differently and none matches SIFMA's. Coverage is limited to rated deals, which excludes most private and unrated structures — a growing share in esoteric and ABF. Rating agency 'issuance' counts rated new-issue only and excludes refi/reset in some series and includes it in others; check the footnote every time. |
| Trepp, KopenTech, Intex, PitchBook LCD, LSTA — commercial CLO and structured-credit data | Commercial vendors | Daily to quarterly | paid | The only deal-level CLO tape: collateral-level holdings, OC/IC test results, manager identity, tranche pricing, and — critically — the ability to separate NEW ISSUE from REFI and RESET. Intex provides the cashflow models. LSTA covers the loan-side leveraged loan index and the SNC-adjacent universe. | Expensive, licence-restricted, and not redistributable, which is why every public source quoting CLO volumes is quoting a vendor. Coverage of private-credit CLOs is thinner than for BSL because collateral is not publicly rated or priced. Different vendors define 'issuance' differently, which is how the same half-year can be reported as ~$77bn (new issue) or ~$230bn (all pricings). |

## Build recipe

Build four separate series. Do not build one. The construction below is ordered by tractability.

=== SERIES A: EURO-AREA TRUE-SALE SECURITISATION STOCK (free, current, 20 minutes) ===
This is the baseline against which the synthetic story is proved.
A1. Pull the ECB FVC dataflow over SDMX. No key, no auth. Base: https://data-api.ecb.europa.eu/service/data/ECB,FVC,1.0/{key}?format=csvdata
 The key is 12 dimensions: FREQ.REF_AREA.ADJUSTMENT.FVC_REP_SECTOR.FVC_ITEM.MATURITY_ORIG.DATA_TYPE.COUNT_AREA.BS_COUNT_SECTOR.FVC_ORI_SECTOR.CURRENCY_TRANS.BS_SUFFIX
 Pull these five, all verified live at 2026-Q2:
  Q.U2.N.F.T00.A.1.A1.0000.ZZ.Z01.E → total FVC assets, €2,733.7bn
  Q.U2.N.F.A20.A.1.A1.0000.00.Z01.E → securitised loans, €1,401.6bn
  Q.U2.N.F.A20.A.1.A1.0000.E1.Z01.E → of which originated by euro-area MFIs, €1,044.1bn
  Q.U2.N.F.A20.A.1.U2.2240.00.Z01.E → of which owed by euro-area NFCs, €455.6bn
  Q.U2.N.F.L40.A.1.A1.0000.ZZ.Z01.E → debt securities issued by FVCs, €2,072.7bn
 Wildcard any dimension by leaving it empty between dots; add ?lastNObservations=N or ?startPeriod=YYYY-Qn.
A2. Deflate by euro-area MFI total loans: dataflow ECB:BSI, key M.U2.N.A.A20.A.1.U2.2000.Z01.E = €15,303.3bn at 2026-06. Securitised-loans-to-MFI-loans is the participation ratio.
A3. Plot A20 from 2019-Q4. You will get €1,289bn → €1,402bn, +8.8% over six and a half years, against total FVC assets +31%. CONCLUSION TO CARRY FORWARD: euro-area true-sale securitisation of loans is not growing. All the growth in FVC balance sheets is in securities-holding vehicles (CLOs) and issued liabilities. Whatever is expanding in Europe is not passing through an FVC — which is the signature of synthetic.
A4. To separate genuine placement from central-bank-collateral manufacturing, take AFME's placed-versus-retained split (Q1 2026: €43.3bn placed of €59.6bn issued = 72.8%, versus 59.4% in Q4 2025). Retained deals are not risk transfer and must not be counted as such.

=== SERIES B: US CLO STOCK AND THE PRIVATE-CREDIT GAP (free, ~1 hour) ===
B1. From the Z.1 (current vintage 19 Mar 2026, data to 2025:Q4) pull three FL codes via FRED or the Fed DDP:
  FL673069005 — L.127 line 6, other loans and advances (domestic/Delaware CLO collateral plus other): $361.6bn
  FL263069563 — L.133 line 15, 'Of which: loans held by CLOs' (offshore/Cayman): $647.0bn
  LM263163063 — L.133 line 35, 'Of which: CLO securities', US-resident holdings of offshore CLO notes: $583.2bn
B2. The official US CLO collateral stock = line 6 + line 15. Do NOT add line 35 — it is the liability side of the same vehicles. Get the line definitions and codes from the guide PDFs at federalreserve.gov/apps/FOF/guide/L127.pdf and /L133.pdf; the HTML preview pages often render blank.
B3. Now run the diagnostic that makes this channel interesting. Line 15 has FALLEN from $667.8bn (end-2023) to $647.0bn (2025:Q4) while middle-market/private-credit CLO issuance set a record. Reconcile: read the Fed's own methodology note (federalreserve.gov FEDS Notes, 20 Sept 2019, 'Collateralized Loan Obligations in the Financial Accounts of the United States'). Domestic CLO holdings are estimated from the SHARED NATIONAL CREDIT database; offshore holdings from the TIC BL2 questionnaire; US holdings of foreign CLO notes from TIC SHC(A). SNC is a syndicated-credit register. Direct-lending loans in private-credit CLOs are largely outside it. Write down the residual — market-reported private-credit CLO AUM minus what Z.1 attributes — and label it explicitly as unmeasured by the official accounts. That residual, not the level, is the finding.
B4. Separate new credit from rollover, which is where most published CLO commentary fails. Vendor tapes (Trepp/KopenTech, PitchBook LCD, Intex) flag each pricing as new issue, refi or reset. H1 2026: total pricings ~$230bn across 521 deals; new issue only ~$59.9bn BSL + ~$17.3bn MM. Roughly two thirds of headline issuance is liability repricing against unchanged collateral. Only the new-issue line is a candidate for credit creation, and even that is a portfolio purchase of already-originated loans.
B5. Cross-check the collateral against the loan side. New-issue CLO volume is bounded by leveraged loan and direct-lending origination; if CLO new issue exceeds it, you are counting secondary purchases as creation.

=== SERIES C: SRT — THE PRIORITY, AND THE HARD ONE ===
There is no single series. Build a triangulated panel from four independent bases and keep them in separate columns forever.

C1. BASIS 1 — SURVEY FLOW (IACPM Global SRT Bank Survey, paid, annual, 2016–2024). Two distinct columns:
  (a) underlying pool size at inception: €55bn (2016) → €260bn (2024)
  (b) protected tranche volume excluding placed senior: <€5bn (2016) → €21bn (2024)
 The ratio (b)/(a) ≈ 8% is your implied average tranche thickness. Track it — thickening means the market is selling more risk per euro of pool, which BCBS says is what US issuers must do under the Collins Amendment.

C2. BASIS 2 — PILLAR 3 BOTTOM-UP STOCK (free, laborious, and the most defensible). Replicate BIS's method. Take the 44-bank sample: EU, UK, Swiss, Canadian, Japanese and US G-SIBs and large regionals. For each, pull the securitisation section of the Pillar 3 report — for EU institutions this is now centralised and machine-readable in the EBA Pillar 3 Data Hub (templates EU SEC1 banking-book exposures, EU SEC3 originator/sponsor exposures with capital requirements, EU SEC4 investor exposures, EU SEC5 exposures in default). For each bank record: total securitisation exposures as originator, synthetic share where disclosed, retained senior tranche notional, RWA before and after. Aggregate. BIS gets almost €800bn of protected loan pool at end-2024 this way. BE WARNED: BCBS states that in Europe only aggregate securitisation-position RWA is mandatory, and asset type, transaction count and capital saving are voluntary. Expect to hand-read PDFs for a large minority of the sample. Do NOT use the EU-wide Transparency Exercise — discontinued from June 2025 and it never carried a securitisation template.

C3. BASIS 3 — CAPITAL RELIEF (the number that actually matters for the operator's question). For each bank in the C2 panel compute:
  RWA_relief = RWA_pre_securitisation − RWA_retained_senior − RWA_retained_junior
  CET1_bp_relief = RWA_relief × capital_requirement_ratio / CET1_capital × 10,000
 Benchmarks to validate against: BIS gets ~43bp average across 44 banks, >100bp in a few; Autonomous Research gets 47bp for the average listed EU/UK/Swiss bank at Q4 2024, assuming a 50% pre-SRT risk weight and 85% exposure retention. State your risk-weight assumption explicitly; the answer is highly sensitive to it.
 Then convert relief into implied lending capacity: freed_CET1 / target_CET1_ratio × (1 / average_risk_weight_on_new_lending) = the balance sheet the freed capital supports. For the euro area, sanity-check against Osberghaus/Schepens: a €525m SRT is associated with ~€570m of additional assets, i.e. roughly 1.1x the transferred amount. THIS IS THE ONLY NUMBER IN THIS CHANNEL THAT SPEAKS DIRECTLY TO CREDIT CREATION. Everything else is descriptive.

C4. BASIS 4 — SUPERVISORY STOCK, EU. If you or a counterparty can obtain it: COREP template C 14.00, which carries transaction-level originator identity, traditional-versus-synthetic flag, origination date, underlying exposure type, outstanding securitised amount and amount at origination, exposures in default, LGD, EL, UL, tranche attachment points, and explicit SRT and STS flags. Filter synthetic = true and SRT = true and sum outstanding securitised exposures. That IS the answer for the EU; it is confidential. ESRB's published aggregate on this basis: ~€300bn EU outstanding at end-2023. Merge with the ESMA synthetic STS notification template (free, deal-level, STS only) on origination date plus originator LEI to recover protection seller, funded/unfunded and protection type; substitute CLN holders from SHSS wherever the SSPE is reported as protection seller.

C5. UNITED STATES — accept that the direct route does not exist and construct a bounded proxy.
 (i) FR Y-9C Schedule HC-L item 7, column B 'Purchased Protection', notional by instrument (7.a.1–4), by regulatory capital treatment (7.c) and by remaining maturity (7.d), filed by holding companies with $5bn+ total assets, bulk-downloadable free from the FFIEC CDR. This is an UPPER bound contaminated by trading-book CDS.
 (ii) Subtract trading-book CDS by netting against column A 'Sold Protection' at the same maturity buckets — a dealer running a matched book shows roughly symmetric sold and purchased notional; capital-relief SRT is one-directional. The residual asymmetry in the banking-book maturity buckets is your SRT proxy.
 (iii) Add directly-issued CLNs, which are NOT derivatives and never appear in HC-L. Identify them from the bank's own debt issuance disclosures and Pillar 3 securitisation tables, and cross-reference Reg Q reservation-of-authority approvals — the Federal Reserve's September 2023 Reg Q FAQ is what catalysed the US market and the Fed limits the capital relief achievable via approved directly-issued CLNs for bank holding companies.
 (iv) DO NOT use Schedule HC-S / RC-S. The March 2026 FR Y-9C instructions confine items 1–6 to securitisations where the transferred assets qualify for sale accounting or are otherwise off balance sheet. SRT never qualifies. US bank SRT is definitionally absent from that schedule.
 (v) Keep US agency CRT (Fannie CAS, Freddie STACR) in a separate column with FHFA's annual CRT reports as the source. Its cumulative $6.7tn of transferred UPB through end-2023 is a cumulative flow, not a stock, and merging it with bank SRT would be the single largest available error in this channel.

C6. THE CIRCULAR LEG — bank financing of protection providers. There is no series. What exists:
 (i) UK PRA: SRT financing associated with ~10% of the outstanding global SRT market, from supervisory engagement with a subset of large internationally active banks ahead of the 9 April 2025 'Significant risk transfer financing: prudential expectations' Dear CFO letter. Read that letter — the PRA states firms adopted an imprudent approach to collateral recognition for regulatory capital in certain financing portfolios.
 (ii) BIS: ~€10bn total leverage outstanding, built from Seer Capital data assuming half of asset-manager/credit/hedge-fund investors lever and borrow ~50% of purchases at 40–60% haircuts. BCBS separately reports observed CLN haircuts of 26–55% and financing tenor 3–12 months.
 (iii) Osberghaus/Schepens: euro-area bank credit to protection providers rises in the months before the SRT investment; thicker sold tranches correlate with more leveraged investors; loans to SRT investors carry a weighted average risk weight of ~29%.
 To size the capital effect apply their arithmetic directly: for each debt-financed deal, capital_destroyed = capital_freed_by_SRT − capital_bound_by_the_financing_loan. Their average case is €5m freed against €230k bound = €4.77m of net capital created out of nothing. Multiply by the financed share (~10% of the market) as a first-order scale. STATE THAT THE MULTIPLIER IS AN ASSUMPTION, NOT A MEASUREMENT.

=== SERIES D: ESOTERIC, DATA-CENTRE AND COMPUTE ABS ===
D1. Headline US ABS issuance: SIFMA Fact Book Table 29 ($517.4bn 2025, $388.1bn 2024) or the monthly ABS statistics page ($322.9bn YTD through July 2026). Record on the sheet that SIFMA's basis INCLUDES CDOs/CLOs, EXCLUDES CMBS, and covers long-term only (≥13 months), and that its ultimate source is Bloomberg/Refinitiv.
D2. Reconcile to KBRA's $385.2bn 2026 forecast by subtracting CLOs from the SIFMA basis. If you skip this step you will report the US ABS market as ~35% larger than the ABS community believes it is.
D3. For OUTSTANDING, do not use SIFMA — the series was discontinued after 2021 (last value $1,585.3bn). Use Z.1 L.127 line 1 (FL674090005), $1,834.5bn at 2025:Q4. Deduct line 13 FL673090543 funding agreements, $280.5bn, before any comparison with another agent's insurance/FABN channel, or you will double-count it.
D4. Data centre: build from KBRA's deal census ($48.69bn / 88 transactions cumulatively since 2018 through May 2025, 75 ABS at $459.7m average and 13 CMBS at $1.09bn average) forward with deal-by-deal collection from KBRA, Moody's, S&P and Fitch new-issue reports. Cross-check the level against the Barclays outstanding series via SFA ($4bn 2020 → $61bn YTD 2026). Keep ABS and SASB CMBS in separate columns — they are different markets with different amortisation profiles (ABS master trusts with anticipated repayment dates versus CMBS balloon maturities).
D5. Then apply the lifecycle test before attributing any of it to capex funding. Ask of each deal: was this a takeout of an existing construction loan, or primary money? SFA's answer for the sector is takeout — construction loans, project finance, private credit and corporate bonds carry the development risk; ABS/CMBS refinance stabilised, leased assets. Frame the resulting number as BANK BALANCE SHEET RECYCLED, not as capex financed. The scale check that settles it: of Morgan Stanley's ~$1.5tn external data-centre financing need through 2028, ABS and CMBS are expected to carry roughly $150bn, about 10%.
D6. GPU/compute ABF: there is no aggregate. Build a deal register by hand from rating agency new-issue reports (Moody's data-centre securitisation methodology Feb 2025; KBRA Data Center ABS Global Rating Methodology 9 Jan 2026) and from issuer disclosure. Record for each deal: SPE name, sponsor, GPU generation, offtake counterparty and tenor, advance rate, and — the field that will determine whether this sector survives — the assumed residual value curve. Publish it as a deal list with an explicit statement that no market aggregate exists. Do not extrapolate a sector total from a deal count.

=== FINAL ASSEMBLY RULE ===
Produce a table with one row per sub-channel and four mandatory columns: measurement basis, stock or flow, vintage, and source class (statutory return / official statistic / survey / vendor / sell-side). Leave the total row EMPTY and say why. There is no defensible sum. A €260bn IACPM pool, a €300bn ESRB COREP stock, a €108.6bn AFME quarterly SRT figure, a $647bn Z.1 CLO loan stock and a $61bn data-centre outstanding are five incompatible measurements over partly overlapping collateral, and three of them describe loans that are already inside published bank credit aggregates.

## Double-counting risk

Ten specific overlaps. Several are large enough to invalidate an aggregate on their own.

1. SRT VERSUS BANK CREDIT AGGREGATES — 100% OVERLAP. This is the biggest trap in the channel. Synthetically transferred loans never leave the bank. They are already counted in euro-area MFI loans (BSI, €15.3tn at 2026-06), in AnaCredit, in US Call Report loan schedules, and in every published credit aggregate. Adding the ~€750–800bn of SRT-protected assets to any measure of credit outstanding double-counts every euro of it. SRT belongs in a capital-relief column, never a credit column.

2. THE THREE INCOMPATIBLE SRT BASES. IACPM protected-tranche volume (€21bn, 2024), IACPM underlying pool at inception (€260bn, 2024), and AFME's SRT metric covering synthetic and true-sale together (€108.6bn in Q4 2025, €34.4bn in Q1 2026) measure three different things over overlapping deals. ESRB's €300bn EU outstanding is a fourth. BIS's €800bn Pillar 3 estimate and IACPM's €700bn duration-derived estimate are two measurements of the SAME stock — averaging them is as wrong as adding them.

3. Z.1 L.133 LINE 15 VERSUS LINE 35. Line 15 ($647.0bn) is the loan collateral held by offshore CLOs; line 35 ($583.2bn) is US residents' holdings of the notes those same vehicles issued. Asset side and liability side of one balance sheet. Line 6 of L.127 and line 15 of L.133 ARE complements and should be added; line 35 must not be added to either.

4. CLO 'ISSUANCE' IS MOSTLY ROLLOVER. H1 2026 total pricings ~$230bn versus new issue ~$77bn. Refinancings and resets reprice liabilities against unchanged collateral and create nothing. Any series that sums pricings across years counts the same collateral repeatedly — a single CLO refinanced twice appears three times.

5. CLO COLLATERAL VERSUS THE PRIVATE-CREDIT CHANNEL. Middle-market and private-credit CLOs securitise loans that a BDC or a direct-lending fund simultaneously reports as assets, and that a bank may have originated on a warehouse or subscription line. The same loan can appear in a private-credit AUM figure, a CLO collateral pool, and a bank credit aggregate. Reconcile with whoever is covering private credit before either of you reports a total.

6. Z.1 L.127 LINE 13 IS FABN. Funding agreements with life insurance companies, $280.5bn at 2025:Q4, up from $190.3bn in 2023, sit inside the ABS-issuer table. That is the insurance/FABN channel. Deduct it from any ABS-issuer total before comparison, or it will be counted twice.

7. DATA-CENTRE ABS OVERLAPS AT LEAST THREE OTHER CHANNELS. It overlaps the AI-linked debt channel by construction. It overlaps CMBS, since roughly 29% of data-centre securitisation volume is SASB CMBS rather than ABS. And because it is a TAKEOUT of construction loans, project finance and private credit, the same asset was already financed — and possibly already counted — at the development stage. Counting both the original construction loan and the ABS refinancing double-counts the asset.

8. SIFMA'S ABS AGGREGATE CONTAINS CLOs. $517.4bn for 2025 includes CDOs and CLOs and excludes CMBS. Adding a separately-sourced CLO issuance number to SIFMA's ABS total double-counts CLOs. KBRA's $385.2bn excludes them. Never mix the two conventions.

9. SRT CLNs AS REPO COLLATERAL. ESRB notes CLNs are frequently used as repo collateral, adding a further layer of interconnectedness and leverage. That repo sits in the repo and collateral-reuse channel. The same CLN is simultaneously an SRT protection instrument and a repo collateral asset; both channels will legitimately count it, and a sum across channels will not.

10. US AGENCY CRT IS NOT BANK SRT. Fannie CAS and Freddie STACR are a distinct programme with a distinct sponsor set, and the headline $6.7tn is CUMULATIVE TRANSFERRED UNPAID PRINCIPAL BALANCE since 2013, not an outstanding stock. IACPM's survey explicitly excludes US agency CRT. Merging the two, or treating $6.7tn as a stock, would be the largest single error available in this channel.

## What cannot be measured

Stated plainly, because in this channel the gaps are larger and more consequential than the published numbers.

1. THERE IS NO GLOBAL SRT DATA REPOSITORY AND NO CONSISTENT REGULATORY REPORTING. BCBS d607 says so in terms, and BIS says monitoring the market is challenged by the scarcity of public information. Every headline figure in circulation — €700bn, €750bn, €800bn — is an estimate built from a member survey, from hand-read Pillar 3 disclosures, or from a supervisory data call, by three institutions using three methods and reaching three answers for the same quantity.

2. US BANK SRT IS DEFINITIONALLY INVISIBLE IN THE PRIMARY US SECURITISATION RETURN. FR Y-9C / Call Report Schedule HC-S/RC-S items 1–6 are confined to securitisations qualifying for sale accounting. SRT never qualifies. BCBS confirms the policy position: in the US and Canada there are no specific regulatory reporting requirements applicable to SRTs. The best available US proxy — HC-L item 7 purchased protection notional — cannot separate banking-book capital relief from trading-book CDS, and misses directly-issued CLNs entirely, which is precisely the structure that is displacing SPVs.

3. 92% OF EU SYNTHETIC SECURITISATION OUTSTANDING IS PRIVATE AND THEREFORE NOT IN ANY SECURITISATION REPOSITORY. Private securitisations report only to the relevant NCA. The one dataset that captures them — COREP C 14.00 — is confidential. The ESRB has formally welcomed the Joint Committee proposal to extend mandatory repository reporting to private securitisations, which tells you it does not exist today.

4. THE SCALE OF BANK FINANCING OF SRT PROTECTION PROVIDERS IS NOT KNOWN. BCBS: "Data on the scale of such financing activities are not available," and supervisors are in the early stages of understanding it. The two public numbers — ~10% of the global market (UK PRA, one supervisory sample) and ~€10bn outstanding leverage (BIS, built on stacked assumptions about what fraction of investors lever and by how much) — are order-of-magnitude indications, not measurements. This is the single most important unmeasured quantity in the channel, because it is the leg that creates capital out of nothing.

5. NO SERIES EXISTS FOR CLO AND ABS WAREHOUSE LINES OUTSTANDING. The bank credit that funds pool accumulation before a deal prices is genuine deposit creation, revolving continuously, and it is nowhere published — not in the Z.1, not in Call Reports as a separable item, not by any trade body. Any claim that securitisation is purely intermediation is technically incomplete without it, and the gap cannot be closed with public data.

6. THE Z.1 UNDERSTATES US CLOs BY AN UNKNOWN AMOUNT AND THE ERROR IS GROWING. L.133 line 15 fell from $667.8bn to $647.0bn between end-2023 and 2025:Q4 while private-credit CLO issuance set records. The Fed estimates domestic CLO collateral from the Shared National Credit database, a syndicated-credit register that does not capture direct lending. The residual — the private-credit CLO stock — is not measured by the official accounts and its size cannot be established from free sources.

7. SIFMA NO LONGER PUBLISHES US ABS OUTSTANDING. Discontinued after 2021 at $1,585.3bn; the 2026 Fact Book prints n/a for 2022 through 2025. The detailed sector XLS is now behind an email-capture form. The EBA EU-wide Transparency Exercise was likewise discontinued from June 2025.

8. THE ULTIMATE HOLDER OF SRT RISK IS OFTEN UNIDENTIFIABLE. Where an SSPE issues the CLN, the ESMA notification names the SSPE, not the risk-taker. SHSS recovers holder sector for euro-area holders but gives no sector or country for non-euro-area holders — and roughly three quarters of synthetic STS CLNs are held outside the euro area. ESRB states outright that the locations of non-euro-area investors are not identifiable from available sources. BCBS notes the same gap for CLNs issued via SPV, which account for a large part of the euro-area market. One third of euro-area SRTs involve foreign investors, predominantly US-based, and beyond that the trail stops.

9. NO AGGREGATE EXISTS FOR GPU AND COMPUTE-COLLATERALISED ABF. Individual transactions are documented and rating methodologies now exist, but no publisher aggregates outstanding or issuance for chip-backed paper as a sector. Anyone quoting a market size for it is extrapolating from a deal count. Nor is there any published series for the residual-value assumptions embedded in these deals, which is the variable that determines whether the sector performs.

10. THE COUNTERFACTUAL IS UNOBSERVABLE. Osberghaus and Schepens establish that banks redeploy freed capital into new lending; they cannot establish what those banks would have lent without SRT. The 'credit created by SRT' figure that the operator's question implicitly asks for does not exist and cannot be constructed from any dataset, public or supervisory. What can be stated is the size of the capital relief and the empirical fact that the capital ratio does not rise. That is the honest limit.

11. EFFICACY IS UNTESTED. BCBS: the risk mitigants have not been tested by large-scale credit losses, and SRT markets are opaque to regulators and participants. The market has grown entirely in an environment of subdued credit losses. There is no observed loss distribution to calibrate against.

---

# Private equity fund-level and structural leverage: NAV loans, subscription/capital-call lines, hybrids, GP-stakes and management-company financing, continuation vehicles, collateralised fund obligations (CFOs) and fund-level preferred equity

**Verdict: `creates_credit_not_money`**

## What it is

Six distinct structures, deliberately conflated in market commentary. Balance-sheet mechanics, entity by entity:

(1) SUBSCRIPTION / CAPITAL-CALL LINE. Borrower: the fund LP itself. Collateral: the *unfunded* capital commitments of the LPs plus the GP's right to issue drawdown notices and enforce against defaulting LPs. Before: LP has a contingent liability (commitment), fund has no asset, bank has nothing. After draw: bank has a loan asset, fund has a deposit and a liability, LP's contingent liability is unchanged but now pledged. The fund buys the portfolio company with the bank's money and calls LP capital later. Critically, this is NOT a 90-day bridge any more: Haynes Boone's own deal data puts average initial facility tenor at 19.28 months in H2 2025 (25.00 months H1 2022, trough 16.61 months H1 2024). A revolving 18–24 month facility financing equity purchases is a persistent stock of bank credit standing in for LP savings.

(2) NAV LOAN. Borrower: usually NOT the fund. ILPA's 2024 guidance is explicit that "one or more SPVs or a master holding company are created below the fund-level, but above the portfolio companies, to incur the indebtedness," and that some GPs treat that SPV as outside the LPA's fund-level leverage limits. Collateral: the equity of the portfolio companies and/or the distribution stream from them — valued at the GP's own reported NAV. Before: fund holds equity marked at model value. After: SPV holds a loan liability, fund receives cash, portfolio unchanged. Nothing was sold; unrealised, Level-3 appreciation has been converted into spendable cash. This is the purest available instance of the operator's mechanism.

(3) HYBRID. Borrowing base blends undrawn commitments and NAV. 39% of Haynes Boone 2026 respondents closed one in 2025.

(4) GP-LEVEL FINANCING (management-fee lines, GP commitment/partner loan programmes, GP-stakes minority sales). Borrower: the management company or the GP entity. Collateral: management fee streams, carried interest, the GP's own LP commitments. 37% of HB respondents closed a management fee line in 2025, 27% a GP financing line, 19% partner loan programmes. Rede explicitly excludes this from its NAV survey scope.

(5) CONTINUATION VEHICLE. Not leverage per se — an asset sale from Fund I to a new CV, funded by secondary buyers' called capital plus, increasingly, acquisition financing at the CV. What it produces for the measurement question is a *transaction price* on an asset that previously had only a mark.

(6) CFO / RATED NOTE FEEDER. A pool of LP interests is contributed to an SPV that issues rated tranches (Dechert, July 2026: Class A typically 60–75% of the stack, rated A- to A; B/C at BBB- to BB; equity residual). Insurers buy the notes at a lower NAIC risk-based capital charge than a direct LP interest carried on Schedule BA. Rated note feeders now appear in 38% of subscription-line borrowing bases (up from 28% in 2024), meaning insurance-sourced capital is itself becoming the collateral for bank fund-finance credit.

## Is it money creation?

Split the answer by lender, not by instrument. The instrument tells you what the collateral is; the lender tells you whether new money is created.

BANK-FUNDED PORTION — YES, MONEY CREATION IN THE STRICT SENSE. When JPMorgan, MUFG, SMBC, Lloyds or Standard Chartered draws down a subscription line or NAV facility, the bank writes a loan on the asset side and a deposit on the liability side. That deposit is spent on a portfolio company acquisition or distributed to LPs. This is the Bank-of-England-Bulletin mechanism, unambiguous. Size is measurable: US Call Report Schedule RC-C Part I Memorandum item 10.c, "Loans to private equity funds," stood at $308.9bn FUNDED at banks with ≥$10bn assets at 2025Q3 (FDIC, Banking Issues in Focus No. 1, February 2026). It shows up in M1/M2 as a deposit — so it is not literally invisible to the monetary aggregates, but it is invisible to anyone reading the aggregates as household saving, because the counterpart asset is a claim on an appraisal.

CRUCIAL QUALIFIER ON THE SUBSCRIPTION LINE: the deposit is extinguished when the LPs fund the capital call. It is a timing device. But at an 18–24 month average tenor and a growing revolving stock, the *aggregate* balance is a permanent standing float of bank money financing equity purchases ahead of the savings that will eventually retire it. That is a real, documentable answer to "who funds the capex before the saving arrives" — and it is precisely the ZIRP-era analogue the operator is reaching for.

NON-BANK LENDER PORTION — NO, PURE INTERMEDIATION. When a private credit fund, an insurance company, a sovereign wealth fund or a secondaries fund provides the NAV loan or the preferred equity, existing savings are transferred. No deposit is created. Rede's 2025 survey and Haynes Boone's 2026 survey both document the lender mix shifting toward exactly these non-bank providers. So the *fastest-growing* part of this channel is the part that does NOT create money.

CFOs AND CONTINUATION VEHICLES — NOT MONEY CREATION. A CFO retranches an existing claim; an insurer pays with existing assets. A CV is a change of ownership at a price. Neither adds purchasing power.

WHAT THIS CHANNEL ACTUALLY DOES THAT IS DISTINCTIVE, AND WHERE THE OPERATOR'S INSTINCT IS RIGHT WITHOUT BEING MONETARY: it monetises unrealised, model-marked appreciation without a sale and without a market price. A GP whose portfolio is carried at 2.5x cost borrows 15–20% of that appraised NAV and distributes it. The LP books DPI, records a realisation, and recycles the cash into new commitments. Aggregate savings did not rise; nothing was sold. The purchasing power delivered is real and spendable; the collateral standing behind it is an appraisal produced by the borrower's own affiliate. Haynes Boone's 2026 survey is the damning number: only 10% of respondents saw an agent or lender appraisal on any NAV facility in 2025, down from 22% in 2024. In roughly nine cases out of ten the loan-to-value ratio is computed on the GP's own mark. Add that 85.5% of all private equity fund assets reported on Form PF Question 14 are Level 3 (2025Q3: $5,448bn of $6,373bn categorised; 87.9% for Section 4 large-adviser PE funds) and you have leverage extended against a number with no observable input.

So: creates credit, not (mostly) money; the credit is created against an appraisal rather than a price; and the subset extended by deposit-taking banks does create money and is separately measurable.

## Size

STOCKS (outstanding, drawn):

• US bank loans to PE funds, FUNDED: $308.9bn at 2025Q3 — 24.0% of $1,288bn of NDFI loans at banks with ≥$10bn total assets. Source: FDIC, "Banking Issues in Focus," Number 1, February 2026, Chart 4, from Call Report Schedule RC-C Pt I M.10.c. Companion buckets: mortgage credit intermediaries $315.8bn, business credit intermediaries $317.3bn, consumer credit $105.0bn, other NDFI $241.4bn. Total NDFI loans all banks $1.32tn. Unfunded commitments to NDFIs in aggregate were $987bn, 42.9% of total NDFI commitments — the PE sub-line equivalent is reported but not broken out in that chart. This series begins December 2024 and was only reported comprehensively from June 2025; treat 2024Q4 and 2025Q1 as best-efforts.

• PE fund borrowings per Form PF: 5.9% of aggregate GAV for all private equity funds at 2025Q3 (GAV $7,958bn) → approximately $470bn. For Section 4 (large-adviser) PE funds: 6.2% of $6,465bn → approximately $401bn. Source: SEC Division of Investment Management Analytics Office, Private Fund Statistics, Third Calendar Quarter 2025, Tables 2.1, 2.2 and 2.9, reported on Form PF Questions 8, 12, 43 and 68. VINTAGE WARNING: all private equity fund advisers file Form PF ANNUALLY (report §11.1.1). The quarterly series is a stale rolling panel — the PE ratio sat flat at 4.8% for four consecutive quarters then jumped to 6.0% in 2024Q4, which is a refresh artefact, not an economic event.

• Fund-level bank loan COMMITMENTS to PE and PC funds combined: approximately $300bn at 2023Q3, versus under $10bn in 2013. Source: Levin & Malfroy-Camine, "Bank Lending to Private Equity and Private Credit Funds: Insights from Regulatory Data," Federal Reserve Bank of Boston Supervisory Research and Analysis Note 25-02 (2025), from FR Y-14Q loan-level data for the 31 banks in the 2024 stress test. That is 14% of those banks' NBFI loan commitments (up from 5%), and 1.8% of PE/PC AUM (up from below 0.2% in 2013). Roughly 2,500 loans across ~1,500 obligors; five sponsors account for ~1,000 loans and about a third of the total (~$100bn).

• NAV loans specifically: NO PRIMARY SERIES EXISTS. Rede Partners — the most cited practitioner survey — publishes no market-size number and states plainly that limited data makes it "difficult to provide a comprehensive picture of the absolute size of the market" (NAV Financing Market Report 2025, June 2025). The "$100–150bn outstanding rising to $600–700bn by 2030" figure circulating in 2026 is a recycled vendor projection with no reproducible construction. Do not use it.

• Total fund finance market: no measurement exists. Haynes Boone's Fund Finance Annual Report 2026 (April 2026, survey of 100+ sponsors/lenders/service providers run Q1 2026) states there is "no global database or aggregator of market size" and reports the *distribution of respondents' guesses*: 5% under $1tn, 27% $1–1.25tn, 35% $1.25–1.5tn, 17% $1.5–1.75tn, 16% over $1.75tn. Subscription lines' share of that: 49% of respondents say 66–80%, 27% say 81–90%. This is an opinion poll, it is a COMMITMENT figure not a drawn figure, and it is not a measurement.

• CFOs: no outstanding stock is published. Dechert (CFO Market Update, 27 July 2026) reports market-participant estimates of $20–25bn ANNUAL ISSUANCE in 2025, versus roughly $4bn in 2022, with some projecting $30bn in 2026. Deal size $350m to over $5bn.

FLOWS:

• Secondary market (a flow, and mostly a change of ownership rather than new credit): FY2025 total $240bn, LP-led $125bn, GP-led $115bn; H1 2026 total $118bn, LP $56bn, GP-led $62bn (first period since 2021 with GP-led in the majority), continuation vehicles 89% of GP-led volume, single-asset CVs 68% of CVs. Source: Jefferies Private Capital Advisory, Global Secondary Market Review, July 2026.

• NAV financing origination per lender: Rede's weighted-average quantum completed per responding lender was €805m in 2024, €455m in 2023, €330m in 2022 (n = 30+ lenders, Q2 2025 survey). Weighted-average deals reviewed per lender 25 in 2024. Only 8% of 2024 deal volume was syndicated, down from 17% in 2023 — lenders are retaining these on balance sheet.

ADVANCE RATES (the number the operator will care about most): Haynes Boone 2026 survey, average initial NAV facility LTV on 2025 vintage deals — under 10%: 8% of deals; 10–25%: 55%; 26–40%: 25%; over 40%: 12%. Rede: conventional PE NAV facilities rarely exceed 25% LTV and rarely fall below 5%.

PRICING: NAV facilities, Rede 2025 — weighted-average target margin 520bp secured, 660bp recourse-light, spreads down ~40bp year on year. Subscription lines, Haynes Boone Q1 2026 — vast majority below 200bp, modal bucket 166–180bp.

COLLATERAL AND MARK EVIDENCE: agent/lender appraisals seen on any NAV facility fell from 22% of respondents (2024) to 10% (2025). Direct pledges of portfolio assets fell from 47% to 33%. NAV facilities in workout or material uncured default: 2% (2025), 4% (2024). Use of NAV proceeds 2025: 74% new/follow-on investment, 13% distributions to investors, 7% portfolio-company debt management, 6% other. Source: Haynes Boone Fund Finance Annual Report 2026, figures 3.3, 3.5, 3.7, 3.8.

**Growth.** Bank supply side, measured: fund-level bank loan commitments to PE and PC funds combined rose from under $10bn in 2013 to about $300bn at 2023Q3 — roughly 40% compound, and from 1% to 14% of large banks' NBFI loan commitments (Boston Fed SRA Note 25-02, FR Y-14Q). Relative to PE/PC AUM, committed bank credit rose from below 0.2% (2013) to 1.8% (2023). Total bank NDFI loans reached $1.32tn at 2025Q3, 10.0% of total bank loans, versus $56bn and under 1% in 2010Q1 (FDIC, February 2026). The PE sub-bucket has only existed since December 2024, so no growth rate can yet be computed from Call Report data itself — one year of comparable data at most, and 2025H1 is contaminated by reclassification out of the "other NDFI" bucket.

Form PF: PE fund borrowings moved from 4.8% to 6.0% of GAV between 2024Q3 and 2024Q4 and have held at 5.9% through 2025Q3. Do not read this as a Q4 event — PE advisers file annually, so this is the FY2024 refresh landing in one quarter.

NAV financing origination: Rede's per-lender weighted-average completed quantum grew 144% from 2022 (€330m) to 2024 (€805m), with deals reviewed per lender up from 21 to 25 over the same window. This is growth in the average responding lender's book, not in the market — the respondent set is not constant and n is only 30+.

Secondaries: total volume $185bn (2024) → $240bn (2025, +48%) → $118bn in H1 2026 (+15% on H1 2025), with GP-led growing 32% year on year in H1 2026 against 0% for LP volume. Jefferies projects roughly $260bn for FY2026.

CFO issuance: roughly $4bn (2022) → $20–25bn (2025) on practitioner estimate, i.e. a five- to six-fold rise in three years, off a tiny base, with a 2023 pause attributed to NAIC uncertainty.

Rated note feeders in subscription-line borrowing bases: 28% of lenders (2024) → 38% (2025).

## Series

| Series | Publisher | Freq | Cost | Gives | Limitations |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Call Report Schedule RC-C Part I, Memorandum item 10.c — Loans to private equity funds (and the matching Schedule RC-L unused-commitment item) | FFIEC / FDIC / Federal Reserve / OCC | Quarterly | free | Funded balances of US bank loans to private equity funds, bank by bank, for institutions with $10bn or more in total assets. The instruction text (FFIEC 031/041, RC-C-17, 12-24) defines the item as: all loans to private equity funds, including capital call commitment and other subscription-based facilities to PE and VC funds or any general partnership fund whose underlying assets are more than 50% equity in private non-listed companies. Item 9.a must equal the sum of Memorandum items 10.a through 10.e for banks over the threshold. Unfunded commitments are reported by subcategory for the same banks. | Starts December 2024; best-efforts reporting permitted through 2025Q1 with comprehensive reporting only required from June 2025, and banks reclassified between subcategories during 2025H1 — so no clean year-on-year comparison exists before 2026Q2. Only banks with >$10bn assets report the subcategory; FFIEC 051 filers do not report it at all. US-chartered entities only: a NAV loan booked in a foreign branch of MUFG or SMBC to a Luxembourg SPV is out of scope. Most seriously, the instructions are internally inconsistent — Memorandum item 10.e ('other loans to NDFIs') also lists 'loans to private capital funds, including private equity and private debt funds', so classification between 10.c and 10.e is at the filer's discretion. Verify the MDRM codes against the Federal Reserve MDRM data dictionary before building a panel; do not assume them. |
| FDIC, Banking Issues in Focus, Number 1: Bank Lending to Nondepository Financial Institutions | FDIC | Ad hoc (first issue February 2026) | free | The published aggregate: $308.9bn of funded loans to private equity funds at 2025Q3, 24.0% of $1,288bn NDFI loans at banks over $10bn. Also the full appendix definitions of the five subcategories and a narrative on the reclassification distortions. | Funded commitments only in Chart 4. Point-in-time; not a maintained time series. |
| FR Y-14Q Schedule H.1 (Corporate Loan Data Schedule) | Federal Reserve Board | Quarterly | confidential/regulatory | Loan-level committed and utilised exposure, obligor name, NAICS, facility type and internal rating for every corporate loan over $1m at CCAR banks. This is the only dataset that can separate a subscription line from a NAV facility at the individual-obligor level, because you can see the collateral and facility type fields. | Not public. The Boston Fed authors had to name-match against a list of known PE/PC sponsors and hand-review over 50,000 loans; they state their $300bn is a lower bound because SPV obligor names do not reveal their sponsor. Covers only the ~31 stress-tested banks, so foreign and regional bank lending is excluded. |
| Levin & Malfroy-Camine, Growth of Bank Lending to Private Equity and Credit Funds, SRA Note 25-02 | Federal Reserve Bank of Boston | One-off (2025) | free | The only public, methodologically documented estimate of fund-level bank lending derived from supervisory data: ~$300bn of commitments at 2023Q3, concentration statistics, and a Covid-era utilisation comparison. It also states the SEC's own Form PF-derived figure of ~$200bn for private credit fund borrowings (Fed FSR May 2023) and explains why the two differ. | PE and PC combined and not separable. Commitments, not drawn. 2023Q3 vintage. Name-matching methodology is not reproducible outside the Fed. |
| SEC Private Fund Statistics — Tables 2.1, 2.3, 2.9, 2.10, 2.11, 2.13, 2.20, 2.21, 2.22, 2.23 and Section 10 | SEC Division of Investment Management, Analytics Office | Quarterly report; private equity fund data filed annually by advisers | free | Table 2.9 gives aggregate borrowings as a percent of GAV by fund type (PE funds 5.9%, Section 4 PE funds 6.2% at 2025Q3). Multiply by Table 2.1/2.2 GAV to get a dollar stock. Table 2.13 gives creditor split (US financial 65.7%, non-US financial 31.8%). Tables 2.20/2.22 give the fair-value hierarchy of PE fund assets — the Level 3 share. Tables 2.11/2.12 give the distribution of funds by borrowing-to-NAV ratio. Section 10 gives controlled portfolio company debt-to-equity distributions (Questions 70–76) and industry concentration (Question 80). | PE advisers file ANNUALLY, so the quarterly series is a stale rolling panel with refresh artefacts (see the 4.8%→6.0% jump at 2024Q4). Only SEC-registered advisers with $150m+ in private fund AUM file at all; exempt reporting advisers and non-US managers are absent. No breakdown of borrowing by facility type — subscription, NAV and hybrid are one number. Region and country exposure tables were REMOVED beginning with the 2024Q2 report under Release No. IA-6279. The 2024 Form PF amendments' compliance date has been pushed repeatedly and now stands at 1 October 2026; an April 2026 SEC/CFTC re-proposal did not streamline Section 4 PE reporting. |
| Rede Partners NAV Financing Market Report (annual) | Rede Partners LLP | Annual (June) | free | Lender-side survey: weighted-average quantum completed per lender (€330m 2022, €455m 2023, €805m 2024), deals reviewed and completed per lender, target margins split secured (520bp) versus recourse-light (660bp), use-of-proceeds direction of travel, syndication share (8% of 2024 volume), LTV conventions (5–25%). | n = 30+ self-selected lenders, not a census; Rede states the results may not be representative. Deliberately publishes NO market-size estimate. Scope excludes GP/management-company facilities, funds of funds and secondaries funds. Perceptions data, not transaction data. Respondent set is not held constant year to year, so the per-lender growth rates conflate market growth with composition change. |
| Haynes Boone Fund Finance Annual Report (annual, third edition 2026) | Haynes and Boone, LLP | Annual (April) | free | The only public source with NAV facility advance-rate distributions (initial LTV buckets), subscription-line spread distributions and tenor time series from actual deal files ('HB Data', distinct from 'Survey Data'), covenant frequencies, borrowing-base construction by facility size, rated-note-feeder penetration, and the appraisal-frequency question that is the single most important datum on the marks (10% of respondents saw a lender appraisal in 2025 versus 22% in 2024). | Survey of 100+ self-selected sponsors, lenders and service providers, Q1 2026, plus one firm's own deal book. The $1.25–1.75tn market-size figure is a distribution of respondent opinions, not a measurement, and the report says so. HB Data is a single law firm's deal flow and is not a market sample. Copyrighted; reproduction restricted. |
| Jefferies Global Secondary Market Review (semi-annual) | Jefferies Private Capital Advisory | Semi-annual (January and July) | free | Figure 2 is the empirical handle on the marks: LP portfolio secondary pricing as a percent of NAV, by strategy, annually 2020 through H1 2026. H1 2026: all strategies 87%, buyout 91%, infrastructure 91%, credit 89%, venture 79%, real estate 68%. Also transaction volume, LP/GP split, CV share, single-asset CV share, structured-solution share, pricing by vintage (recent vintages at single-digit discounts, 10+ year tail-end funds at 25%+ discounts) and by geography (Asia at 30%+ discounts). | 'Based on Jefferies internal data' — one intermediary's deal flow, and Jefferies is a principal beneficiary of higher volumes. Selection bias is severe and one-directional: LPs sell what buyers want to buy, so the observed discount is the discount on the most saleable tail of the distribution, not on the average asset. Pricing is quoted against a reference-date NAV that is typically two to three quarters stale, so 'percent of NAV' embeds an unstated valuation lag. And the headline price is not a cash price — 33% of LP sales in H1 2026 carried structured elements, 26% deferrals, which flatter the headline percentage. Peer reports from Lazard, Evercore, Campbell Lutyens and PJT should be triangulated; they use different denominators. |
| ILPA NAV-Based Facilities Guidance (July 2024) and ILPA Model LPA | Institutional Limited Partners Association | One-off, with the Quarterly Reporting Standards Initiative as the live workstream | free | Defines the reporting fields an LP should be receiving: facility balance and percentage of total uncalled capital, days outstanding of each drawdown, current use of proceeds, net IRR with and without the facility, all facility terms including upfront, drawn and undrawn fees, and total cost to the fund (Model LPA 15.2.3.5 and 15.2.2.8). Also the model definitions of 'NAV-based facility' and 'Borrowing Subsidiary' that determine whether SPV debt counts toward a fund's leverage cap. | Voluntary. Applies to private equity only, not secondaries, credit or real estate. Adoption was 43% of Rede's lender respondents by the 2025 survey. Most decisively, it documents rather than closes the measurement hole: it states that GPs commonly interpret SPV/master-holdco debt as outside fund-level leverage provisions. |
| Dechert CFO Market Update / GLI Pink Book chapter on Collateralised Fund Obligations | Dechert LLP | Annual | free | The only public quantification of CFO issuance: ~$4bn (2022), $20–25bn (2025), $30bn projected 2026; deal size range $350m to $5bn+; capital structure attachment points; liquidity facility sizing at 10–15% of issuance; 5–9 month execution. | Explicitly labelled market-participant estimates and practitioner experience, not data. No outstanding stock. No public deal register: rated CFO tranches are almost all 144A or private placements and many carry private ratings that never appear on a rating agency's public site. |
| NAIC Annual Statement Schedule BA (Other Long-Term Invested Assets) and Schedule D Part 1, with the SVO/Capital Markets Bureau special reports | National Association of Insurance Commissioners | Annual (statutory year-end), with quarterly statements | paid | The insurer end of the CFO and rated-note-feeder chain. Direct LP interests in PE funds sit on Schedule BA; rated CFO/feeder notes sit on Schedule D as bonds. Comparing the growth of the two, at the same insurer group, is the cleanest available measure of the capital-charge arbitrage that is driving CFO issuance. | Statutory filings are paid via NAIC or a vendor. US insurers only — Bermuda entities file with the BMA on a different basis, and a great deal of this activity is precisely offshore. Schedule BA has no PE/PC/RE sub-classification granular enough to isolate buyout fund LP interests cleanly. |
| EDGAR full-text search over N-CSR, N-2, 10-K and 10-Q | SEC | Continuous | free | Audited, statutory disclosure of fund-level facilities in the small but growing registered corner of the market: interval funds, tender-offer funds and registered funds-of-funds that hold PE (Hamilton Lane, StepStone, Partners Group master funds and peers) file N-CSR with audited financials showing credit facility balances, drawn amounts, rates, covenants and the ASC 820 fair-value hierarchy. Listed alternative managers (Blackstone, KKR, Apollo, Carlyle, Ares, Blue Owl, TPG) disclose consolidated fund debt and, for Blue Owl, a GP Strategic Capital segment that is the largest public window on GP-stakes economics. | Tiny slice of the universe. All-time EDGAR hit counts are trivial: 129 for 'subscription credit facility', 34 for 'NAV facility', 11 for 'net asset value facility', 16 for 'preferred equity facility'. Unregistered buyout funds do not file. Consolidated-fund debt in a manager's 10-K is not the same population as its clients' fund-level debt. |
| Fed Z.1 Financial Accounts of the United States | Federal Reserve Board | Quarterly | free | Effectively nothing for this channel. Z.1 has a supplementary balance sheet for DOMESTIC HEDGE FUNDS (S.124.7.b, sourced from Form PF) but NO private equity fund sector table. PE fund borrowings are not identified anywhere in Z.1; they are buried in the residual of other financial business and in the counterparty banks' loan totals. | The absence is the finding. Any analyst told to 'get it from Z.1' should be told it is not there, and that the hedge fund table exists precisely because the Fed chose to build one from Form PF and has not done the same for PE. |
| Association of Investment Companies / listed private equity investment trust discounts | AIC (data prepared by Morningstar); underlying NAVs from the trusts' own RNS announcements | Daily prices, monthly NAVs | free | A free, daily, exchange-observable market price against a published PE NAV, for LSE-listed vehicles (Pantheon International, HarbourVest Global Private Equity, ICG Enterprise Trust, NB Private Equity Partners, Oakley Capital Investments and peers). The discount is the market's continuous vote on the credibility of PE marks, with none of the selection bias of a broker's secondary pricing survey. | Contaminated by fund-specific factors that have nothing to do with the marks: shareholder register, buyback policy, board pressure, UK cost-disclosure regime changes, and the structural discount that has affected the whole UK closed-end sector. Fund-of-funds vehicles carry a double fee layer. Small sample. But it is free, daily, and unmediated by any intermediary with an interest in the answer. |

## Build recipe

Build three separate series. Do not add them. Series A is the money-creating bank subset; Series B is the total fund-level stock; Series C is the mark-credibility discount that tells you what the collateral behind A and B is actually worth.

=== SERIES A: BANK CREDIT TO PE FUNDS (quarterly, US, from 2024Q4) ===

A1. Pull the FFIEC Central Data Repository bulk Call Report extracts (https://cdr.ffiec.gov/public/, "Bulk Data Download", Call Reports — Single Period, one ZIP per quarter). Take the Schedule RC-C Part I file and the Schedule RC-L file.
A2. Resolve the MDRM codes for Schedule RC-C Part I Memorandum items 10.a through 10.e and for the matching Schedule RC-L unused-commitment subcategory items, using the Fed's MDRM data dictionary (https://www.federalreserve.gov/apps/mdrm/). Do NOT guess these codes; they were created for the December 2024 report date and both RCON (domestic) and RCFD (consolidated) variants exist for FFIEC 031 filers. Memorandum item 10.c is "Loans to private equity funds"; the instruction text is at FFIEC 031/041 RC-C-17 (12-24).
A3. Validate every filer with the identity the instructions impose: RC-C Pt I item 9.a must equal the sum of M.10.a through M.10.e, columns A and B respectively, for any bank with ≥$10bn total assets (RCON2170/RCFD2170). Drop or flag filers that fail.
A4. Sum M.10.c across filers by quarter to get funded loans to PE funds. Sum the matching RC-L item to get unfunded commitments. Utilisation = funded ÷ (funded + unfunded). Report all three.
A5. Sanity-check the level against the FDIC's published figure: $308.9bn funded at 2025Q3, 24.0% of $1,288bn NDFI loans at ≥$10bn banks. If your sum is materially off, you have the wrong MDRM code or the wrong filer screen.
A6. Do NOT compute a growth rate before 2026Q2 versus 2025Q2. The FDIC states that best-efforts reporting was permitted through 2025Q1, that comprehensive reporting was only required from 30 June 2025, and that reclassification out of M.10.e into M.10.c continued through the June 2025 filings. Everything before that is a reporting artefact.
A7. Extend backwards, coarsely, using item 9.a (total NDFI loans, available from 2010) and the H.8 release line "Loans to nondepository financial institutions" (FRED series LNFACBW027SBOG) as the shape. Do not impute a PE share backwards; state the series starts in 2024Q4.
A8. For the pre-2024 level and for facility-type detail, cite Boston Fed SRA Note 25-02 as the single documented FR Y-14Q estimate (~$300bn commitments, PE+PC, 2023Q3) and reproduce its caveats verbatim rather than re-deriving it — you cannot, the data are confidential.
A9. For the non-US bank leg, which is where a Japanese mega-bank's own book sits: there is no equivalent. BIS locational banking statistics Table A6 (cross-border claims by sector) separate "non-bank financial institutions" from non-financial corporates from 2013Q4 onward, which lets you bound cross-border bank claims on NBFIs by residence of counterparty — useful for Cayman and Luxembourg, which are where the SPVs are. But BIS does not identify PE funds within NBFI. Say so.

=== SERIES B: TOTAL PE FUND-LEVEL BORROWING STOCK (annual in substance, published quarterly) ===

B1. Download the SEC Private Fund Statistics PDF for the latest quarter from https://www.sec.gov/files/investment/private-funds-statistics-YYYY-qN.pdf. Note the file is served with a 403 to naive fetchers; set a declared user agent per SEC's access policy.
B2. Extract Table 2.1 (aggregate GAV by fund type), Table 2.3 (NAV), Table 2.2/2.4 (large-filer versions) and Table 2.9 (aggregate borrowings as a percent of aggregate GAV).
B3. Compute borrowings = Table 2.9 ratio × Table 2.1 GAV, for both the "Private Equity Fund" and the "Section 4 Private Equity Fund" rows. At 2025Q3: 0.059 × $7,958bn ≈ $470bn and 0.062 × $6,465bn ≈ $401bn.
B4. Date-stamp each observation with the FILING vintage, not the reference quarter. Report §11.1.1 says all private equity fund advisers file annually. Build a second column recording where in the annual cycle each quarter sits, and flag the step changes (2024Q3→2024Q4 is 4.8%→6.0%) as refresh artefacts.
B5. Cross-check the composition against Table 2.13 (creditor type: US financial 65.7%, non-US financial 31.8%, US non-financial 2.2%, non-US non-financial 0.3% at 2025Q3). The 31.8% non-US financial share is your first-order estimate of how much of this sits outside Series A's US Call Report perimeter.
B6. Reconcile B against A explicitly, and expect them not to tie. B ($470bn, all lenders, drawn, global, annual vintage) versus A ($308.9bn, US banks ≥$10bn, funded, quarterly). The gap is roughly the non-US bank leg plus non-bank lenders plus small US banks — but it is also partly definitional, and you must say so rather than presenting the residual as an estimate.
B7. Bound the SPV hole. Read Form PF Question 12's instruction text in the current form (available with the Form PF instructions on sec.gov) and determine whether "borrowings" is defined to include debt of consolidated Borrowing Subsidiaries. ILPA's July 2024 guidance states that GPs commonly treat SPV/master-holdco debt as outside fund-level leverage provisions in the LPA. If Form PF's fund-level question sits on the same boundary, the NAV-loan population is systematically under-reported in B and the size of that gap is unknown. This is the single most important open question in the whole channel and it can be resolved by reading one page of instructions.
B8. Separate subscription from NAV within B: you cannot. Form PF does not distinguish facility type. State this and stop.
B9. Split by facility type using the survey layer instead, and label it as such. Haynes Boone 2026, figure 4.5: 94% of respondents closed subscription lines in 2025, 52% NAV, 39% hybrid, 37% management fee lines, 27% GP financing lines, 19% partner loan programmes, 8% CFOs. These are participation frequencies, not volumes. The only volume-shaped statistic is the respondent distribution on subscription lines' share of the fund finance market: 49% say 66–80%, 27% say 81–90%.
B10. If you insist on a bottom-up NAV origination flow, construct it transparently and publish the assumption: annual origination ≈ (weighted-average quantum completed per lender, from Rede: €805m for 2024) × (assumed count of active NAV lenders). Rede's respondent base is 30+. State the lender count you assume, state that Rede's cohort is self-selected and skewed to the most active, and present a range rather than a point. Then convert flow to stock with an explicit tenor assumption (Haynes Boone figure 3.1: 24% of 2025 facilities at 1–2 years, 55% at 3–4 years, 21% at 5+ years) and an amortisation assumption. Label the whole thing a construction, not a measurement.

=== SERIES C: THE MARK ITSELF ===

C1. LEVEL 3 SHARE. From the same Private Fund Statistics PDF, Tables 2.20 and 2.22. Compute Level 3 ÷ (Level 1 + Level 2 + Level 3 + Cost-Based). At 2025Q3: PE funds $5,448bn ÷ $6,373bn = 85.5%; Section 4 PE funds $4,569bn ÷ $5,199bn = 87.9%. Note that the denominator is well below Table 2.1 GAV ($7,958bn) because funds that do not use a US-GAAP-style fair value hierarchy are not required to answer Question 14 — so the coverage of the Level 3 statistic is itself only ~80% of the population.
C2. SECONDARY PRICE VERSUS NAV. Take Jefferies figure 2 (LP portfolio pricing as % of NAV by strategy, 2020–H1 2026) and the equivalent series from Lazard, Evercore, Campbell Lutyens and PJT. Do not average them; they use different denominators and different deal samples. Present them as a band. Then apply three corrections that the headline number does not: (i) the reference NAV is typically two to three quarters stale, so restate the price against the NAV date, not the trade date; (ii) 33% of H1 2026 LP sales carried structured elements and 26% carried deferrals, which inflate the headline percentage relative to a cash-equivalent price — request or estimate a cash-equivalent discount rate on the deferred consideration; (iii) selection: the observed sample is the assets LPs chose to sell into buyer demand, so it is the *upper* tail of saleability. The 25%+ discount Jefferies reports on 10+ year tail-end funds and the 30%+ on Asia funds are the closest thing you have to the untraded distribution.
C3. LISTED PE DISCOUNT. Pull daily share prices and monthly published NAVs for the LSE-listed PE vehicles (Pantheon International PIN, HarbourVest Global Private Equity HVPE, ICG Enterprise Trust ICGT, NB Private Equity Partners NBPE, Oakley Capital Investments OCI) from the AIC comparison tool and the trusts' own RNS NAV announcements. Compute discount to last published NAV, daily. This is free, continuous, exchange-cleared and has no intermediary's thumb on it. Use it as the high-frequency proxy and the two broker series as the low-frequency level check.
C4. STATISTICAL UNSMOOTHING. Take a quarterly NAV-based PE return index (MSCI Private Capital / Burgiss, Cambridge Associates, State Street Private Equity Index, or Preqin — all paid) and run the Getmansky–Lo–Makarov (2004, Journal of Financial Economics) MA(q) smoothing-profile estimator to recover the implied true-return series, then compare the unsmoothed volatility and beta to the reported ones. The reference results to benchmark against: Boyer, Nadauld, Vorkink & Weisbach, "Private Equity Indices Based on Secondary Market Transactions", NBER Working Paper 25207 (November 2018) — they build indices from actual secondary transaction prices, 2006–2017, and find materially higher volatility and betas and materially lower alphas than the Preqin and Burgiss NAV-based indices. That paper is the direct empirical precedent for exactly what you are trying to do.
C5. THE MARK-MANIPULATION LITERATURE. Read, in this order, and verify each citation before quoting it: Brown, Gredil & Kaplan, "Do private equity funds manipulate reported returns?", Journal of Financial Economics (2019) — inflation of interim marks around fundraising; Barber & Yasuda, "Interim fund performance and fundraising in private equity", Journal of Financial Economics (2017) — the same effect from the fundraising side; Jenkinson, Sousa & Stucke, "How fair are the valuations of private equity funds?" (2013 working paper) — conservatism in reported NAVs relative to eventual distributions; Ang, Chen, Goetzmann & Phalippou, "Estimating private equity returns from limited partner cash flows", Journal of Finance (2018); Gupta & Van Nieuwerburgh, "Valuing private equity investments strip by strip", Journal of Finance (2021); Brown, Ghysels & Gredil on nowcasting NAVs. Note that this literature is not unanimous in direction — some of it finds conservatism, not inflation — and that all of it predates the current NAV-lending regime in which the mark now has a financing consequence it did not previously have.
C6. THE APPRAISAL QUESTION. The decisive datum is Haynes Boone 2026 figure 3.7: 10% of respondents saw an agent or lender appraisal on any NAV facility in 2025, down from 22% in 2024. Combined with figure 3.3's LTV distribution (55% of 2025 deals at 10–25% initial LTV, 12% above 40%) this gives you the whole mechanism in two charts: leverage of up to 40%+ of a number, computed on that number, with independent verification of that number in one case out of ten. Track both charts annually. If the appraisal frequency rises sharply in a future edition, that is the leading indicator of stress, not the LTV.

=== CROSS-CUTTING ===

Report every figure with (i) stock or flow, (ii) drawn or committed, (iii) reference date and publication vintage, (iv) geographic and filer perimeter, (v) whether it is measured, surveyed or estimated. Four of the numbers most often quoted in this market — the fund finance market total, the NAV loan outstanding, the CFO market, and any "PE dry powder plus leverage" aggregate — are none of the above; they are polls or projections. Label them.

## Double-counting risk

Six overlaps that would corrupt any sum.

1. SERIES A IS A SUBSET OF SERIES B, PARTLY. The $308.9bn of US bank funded loans to PE funds (Call Report M.10.c) is largely inside the ~$470bn of PE fund borrowings implied by Form PF — but not wholly, because the two have different perimeters (Form PF covers non-US lenders and non-bank lenders but only SEC-registered advisers; the Call Report covers only US banks ≥$10bn but all their fund borrowers regardless of adviser registration). Adding them would double-count most of the US bank leg and still miss the same holes. Report both, reconcile, never add.

2. THE BOSTON FED $300bn IS PE AND PRIVATE CREDIT COMBINED, AND IT IS COMMITMENTS. Adding it to a drawn-balance figure mixes a limit with a balance; adding it to any PE-only figure imports the private credit fund population, which is a different channel and is almost certainly being covered by a colleague researching private credit. Overlap with any BDC or private credit series is total, not partial.

3. THE $1.25–1.75tn FUND FINANCE FIGURE IS FACILITY COMMITMENTS, NOT OUTSTANDINGS, AND IT SPANS EVERY FUND STRATEGY. It contains subscription lines to private credit funds, real estate funds, infrastructure funds and secondaries funds, not just buyout. It overlaps the private credit channel and the real estate channel wholesale. It also overlaps itself: hybrid facilities are counted by respondents in both the subscription and the NAV column.

4. SECONDARY MARKET VOLUME IS A TRANSACTION FLOW AND IS NOT LEVERAGE AT ALL. The $240bn FY2025 / $118bn H1 2026 figures are the gross value of ownership changes. Adding them to any stock of debt is a category error. Worse, they partly double-count *within themselves*: continuation vehicles are increasingly financed with acquisition debt and NAV facilities at the CV level (Haynes Boone: 23% of respondents saw a subscription line at a continuation vehicle; Jefferies notes financing solutions used alongside or in place of syndicate capital), so CV volume and NAV loan volume overlap on the same assets.

5. THE COLLATERAL STACKS. A single portfolio company can simultaneously carry (a) its own LBO debt, counted in the leveraged loan/private credit channel and in Form PF Section 10 controlled-portfolio-company statistics, (b) a NAV loan at an SPV above it, secured on its equity, (c) a subscription line at the fund that bought it, (d) a CV acquisition facility if it has been rolled, and (e) exposure through a CFO note whose collateral pool includes the LP interest in the fund that owns it. Five claims, one operating cash flow. Summing the notionals produces a number with no economic referent. The only defensible aggregate is a look-through to the operating asset, and no dataset supports that look-through.

6. RATED NOTE FEEDERS DOUBLE-COUNT INTO THE INSURANCE CHANNEL. Rated note feeders now appear in 38% of subscription-line borrowing bases. That means an insurer's rated note, already counted as an insurance-sector asset in NAIC Schedule D and in whatever insurance-channel series a colleague is building, is simultaneously serving as bank borrowing-base collateral in Series A. The same capital appears twice, in two channels, doing two jobs.

Corollary for the operator's aggregate: the only numbers in this channel that can honestly be added to anything are the Call Report bank claims, and even those should be added only to other *bank balance sheet* claims, never to notionals or commitments from surveys.

## What cannot be measured

Be blunt about all of this.

1. THE STOCK OF NAV LOANS OUTSTANDING IS NOT PUBLISHED BY ANYONE. Rede Partners, the most active surveyor of the lender base, declines to publish one and says why. Haynes Boone states there is no global database or aggregator of fund finance market size. No regulator collects it. The $100–150bn-rising-to-$700bn figure that saturates 2026 commentary is a recycled 2021 vendor projection with no reproducible construction behind it and should not appear in any serious document.

2. NO DATA SOURCE ANYWHERE SEPARATES A SUBSCRIPTION LINE FROM A NAV LOAN AT AN AGGREGATE LEVEL. Form PF Question 12 is one number. Call Report Memorandum item 10.c is one number and its instruction text names only "capital call commitment and other subscription-based facilities" while the caption says "all loans to private equity funds" — so whether NAV loans are inside or outside it is filer discretion. Only FR Y-14Q loan-level data could distinguish them, and it is confidential.

3. THE SPV HOLE IS THE CENTRAL UNMEASURED THING. NAV facilities are routinely incurred at a Borrowing Subsidiary or master holdco below the fund and above the portfolio companies. ILPA's 2024 guidance documents that GPs commonly treat that entity as outside the LPA's fund-level leverage limits, and that LPs frequently discover facilities only through financial reporting after the fact. Whether such debt is inside Form PF's fund-level "borrowings" is unresolved in this research. It is not portfolio-company debt either, so it may sit in a gap between the two questions Form PF asks. This is resolvable by reading the current Form PF instruction text and should be the first thing anyone does.

4. THE CALL REPORT INSTRUCTIONS CONTRADICT THEMSELVES. Memorandum item 10.c is "loans to private equity funds"; Memorandum item 10.e ("other loans to NDFIs") separately lists "loans to private capital funds, including private equity and private debt funds." Two items claim the same population. The FDIC itself records that banks were reclassifying between 10.e and the other four subcategories throughout 2025H1. The $308.9bn is therefore a soft number even though it comes from a hard filing.

5. THE US CALL REPORT PERIMETER EXCLUDES MOST OF THE ACTUAL LENDING. Non-US banks lending from non-US branches, banks under $10bn, and the entire non-bank lender population — private credit funds, insurers, sovereign wealth funds, secondaries funds — are absent. Form PF Table 2.13 says 31.8% of private fund borrowing is from non-US financial creditors. And the fastest-growing part of the NAV lender base, per both Rede and Haynes Boone, is precisely the non-bank part.

6. THE COLLATERAL IS NOT A PRICE AND CANNOT BE MADE INTO ONE. 85.5% of PE fund assets reported to the SEC under a fair-value hierarchy are Level 3. Loan-to-value is computed on the borrower's affiliate's own estimate of that Level 3 value. Only 10% of NAV facilities saw a lender appraisal in 2025. There is no independent valuation series to check against, only the secondary market — and the secondary market prices a self-selected saleable subset, against a stale reference NAV, with a quarter of transactions carrying deferred consideration that inflates the headline percentage.

7. CFO OUTSTANDINGS ARE UNKNOWN. Issuance is a practitioner estimate. Most tranches are 144A or privately placed with private ratings that never surface publicly. There is no CFO equivalent of the CLO deal registers.

8. GP-STAKES AND MANAGEMENT-COMPANY FINANCING IS ALMOST ENTIRELY DARK. Rede excludes it by design. Haynes Boone gives participation frequencies (27% of respondents closed a GP financing line, 37% a management fee line) and no volumes. Blue Owl's GP Strategic Capital segment disclosures in its 10-K and a small number of listed GP-stakes vehicles are the only audited windows, and they cover a minority of the market.

9. PREFERRED EQUITY AT THE FUND LEVEL IS NOT SEPARATELY MEASURED ANYWHERE. It appears only as a fraction of a fraction: Jefferies puts preferred equity together with managed funds and CFOs at 7% of LP sales in H1 2026, and structured solutions at 9% of total secondary volume.

10. FED Z.1 DOES NOT COVER THIS. There is a supplementary Z.1 balance sheet for domestic hedge funds built from Form PF; there is no private equity fund sector table. Anyone who says "check Z.1" has not checked Z.1.

11. THE FORM PF DATA LANDSCAPE IS IN FLUX AND MAY GET WORSE, NOT BETTER. Region and country exposure tables were withdrawn from the report from 2024Q2 under Release No. IA-6279. The February 2024 amendments' compliance date has been extended repeatedly and now stands at 1 October 2026. An SEC/CFTC re-proposal of 20 April 2026 is described by counsel as slimming private fund reporting. Assume the series you build today may not exist in the same form in 2028.

12. NOTHING PUBLISHED TIES A NAV LOAN TO ITS USE.
