The best available is a survey of lender perception: Haynes Boone puts 2025 use of proceeds at 74% new/follow-on investment, 13% distributions, 7% portfolio company debt management, 6% other, and Rede reports 63% of lenders seeing a decrease in DPI-motivated facilities in 2024. Neither is a transaction-level accounting, and the incentive of respondents to under-report the distribution motive — which is the one LPs object to — is obvious.

---

# Repo, securities lending and the collateral channel: collateral reuse (rehypothecation), the dealer collateral multiplier, sponsored repo, and securities-lending cash-collateral reinvestment

**Verdict: `creates_credit_not_money`**

## What it is

THE INSTRUMENT. Three legally distinct structures share one balance-sheet mechanic.

(1) REPO under title transfer (GMRA/MRA, and Japanese Gensaki Repo) — legal title to the security passes to the cash lender, who may sell or re-deliver it. (2) SECURITIES LENDING under GMSLA/MSLA — same title transfer, collateralised with cash or non-cash. (3) PLEDGE WITH RIGHT OF REUSE — prime-brokerage margin lending and derivatives IM/VM, where a security-interest pledge carries an express contractual right of rehypothecation (US: Reg T / SEC Rule 15c3-3 caps customer rehypothecation at 140% of the debit balance; UK/EU: no equivalent cap, which is why the London and Dublin books are where reuse concentrates).

MECHANICS BEFORE AND AFTER. Beneficial owner B owns bond X (par 100). B reverse-repos X to dealer D1 at a 2% haircut: B receives 98 cash, D1 receives X plus a forward obligation to redeliver equivalent securities. B retains the price, coupon and credit exposure to X (it stays in B's NAV) and now additionally holds 98 of cash or a cash-equivalent reinvestment. D1 re-delivers X to D2 for 98.5, D2 to a money fund for 99. At the end of the chain: one bond, three simultaneous forward redelivery obligations, three cash movements, and one final holder of the security. The number of live claims on X equals the chain length. What has been created is not deposits — it is *collateral*: the same underlying asset now discharges the collateral requirement of three separate financings.

WHAT IS AND IS NOT CREATED. No deposit is created anywhere in that chain unless a commercial bank is the cash lender (in which case the bank creates a deposit and it is already inside M2/M3, and is another channel's territory). What is created is (i) additional secured borrowing capacity per unit of underlying security, and (ii) on the securities-lending leg specifically, a gross-up of the non-bank sector's short-term claims: B keeps the economic exposure to X *and* holds a reinvested cash asset. This second effect is the closest thing in this channel to near-money creation, and it is measurable and separately sized (see below).

THE ONE STRUCTURAL FACT THAT GOVERNS THE MEASUREMENT. Reuse is not uniform across venues. Collateral a dealer receives in tri-party (including GCF) cannot be rehypothecated out — the collateral is immobilised at BNY. Only DVP (FICC-cleared bilateral) and non-centrally-cleared bilateral (NCCBR) collateral can be re-delivered. OFR Brief 24-07 (14 Nov 2024) shows this directly: in its segment-by-segment rehypothecation matrix, "the GCF and Tri-Party rows are zero" (OFR, 24-07). Any collateral-multiplier estimate that treats tri-party collateral as reusable is wrong by construction.

SPONSORED REPO. FICC's Sponsored Service lets a netting member ("sponsor") face FICC on behalf of a non-member ("sponsored member" — hedge funds on the borrowing side, money funds and GSEs on the lending side). Both legs are novated to FICC and balance-sheet-netted at the sponsor, so a trade that previously sat gross in NCCBR moves into the cleared segment and largely off the sponsor's leverage exposure. This is the single largest structural change to what the statistics see: sponsored activity has gone from a rounding error to roughly one-sixth of primary-dealer Treasury securities-financing legs in four years.

## Is it money creation?

NO for the repo/reuse core. YES, in a strictly gross sense, for securities-lending cash-collateral reinvestment. The distinction is worth being pedantic about because the aggregate story is usually inflated.

ARGUED FROM THE BALANCE SHEET.

(a) Repo between a dealer and a non-bank cash lender is pure intermediation. Money fund's deposit at its custodian bank → dealer's deposit at its bank. Total deposits unchanged. Total M unchanged. The dealer's balance sheet grosses up (asset: securities purchased under agreement to resell; liability: securities sold under agreement to repurchase) but nobody's net worth or spending power moves.

(b) Reuse does not create money either. Each re-delivery of the same security funds a *new cash borrowing*, but the cash borrowed is always somebody's existing balance. Reuse relaxes the *collateral* constraint, not the *cash* constraint. In a system where secured leverage is rationed by the availability of acceptable collateral rather than by the availability of cash — which is exactly the post-2014 configuration, with abundant reserves and scarce balance sheet — relaxing the collateral constraint is what permits leverage to grow. That is credit creation at the level of the borrower's position, not money creation at the level of the aggregate.

(c) The exception, and it is a real one: SECURITIES LENDING AGAINST CASH COLLATERAL. The beneficial owner lends bond X and receives cash of 102–105% of X's value (NAIC: collateral "is generally between 102% and 105% of the fair value of the securities loaned"). For accounting and performance purposes X remains the lender's asset. The lender now shows X *and* the reinvested collateral asset, against a liability to return the cash. The non-bank sector's gross holdings of short-term, cash-like claims have risen by the full amount of the cash collateral, and that increment is invested in exactly the instruments that function as money substitutes: repo, CP, T-bills, bank deposits, ultrashort funds. Globally that pool is ~EUR 1,240bn (ISLA, week ending 26 March 2026). It is a gross-up, not net new purchasing power — net worth is unchanged, and the cash was already someone's money before it was posted — but it is genuinely invisible to M1–M3 in the sense the operator means: it expands the stock of short-term claims financing balance-sheet positions without any deposit being created.

(d) A caution the operator will want, because it cuts against the thesis: THE COLLATERAL MULTIPLIER IS NOT RISING. My reconstruction from FR 2004C (below, full weekly series, 2013–2026) gives annual means of 7.4–8.0 in 2019–2021, 13.6–14.2 in 2022–2023, and 10.1 / 10.6 / 9.1 in 2024 / 2025 / 2026-to-date. Singh's independent measure (Central Banking, 12 Jan 2026) reports velocity "almost constant at around 2.0" for a decade. What has grown is the *stock of collateral* (Treasury supply) and the *reach* of the pipes (sponsored clearing), not the number of times each security is turned. So this channel is a permissive condition for the leverage build, not its proximate source. Do not sell it as the answer to the funding puzzle.

## Size

Every figure below is labelled STOCK (outstanding at a point in time) or FLOW (per period). No figure in this field may be added to another.

=== US REPO, GROSS OUTSTANDING (STOCK, daily average) ===
• $12.6tn — total US repo, daily average exposures, Q3 2025. OFR blog "Sizing the U.S. Repo Market", 4 Dec 2025. Split: FICC centrally cleared $4.4tn; BNY tri-party ex-cleared $3.1tn; NCCBR $5.0tn. 69.4% Treasury-collateralised overall (88.9% in cleared, 61.8% in NCCBR).
• $12.5tn — same measure, H2 2025 daily average. OFR Brief 26-03, 31 Mar 2026. First two quarters for which complete-market data exist.
• Of which AFFILIATE (intragroup) repo: $2.1tn, 16.6% of the market, H2 2025. OFR Brief 26-04, 28 May 2026. Predominantly G-SIBs in NCCBR.
• Of which CROSS-BORDER NCCBR: $1,160.8bn, 23.6% of NCCBR ($4,928.2bn), daily average July 2025–February 2026. OFR blog, 9 Apr 2026.

From OFR U.S. Repo Markets Data Release API:

• Securities in via reverse repo (PDSIRRA-UTSETTOT): 2,744,335

Read directly from the 10-K/20-F filings, verified 21 Aug 2026. Format: collateral received permitted to be sold/repledged → portion actually sold/repledged.

=== NOT PUBLISHED ===
There is no published series for the stock of reused collateral in the United States, at any frequency, on any lag. The 65% reuse figure everyone cites is from a three-day pilot in June 2022.

**Growth.** Rates, each with its period, base and measurement basis stated. Never combine them.

COLLATERAL MULTIPLIER — NOT GROWING. My reconstructed FR 2004C series, annual means of weekly c: 7.57 (2019), 7.38 (2020), 7.95 (2021), 13.56 (2022), 14.21 (2023), 10.11 (2024), 10.63 (2025), 9.08 (2026 to 12 August). Down 36% from the 2023 peak and roughly flat over 2024–2026. Singh's independent measure (Central Banking, 12 January 2026) is "almost constant at around 2.0" for a decade. Both say the same thing: reuse intensity is not the growth story.

DEALER PLEDGED COLLATERAL RECEIVED — GROWING FAST. *(Relabelled 25 Aug, C-060: this is Singh's NUMERATOR. "Source collateral" means his denominator — what nonbanks pledge in — and must not be used for this figure.)* Sum of the collateral-received footnote across JPM, GS, MS, BAC, Citi and WFC: $5,574bn at FY2024 to $6,907bn at FY2025, +23.9% in one year (fiscal year-end stocks, fair value). The reused portion across the five that disclose it: $3,872bn to $4,756bn, +22.8%. Per firm, FY2024 to FY2025: Wells Fargo +62.5% on source and +117.5% on reused, the fastest by a wide margin; Citi +25.9% on source; Goldman +26.3% and +22.7%; Morgan Stanley +27.7% and +24.3%; Bank of America +19% and +13% (on rounded figures); JPMorgan +14.7% and +17.8%. Jefferies +32.0% on source over the year to 30 November 2025. Nomura +7.4% on source and +15.6% on reused over the year to 31 March 2026, in yen.

FICC SPONSORED REPO — THE FASTEST-GROWING THING IN THE CHANNEL. Daily outstanding, calendar-year means, sponsored-member perspective: sponsored repo (hedge funds borrowing) $142.2bn 2022 → $264.4bn 2023 (+86%) → $511.6bn 2024 (+93%) → $911.2bn 2025 (+78%) → $1,081.4bn 2026 to date (+18.7% annualised against 2025). That is 7.6x in four years. Sponsored reverse repo (money funds lending) $191.0bn 2022 → $1,397.5bn 2026 to date, 7.3x. Sponsored venues as a share of primary-dealer Treasury securities-financing legs, from the FR 2004C components: 2.5% (2022) → 4.9% (2023) → 9.2% (2024) → 14.6% (2025) → 15.0% (2026 to date).

TOTAL US REPO — GROWING, WITH A DATA-COVERAGE CAVEAT. OFR sizes the market at $12.6tn daily average exposures in Q3 2025, "an increase of approximately $700 billion over prior estimates" — but that increase is substantially a coverage effect, since the NCCBR collection only reached full population in July 2025. Do not read it as growth. The cleaner growth read is Z.1: broker-dealer repo liabilities (net of ASC 210-20 offsetting) $2,110.0bn at end-2023 → $2,827.8bn at end-2025 → $3,040.7bn at 2026Q1, +44% over nine quarters.

GLOBAL GOVERNMENT-BOND REPO — FSB, one-sided outstanding: approximately +20% between 2022 and end-2024, to ~$16tn, with the jurisdictional shares stable throughout.

EUROPEAN REPO — ICMA survey outstanding: EUR 13,651.1bn at the December 2025 survey date. Growth against prior surveys is partly an artefact of a changing 59-firm panel; treat the level as an estimate and the growth rate as unreliable.

SECURITIES LENDING — ON-LOAN GROWING FASTER THAN CASH COLLATERAL, i.e. the mix is shifting away from cash. S&P Global full-year 2025: average on-loan balances +16% y/y to $3.100tn, average lendable +15% to $44.038tn, utilisation flat at 5.5%; revenues +27% to $14.906bn, of which cash reinvestment revenues +13% to $2.293bn. ISLA weekly collateral balances over the six months to 26 March 2026: non-cash +16.9% (EUR 2,150bn → 2,514bn), cash +5.7% (EUR 1,173bn → 1,240bn). SEC N-PORT, US registered funds ex-MMFs, 31 March cross-sections: securities on loan +29.0% y/y ($115.9bn → $149.5bn), reinvested cash collateral +12.9% ($96.8bn → $109.3bn). NAIC, US insurers' reinvested collateral: +22.9% ($34.1bn at YE2024 → $41.9bn at YE2025), while holding steady at 0.4% of invested assets.

THE SYNTHESIS FOR THE OPERATOR'S PUZZLE. Reuse intensity is flat to falling. The stock of collateral in the system and the reach of the plumbing that distributes it are growing 20–25% a year, and the sponsored channel — which shifts activity into a segment where the collateral is largely immobilised and the public data are thinnest — is growing 80%+ a year and is now a sixth of dealer Treasury financing. If secured funding capacity is expanding, it is because there is more collateral and more counterparties can reach it, not because each security is being turned more times. That is a supply-of-collateral story, not a velocity story, and it points at whoever is covering Treasury issuance and hedge fund basis positions rather than at this channel.

## Series

| Series | Publisher | Freq | Cost | Gives | Limitations |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Primary Dealer Statistics — Financing: Securities Out, Repurchase Agreements, U.S. Treasury securities excl. TIPS (PDSORA-UTSET*) and Securities In, Reverse Repurchase Agreements (PDSIRRA-UTSET*) | Federal Reserve Bank of New York (source form: Board of Governors FR 2004C, "Weekly Report of Dealer Financing and Fails") | Weekly, Wednesday as-of date | free | Amounts of Treasury collateral flowing in (reverse repo) and out (repo) of the 24 primary dealers, disaggregated by settlement venue — Cleared Bilateral General / Specified / SPONSORED; GCF; Tri-party ex-GCF General / SPONSORED General Collateral; Uncleared Bilateral General / Specified — crossed with three maturity buckets (Overnight and Continuing; Term <30d; Term >=30d). Also separate 'Other Financing Activity' tables PDSIOSB (securities borrowed) and PDSOOS (securities lent). This is the only free, high-frequency public dataset from which a collateral multiplier can be built. | (1) The all-maturity totals (…-UTSETTOT) are heavily suppressed with '*' for disclosure reasons from 2022 onward — 33 of 53 weeks suppressed in 2025 — but the venue x maturity COMPONENTS are almost never suppressed, so the total is recoverable (see build recipe). (2) Three vintages with non-overlapping venue schemas: SBN2015 (Jan 2015–4 Jan 2022, no venue split), SBN2022 (5 Jan 2022–2 Jul 2024, venues CBG/CBS/CBSP/GCF/TRI/UBG/UBS), SBN2024 (from 3 Jul 2024, TRI split into TRIG/TRISP). (3) Excludes collateral related to derivatives activity. (4) Excludes the financing books of UK broker-dealer subsidiaries of US bank holding companies — i.e. it systematically misses the London leg where reuse is least constrained. (5) 24 primary dealers only; the sponsored-member and non-dealer sides are invisible. |
| OFR U.S. Repo Markets Data Release (dataset 'repo') | Office of Financial Research, US Treasury | Daily; preliminary series (-P) published 3pm ET; final series (-F) fixed quarterly and not revised thereafter | free | 164 mnemonics across exactly three segments: DVP (FICC Delivery-versus-Payment), GCF, and TRI/TRIV1 (BNY tri-party, TRIV1 excluding Federal Reserve transactions). Three measures per segment: OV (outstanding volume — DVP and GCF only), TV (transaction volume), AR (average rate). Sliced by tenor (Overnight/Open; 2–7d; 8–30d; <=30d; >30d) and, for GCF and tri-party, by collateral class (U.S. Treasury; Federal Agency and GSE; Corporate Debt; Other). Mnemonic grammar: REPO-{SEGMENT}_{MEASURE}_{SLICE}-{P\|F}. | NO SPONSORED BREAKOUT and NO NCCBR series. No collateral-reuse field, no haircut field, no counterparty-type field. Tri-party has no OV series, only TV. Rates are explicitly not IOSCO-administered and must not be used as benchmarks. DVP OV counts both novated legs of each FICC trade and therefore double-counts relative to the OFR's own one-sided 'exposure' framing. |
| OFR SFT-2 — Ongoing Data Collection of Non-centrally Cleared Bilateral Repo (NCCBR) | Office of Financial Research, 12 CFR § 1610.11; Final Rule 89 FR 37091 (6 May 2024); OMB 1505–0279 | Daily, transaction level; due 11am ET the following business day | confidential/regulatory | 32 data elements per transaction from Covered Reporters (>=$10bn average daily NCCBR commitments; Category 1 brokers/dealers reporting from 3 Dec 2024, Category 2 other financial companies from 1 Jul 2025). Elements include Cash_Lender_LEI, Cash_Borrower_LEI, Guarantee (Boolean), Trading_Platform, Trade_Timestamp, Start_Date, End_Date, Minimum_Maturity_Date, Start_Leg_Amount, Close_Leg_Amount, Current_Cash_Amount, Rate, Floating_Rate_Benchmark, Spread, Securities_Identifier_Type, Security_Identifier, Securities_Quantity, Securities_Value, Securities_Value_at_Inception, Haircut, Special_Instructions_Notes_or_Comments. | THERE IS NO COLLATERAL REUSE FIELD AND NO SOURCE-OF-COLLATERAL FIELD. The word 'reuse' appears once in the whole instruction document, in the unrelated sense of not reusing a Transaction_ID. Reuse can only be inferred by matching Security_Identifier across a reporter's simultaneous incoming and outgoing legs — which OFR can do and the public cannot, and which cannot in any case be linked across to FICC or tri-party. The collection is confidential; no microdata and no standing aggregate series are published. |
| FICC Sponsored Repo Service Volumes (FICC-SPONSORED_REPO_VOL, FICC-SPONSORED_REVREPO_VOL) | Fixed Income Clearing Corporation, redistributed by OFR Hedge Fund Monitor | Daily observations (1,578 points from 2 Apr 2020) | free | Outstanding sponsored volume from the sponsored member's perspective, combining Sponsored GC (tri-party) and Sponsored DVP. 'Repo' = sponsored member borrows cash (overwhelmingly hedge funds); 'reverse repo' = sponsored member lends cash (overwhelmingly money market funds). This is the cleanest public window on the migration of bilateral activity into FICC. | Two series only — no counterparty detail, no collateral detail, no tenor, no rate. Combines GC and DVP so you cannot separate the tri-party-settled sponsored leg (non-reusable collateral) from the DVP sponsored leg (reusable). Sponsor identity not disclosed, so concentration is unmeasurable from this source. |
| SEC Form 10-K / 20-F collateral footnote — 'collateral received permitted to be sold, delivered or repledged' and 'portion sold, delivered or repledged' | SEC EDGAR (filer disclosures under US GAAP ASC 860-30-50 / IFRS 7.15) | Annual (10-K/20-F); the same two lines also appear quarterly in 10-Qs | free | The numerator of Singh's velocity measure, firm by firm, at fair value: total collateral received across ALL sources (reverse repo, securities borrowed, prime-brokerage margin loans, derivative collateral) that the firm may reuse, plus the portion actually reused. Note locations verified for FY2025: JPM Note 29 'Pledged assets and collateral'; GS Note 11 'Collateralized Agreements and Financings', sub-heading 'Collateral Received and Pledged'; MS Note 6, table 'Fair Value of Collateral Received with Right to Sell or Repledge'; BAC Note 10 'Securities Financing Agreements'; Citi Note 28 'Pledged Assets, Restricted Cash, Collateral, Guarantees and Commitments'; WFC Note 18 'Pledged Assets and Collateral'; Jefferies Note 7 'Collateralized Transactions'; Nomura 20-F Note 5 'Collateralized transactions', sub-heading 'Collateral received by Nomura'. | (1) End-of-period snapshot — window-dressed at year-end and, per Singh, now increasingly blind to intraday tokenised pledges that settle and unwind within the day. (2) Citigroup discloses the source amount but NOT the repledged amount. Bank of America rounds to $0.1tn. (3) Different fiscal year-ends (Jefferies 30 Nov; Nomura 31 Mar) prevent a clean same-date cross-section. (4) Non-US dealers report under IFRS in home currency with non-identical scope. (5) The sum across firms double-counts by construction: collateral GS repledges to MS appears in both firms' 'received' line. That is a feature if you are measuring chain length and a fatal flaw if you are measuring a stock of securities. |
| SEC Form N-PORT structured data sets — SECURITIES_LENDING.tsv | SEC Division of Economic and Risk Analysis | Monthly reports, packaged in quarterly ZIPs (~400–450MB each); coverage October 2019 to June 2026 | free | Position-by-position Form N-PORT Item C.12: IS_CASH_COLLATERAL / CASH_COLLATERAL_AMOUNT (the value of that investment representing REINVESTED CASH COLLATERAL from securities lending), IS_NON_CASH_COLLATERAL / NON_CASH_COLLATERAL_VALUE, IS_LOAN_BY_FUND / LOAN_VALUE. Joins to FUND_REPORTED_HOLDING.tsv on HOLDING_ID and to SUBMISSION.tsv on ACCESSION_NUMBER for REPORT_DATE. Also REPURCHASE_AGREEMENT.tsv / REPURCHASE_COUNTERPARTY.tsv / REPURCHASE_COLLATERAL.tsv with IS_CLEARED, CENTRAL_COUNTER_PARTY, IS_TRIPARTY, REPURCHASE_RATE. | (1) Excludes money market funds (2a-7 funds file N-MFP instead) — a large part of the repo cash-lending side is therefore absent. (2) NON_CASH_COLLATERAL_VALUE is essentially always empty, because non-cash collateral is held by the lending agent and is not a fund asset; the dataset therefore measures the cash-collateral channel only. (3) Only the last business day of each month. (4) US registered funds only — no pensions, no insurers, no sovereign wealth, no UCITS. (5) Month coverage inside each quarterly ZIP is very uneven: in 2026Q2, 6,894 reports carry a 31 Mar 2026 date but only 417 carry 31 Jan 2026, so only calendar-quarter-end cross-sections are usable. |
| Form N-CEN Item C.6 — Securities lending | SEC EDGAR (structured XML) | Annual per fund | free | C.6.c the securities lending agent's name and LEI/RSSD and whether it indemnifies against borrower default; C.6.d the identity and LEI of the CASH COLLATERAL MANAGER of the pooled vehicle in which cash collateral is invested, where different from the lending agent; C.6.e the fee arrangement; C.6.f the monthly average value of portfolio securities on loan over the year; C.6.g net income from securities lending. This is the only free source that maps the agent-lender and cash-collateral-manager concentration. | No dollar amount of cash collateral — only the identity of who manages it. On-loan value is a monthly average over the year, not a point-in-time stock. Annual only. |
| ISLA securities lending market data dashboard, and the biannual ISLA Securities Lending Market Report | International Securities Lending Association (trade body; underlying data from S&P Global, DataLend, FIS and the four tri-party agents JP Morgan, BNY Mellon, Euroclear, Clearstream) | Dashboard refreshed regularly with weekly observations; report twice a year | free | Lendable and on-loan by asset class; on-loan by client type; European tri-party collateral composition and government-bond collateral by issuer domicile; and — the item that matters for this channel — a weekly time series of GLOBAL CASH vs NON-CASH COLLATERAL BALANCES in EUR bn. The cash line is the global securities-lending cash-collateral reinvestment pool. | Not a primary regulatory collection — it is vendor data aggregated by a trade association, with no published methodology for reconciling S&P Global, DataLend and FIS. The chart series are only exposed through the page's embedded chart JSON (a 26-week rolling window), not as a downloadable file, so history must be harvested week by week or bought from the vendors. Says nothing about what the cash is reinvested IN. |
| S&P Global Market Intelligence Securities Finance (formerly IHS Markit Securities Finance) | S&P Global Market Intelligence | Daily underlying; monthly and annual public headlines | paid | Average on-loan balances, average lendable, utilisation, and revenue split by asset class INCLUDING a separately identified 'cash reinvestment revenue' line — which is the only regular public read on the earnings, and therefore the scale, of the cash-collateral reinvestment book. | Vendor panel, not a census; contributor coverage is not disclosed. Revenue is a FLOW and tells you nothing directly about the STOCK of reinvested collateral. Full data requires a licence. |
| NAIC Annual Statement Schedule DL (Parts 1 and 2), 'Securities Lending Collateral Assets', under SSAP No. 103 | National Association of Insurance Commissioners | Annual (statutory year-end), with quarterly interrogatories | confidential/regulatory | A CUSIP-level listing of what each US insurer's securities-lending cash collateral has actually been reinvested in, categorised into bonds, preferred stock and common stock. SSAP 103 splits collateral into on-balance-sheet ('may be sold or repledged') and off-balance-sheet ('may not be sold or repledged'). This is the single most granular public-sector view of cash-collateral reinvestment anywhere in the world. | US insurers only, and small: $41.9bn at YE2025, 0.4% of the industry's $9.6tn invested assets. Annual snapshot. Filing-level Schedule DL data requires an NAIC data licence; only aggregates are free. |
| FINRA SLATE (Securities Lending and Transparency Engine), FINRA Rule 6500 Series under SEC Rule 10c-1a | FINRA / SEC | Will be transaction-level, reported by end of day | free | When it exists, the first comprehensive transaction-level record of US securities lending with public dissemination of loan terms. | DOES NOT EXIST YET AND WILL NOT FOR YEARS. Rule 10c-1a became effective 2 January 2024; the SEC approved FINRA's Rule 6500 Series on 2 January 2025; then on 3 December 2025 the SEC granted temporary exemptive relief extending the reporting date to 28 September 2028 and the public dissemination date to 29 March 2029 (rule filing SR-FINRA-2024-007). Anyone building a US securities lending series before 2029 must use vendor data. |
| ICMA European Repo Market Survey | International Capital Market Association | Semi-annual; single-day snapshot each December and June | free | The baseline size of the European repo market — EUR 13,651.1bn at the December 2025 survey date from 59 participants — with breakdowns by counterparty, currency, collateral, maturity, rate type and electronic/voice/direct. | Self-reported by participating institutions on one day, gross of both legs of their own books, with a changing participant panel that makes the growth rate partly an artefact. No collateral-reuse question. ICMA states plainly it is 'hard to obtain figures on the size of the European repo market' — this survey is an estimate, not a census. |
| FSB, 'Vulnerabilities in Government Bond-backed Repo Markets' (4 February 2026) and its Annex metric set | Financial Stability Board | One-off report; underlying member data collected annually | free | The only cross-jurisdiction sizing built from national regulatory collections rather than surveys: ~$16tn government-bond-backed repo outstanding at end-2024, one-sided to avoid cross-border double counting, with jurisdictional and collateral splits. Critically, its data-gaps annex specifies the internationally agreed reuse metric formulas: collateral reuse rate = collateral reused / collateral received eligible for reuse; collateral reuse ratio = [collateral received eligible for reuse / (collateral received eligible for reuse + own assets)] x collateral posted; collateral reuse share = collateral reused / total assets (or / collateral posted). Use these definitions rather than inventing your own. | Government-bond collateral only. Annual, and already 14 months stale on publication. The reuse metrics are listed as a data GAP — i.e. the FSB is telling members they should collect these, not reporting that they do. No numbers are attached to them. |
| OFR Brief 24-07, 'Repo Market Intermediation' (14 November 2024) | Office of Financial Research | One-off | free | The only CUSIP-matched, cross-segment measurement of US collateral reuse in existence. Nine dealers, three dates (15, 22, 30 June 2022), all four segments combined. Repo outstanding $1,355bn against reverse repo $927bn; dealers reuse $607bn — 65% — of collateral received through reverse repo; conversely 44% of outstanding repo collateral originates in a reverse repo. Treasuries: 69% of reverse-repo Treasury collateral rehypothecated, 58% of repo Treasury collateral sourced from a reverse repo. Non-Treasuries: 45% and 14%. GCF and tri-party reuse rows are zero. Sample coverage: 17% of tri-party, 12% of DVP, 21% of GCF, ~40% of NCCBR. | Three days, four years ago, nine voluntary participants, from a PILOT collection that no longer exists in that form. Prorating assumption required to allocate collateral across segments. Not a series and has never been updated — even though the production NCCBR collection now carries Security_Identifier and OFR could in principle rerun it monthly. |
| Federal Reserve Z.1 Financial Accounts — table F.4.1.s and sector table S.125.s (security brokers and dealers) | Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System | Quarterly | free | Repo by holding and issuing sector, on a consistent national-accounts basis. Key codes: FL662151003 broker-dealer repo liabilities ($3,040.7bn, 2026Q1); FL662051003 broker-dealer repo assets ($2,071.6bn); FL632051000 money market fund repo assets ($2,932.0bn); FL262151005 rest-of-world repo liabilities ($2,205.6bn); totals FL892150005 / FL892050005 and the discrepancy FL902050005. | Reported NET of US GAAP balance-sheet offsetting (ASC 210-20), which is why broker-dealer repo liabilities are $3.0tn here against a $12.5tn gross market at OFR. Do not compare the two without adjusting. The 2026Q1 asset/liability discrepancy of −$1,584.0bn is a standing warning about how incompletely the repo loop closes in the national accounts. Quarterly, and heavily revised. |
| FR 2052a Complex Institution Liquidity Monitoring Report | Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System | Daily for the largest firms | confidential/regulatory | Firm-level secured funding and secured lending by collateral class, counterparty and encumbrance, at daily frequency. This is the dataset from which Infante and Saravay (2020) computed the Federal Reserve's own internal collateral multiplier — the estimate that Michl and Park explicitly set out to approximate from public data. | Confidential. Not obtainable. Its existence is the reason the public FR 2004C exercise is a second-best approximation rather than the real measurement. |

## Build recipe

There are four buildable series here and one that cannot be built. Take them in order.

==============================================================
SERIES 1 — WEEKLY US TREASURY COLLATERAL MULTIPLIER FROM FR 2004C
The Michl–Park (Eastern Economic Journal 49(2), 2023, pp. 156–175; working paper UMass Amherst 2021-12, DOI 10.7275/23485383) method, with the fix that makes it work on current data.
==============================================================

THE FORMULA. Michl and Park adopt Infante et al.'s definition, their equation (1):
    c = (R_T + R_M) / R_T
where R_T is trading-book repo (repo borrowing collateralised by securities the dealer owns outright and has not rehypothecated) and R_M is matched-book repo (repo borrowing collateralised by securities the dealer took in through reverse repo and re-delivered). Their own words: it is "the ratio of the total repo liabilities of the dealer banks to their unrehypothecated repo". Equivalently it is one plus the ratio of matched book to trading book.

THE IDENTIFICATION. Trading-book repo is not reported. Michl and Park recover it as a residual: "We estimated the trading book repo by subtracting the total incoming Treasury collateral of all maturities from the total outgoing Treasury collateral of all maturities. The rest of the total outgoing Treasury bonds is the matched book repo." So with SO = securities out via repo and SI = securities in via reverse repo:
    R_T = SO − SI
    R_M = SI
    c = SO / (SO − SI)

STEP 1 — GET THE CATALOGUE AND THE VINTAGES.
    GET https://markets.newyorkfed.org/api/pd/list/timeseries.json      (1,539 series)
    GET https://markets.newyorkfed.org/api/pd/list/seriesbreaks.json
The vintages are SBP2001, SBP2013, SBN2013, SBN2015 (1 Jan 2015 – 4 Jan 2022), SBN2022 (5 Jan 2022 – 2 Jul 2024), SBN2024 (3 Jul 2024 onward). Data are pulled with
    GET https://markets.newyorkfed.org/api/pd/get/{SERIESBREAK}/timeseries/{KEYID}.json
or, for the spliced unbroken history,
    GET https://markets.newyorkfed.org/api/pd/get/{KEYID}.json
Values are $ millions. Suppressed cells come back as the string "*".

STEP 2 — DO NOT USE THE TOTALS. THIS IS THE WHOLE TRICK.
The obvious series are PDSORA-UTSETTOT ("Total – Repurchase Agreements: U.S. Treasury securities (excluding TIPS)") and PDSIRRA-UTSETTOT. They are clean from April 2013 to end-2021 and then fall apart: on the SO leg, 15 of 52 weeks suppressed in 2022, 16 in 2023, 21 in 2024, 33 of 53 in 2025, 17 of 32 so far in 2026. Naively dropping suppressed weeks leaves you seven usable observations in 2025. The venue-level components, however, are almost never suppressed. Build the total from them.

STEP 3 — ENUMERATE THE COMPONENTS PER VINTAGE. Key grammar is {PREFIX}-{VENUE}UTSET{MATURITY}.
  PREFIX: PDSORA (securities out, repo) and PDSIRRA (securities in, reverse repo).
  MATURITY suffix: "" = Overnight and Continuing; "TAL30" = Term <30 days; "TAG30" = Term >=30 days.
  VENUE, vintage SBN2022 (7 venues, 21 series per leg): CBG (Cleared Bilateral, General), CBS (Cleared Bilateral, Specified), CBSP (Cleared Bilateral, SPONSORED), GCF, TRI (Tri-party excluding GCF), UBG (Uncleared Bilateral, General), UBS (Uncleared Bilateral, Specified).
  VENUE, vintage SBN2024 (8 venues, 24 series per leg): as above but TRI splits into TRIG (Tri-party ex-GCF, General Collateral) and TRISP (Tri-party ex-GCF, SPONSORED General Collateral).
  There is no venue split before 5 January 2022; for 2013–2021 you must use the -UTSETTOT series, which is unsuppressed throughout that period.
  Repeat the whole exercise with UTST in place of UTSET if you want TIPS; keep it separate, do not merge.

STEP 4 — IMPUTE THE SPARSE SUPPRESSED COMPONENT CELLS AND SUM.
Linearly interpolate each component series across its own "*" cells (end-fill with the nearest observation), then sum the 21 or 24 components per leg per week.

STEP 5 — VALIDATE THE IMPUTATION AGAINST THE WEEKS WHERE THE TOTAL IS PUBLISHED. I did this. Over the SBN2024 vintage (111 weeks, 3 Jul 2024 – 12 Aug 2026) the total was published on 49 weeks for the SO leg and 64 for the SI leg. Mean error of the imputed component sum against the published total: +0.044% on SO, +0.005% on SI; worst single week 0.68% on SO, 0.25% on SI. Without imputation the raw component sum is a lower bound and understates by 0.05–0.7% on the 22 weeks that carry a suppressed component.

STEP 6 — SPLICE. Use the published total wherever it exists; use the imputed component sum only where it does not. Then compute R_T = SO − SI and c = SO / R_T.

STEP 7 — SANITY CHECK ON THE RESIDUAL. R_T is roughly 8% of SO. A 0.5% measurement error in either leg moves R_T by about 6% and therefore c by about 6% the other way. This ratio is extremely fragile. Never report a single week; use a 4- or 13-week moving average, and always publish R_T alongside c so a reader can see how thin the denominator is.

WHAT I GOT (full series written to /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-martinschroeder-Downloads/c58870c4-abdd-4920-bbdd-957d69c89922/scratchpad/collateral_multiplier.csv, 698 weekly rows, 3 Apr 2013 – 12 Aug 2026). Annual means of c: 2013 6.58, 2014 8.00, 2015 11.10, 2016 12.67, 2017 10.49, 2018 8.88, 2019 7.57, 2020 7.38, 2021 7.95, 2022 13.56, 2023 14.21, 2024 10.11, 2025 10.63, 2026-to-date 9.08. Week of 12 Aug 2026: SO $3,001bn, SI $2,744bn, R_T $257bn, c = 11.68.

A DISCREPANCY YOU MUST KNOW ABOUT BEFORE YOU CITE THE PAPER. Michl and Park's published text says primary dealers "have been able to create up to five times as many repo liabilities backed by U.S. Treasuries as they owned". Applying their own equation (1) to their own stated data source over their own sample window (January 2015 – May 2021) I get annual means of 7.4 to 12.7, never five. Their Figure 3 caption also describes the multiplier as "the ratio between the dotted line and the solid line", where the dotted line is the non-rehypothecated portion and the solid line the total — that ratio is R_T/SO ≈ 0.08–0.20, the reciprocal of equation (1), not five either. The caption and the formula are mutually inconsistent. Use the formula, replicate it yourself, and do not quote the "five times" figure.

BONUS FROM THE SAME BUILD — THE SPONSORED SHARE. Sum the CBSP and TRISP components (CBSP only before July 2024) and divide by (SO + SI). Sponsored venues as a share of primary-dealer Treasury securities-financing legs: 2.5% in 2022, 4.9% in 2023, 9.2% in 2024, 14.6% in 2025, 15.0% in 2026 to date. On 12 Aug 2026, sponsored securities out $338bn and sponsored securities in $477bn.

A UNIT CAVEAT TO SETTLE BEFORE PUBLISHING. The NY Fed labels these tables "Securities In" and "Securities Out" and the series names read as amounts of collateral by asset class, not cash amounts. Read the FR 2004C instructions to confirm the valuation convention (market value versus contract amount) before attaching a level interpretation. The ratio c is invariant to that choice provided both legs use the same convention; it is NOT invariant to a systematic haircut difference between the repo and reverse-repo legs, which is a real, small, unquantified bias.

TWO EXCLUSIONS THAT MATTER MORE THAN ANY OF THE ABOVE. Michl and Park state them and they are still true: the FR 2004 data exclude collateral related to derivatives activity, and they exclude the financing activities of UK broker-dealer subsidiaries of US bank holding companies. The London books are precisely where reuse is legally least constrained. Whatever c you compute is a lower bound on the group-wide figure.

==============================================================
SERIES 2 — ANNUAL SINGH "SOURCE COLLATERAL" AND VELOCITY FROM THE STATUTORY FOOTNOTE
==============================================================

WHAT SINGH ACTUALLY DOES. From the Annex II methodology of IMF WP/17/113 (April 2017): velocity is the ratio of the total collateral received by the 10–15 core dealer banks (the footnote figure, summed) to the stock of collateral coming from PRIMARY sources — hedge funds via prime brokerage, plus securities lenders acting for pension funds, insurers and official accounts. His worked numbers: $10tn / $3.3tn ≈ 3.0 at end-2007; $5.6tn / ($2.0tn hedge funds + $1.1tn securities lending) = $5.6tn / $3.1tn ≈ 1.8 at end-2015. The numerator comes straight from the footnotes; the denominator has to be estimated separately and is the weak link.

WHICH FOOTNOTE. Under US GAAP the disclosure sits in the collateralised-financing note as two lines: collateral accepted that the firm is permitted to sell/deliver/repledge, at fair value, and the portion actually sold/delivered/repledged. Under IFRS the equivalent is IFRS 7.15. Verified locations and FY2025 values:
  • JPMorgan, 10-K Note 29 "Pledged assets and collateral": "Collateral permitted to be sold or repledged, delivered, or otherwise used $1,771.0bn"; "Collateral sold, repledged, delivered or otherwise used $1,426.4bn". FY2024: $1,544.0bn / $1,210.7bn.
  • Goldman Sachs, 10-K Note 11 "Collateralized Agreements and Financings", sub-heading "Collateral Received and Pledged": available to be delivered or repledged $1,312,079mn; delivered or repledged $1,119,825mn. FY2024: $1,038,740mn / $912,863mn.
  • Morgan Stanley, 10-K Note 6, table "Fair Value of Collateral Received with Right to Sell or Repledge": $1,190,694mn received; $900,282mn sold or repledged (footnoted as excluding securities used to meet federal regulations for the firm's US broker-dealers). FY2024: $932,626mn / $724,177mn.
  • Bank of America, 10-K Note 10 "Securities Financing Agreements": $1.1tn received, $1.0tn sold or repledged. FY2024: $925.7bn / $882.2bn.
  • Citigroup, 10-K Note 28: $1,064bn received. NO REPLEDGED FIGURE — Citi says only "a substantial portion". FY2024: $844.8bn.
  • Wells Fargo, 10-K Note 18 "Pledged Assets and Collateral": $469.2bn received, $309.3bn sold or repledged. FY2024: $288.7bn / $142.2bn.
  • Jefferies, 10-K (FY to 30 Nov 2025) Note 7 "Collateralized Transactions": $49.68bn received; repledged not quantified. FY2024: $37.63bn.
  • Nomura, 20-F (FY to 31 Mar 2026) Note 5 "Collateralized transactions", sub-heading "Collateral received by Nomura": ¥69,667bn fair value of collateral received; ¥56,304bn sold or repledged. FY to 31 Mar 2025: ¥64,853bn / ¥48,717bn.

WHICH BANKS. Singh names them in footnote 5 of WP/17/113: Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley, JPMorgan, Bank of America/Merrill and Citibank in the US; Deutsche Bank, UBS, Barclays, Credit Suisse, Société Générale, BNP Paribas, HSBC, Royal Bank of Scotland and Nomura elsewhere. Update that roster for 2026: Credit Suisse is inside UBS, RBS is NatWest and has largely exited, and Wells Fargo has entered — its collateral received grew 62.5% and its repledged amount grew 117.5% in FY2025 alone, the fastest of the group. Add Jefferies, BNY and State Street if you want the agent-lender side; add Mizuho and MUFG (both file 20-Fs) if you want the Japanese complex.

HOW TO PULL IT MECHANICALLY.
  1. EDGAR full-text search for the disclosure language, one query per phrasing family:
     https://efts.sec.gov/LATEST/search-index?q=%22permitted+to+deliver+or+repledge%22&forms=10-K&startdt=…&enddt=…
     and the same for "sell or repledge", "sold or repledged", "resold or repledged". These four cover every large US filer. Each hit returns {ACCESSION}:{PRIMARY_DOC} and the CIK.
  2. Fetch https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/{CIK}/{ACCESSION_NO_DASHES}/{PRIMARY_DOC}, strip tags, collapse whitespace, and regex the numeric pair out of the sentence or table. Set a User-Agent with a contact address or EDGAR will 403 you.
  3. For non-US filers, use the 20-F on EDGAR where one exists (Nomura CIK 1163653, MUFG 67088, Mizuho 1335730, HSBC, DB, Barclays, UBS all file); otherwise pull the group annual report PDF and search for "permitted to sell or repledge".
  4. Record the fiscal year-end with each observation. Jefferies is 30 November and Nomura is 31 March; you cannot build a clean same-date cross-section, so present the series as fiscal-year observations with the date attached, not as a calendar-year total.
  5. Keep each firm in its reporting currency. If you must convert, use a single stated spot date and say which.

WHAT THIS GIVES YOU. US-six pledged collateral received (Singh's numerator, C-060): $6,907bn at FY2025 against $5,574bn at FY2024, +23.9%. Reused, the five that disclose it: $4,756bn against $3,872bn, +22.8%, an 81.4% reuse rate on the corresponding source base. Nomura's reuse rate rose from 75.1% to 80.8%. Singh's own latest published figure (Central Banking, 12 January 2026) is roughly $13tn of pledged collateral globally with velocity around 2.0, and he notes JPMorgan and Barclays each offer about $1.8tn for reuse — which is consistent with JPMorgan's $1,771bn footnote, a useful independent tie-out.

TO GET SINGH'S VELOCITY AND NOT JUST THE NUMERATOR you need the primary-source denominator. Build it as: (a) hedge fund collateral pledged to prime brokers — no clean public source; the nearest are Form PF Section 2 aggregates published by SEC DERA and the OFR Hedge Fund Monitor, and the OFR's H2 2025 finding that hedge funds borrow $1,804.7bn net in repo; plus (b) the securities-lending on-loan stock from ISLA (EUR 3.9tn at 31 March 2026), which needs deflating to the portion actually delivered into dealer hands. This denominator is an estimate. Singh says so. Say so too.

DO NOT ADD THE FOOTNOTE FIGURES TO ANYTHING ELSE. The footnote sum double-counts across firms by construction — collateral GS repledges to Morgan Stanley appears in both firms' "received" line. That double counting is the signal if you are measuring chain length; it is a fatal error if you present the sum as a stock of securities.

==============================================================
SERIES 3 — WHAT OFR GIVES YOU, AND WHERE IT STOPS
==============================================================

WHAT THE U.S. REPO MARKETS DATA RELEASE GIVES. Daily, free, API-served, three segments and no more:
    GET https://data.financialresearch.gov/v1/metadata/mnemonics?dataset=repo        (164 mnemonics)
    GET https://data.financialresearch.gov/v1/series/dataset?dataset=repo&mnemonics=REPO-DVP_OV_TOT-P,REPO-GCF_OV_TOT-P,REPO-TRIV1_TV_TOT-P
Send Accept-Encoding: gzip or you get a binary blob. Response shape is {short_name, long_name, timeseries:{MNEMONIC:{timeseries:{aggregation:[[date,value],…]}}}}. Grammar: REPO-{DVP|GCF|TRI|TRIV1}_{OV|TV|AR}_{TOT|OO|B27|B830|LE30|G30|T|AG|CORD|O}-{P|F}. Preliminary at 3pm ET daily; -F finalised quarterly and never revised. TRIV1 excludes Federal Reserve transactions and is the one you want. History: tri-party from 22 Aug 2014, DVP and GCF from 7 May 2018.

WHAT IT DOES NOT GIVE. No sponsored breakout. No NCCBR. No haircut. No counterparty type. No collateral reuse. Tri-party has no outstanding-volume series, only transaction volume. If you want sponsored you must go to the Hedge Fund Monitor's separate FICC dataset (https://data.financialresearch.gov/hf/v1/series/dataset?dataset=ficc, two mnemonics: FICC-SPONSORED_REPO_VOL and FICC-SPONSORED_REVREPO_VOL, daily from 2 April 2020). If you want NCCBR you get nothing at all as a series — only the figures OFR chooses to publish inside briefs and blog posts.

WHY NCCBR HAS NO REUSE FIELD, PRECISELY. The OFR SFT-2 report carries 32 data elements, listed in Table 1 to 12 CFR § 1610.11(c)(3) and formatted per Appendix A of the reporting instructions. I have read the full list. It contains Security_Identifier, Securities_Quantity, Securities_Value, Securities_Value_at_Inception, Haircut, both counterparty LEIs, both internal identifiers, a Guarantee boolean, trade and settlement timestamps, rate, benchmark, spread, and a free-text notes field. It contains no field for whether the collateral posted was itself received in another transaction, no field for the collateral's provenance, and no reuse or rehypothecation flag of any kind. The only occurrence of "reuse" in the entire instruction document is a rule that a Transaction_ID "must never be reused".

WHAT THAT MEANS IN PRACTICE. Reuse inside NCCBR is inferable by OFR — match Security_Identifier across a single reporter's simultaneous incoming and outgoing legs on the same day — but by nobody else, and not even by OFR across segments, because the FICC and tri-party collections do not carry a comparable identifier the same way. The one time anybody did the cross-segment CUSIP match publicly is OFR Brief 24-07 (14 November 2024), using the 2022 NCCBR PILOT (nine voluntary dealers, three dates in June 2022) joined to the cleared and tri-party collections. That produced the 65% figure. It has never been repeated on the production data, even though the production data would support it.

USE 24-07 AS YOUR CALIBRATION, NOT AS A SERIES. Its numbers, to carry alongside anything you build: repo outstanding $1,355bn, reverse repo $927bn, reused $607bn = 65% of reverse-repo collateral; 44% of repo collateral originates in a reverse repo; Treasuries 69% / 58%, non-Treasuries 45% / 14%; 91% of all offsetting collateral volume is Treasury-backed; GCF and tri-party reuse rows are zero. Sample covered 17% of tri-party, 12% of DVP, 21% of GCF and about 40% of NCCBR.

THE THREE OFR BRIEFS FROM 2026 THAT CHANGE YOUR PRIORS. 26-01 (3 March 2026) on hedge fund participation in sponsored DVP. 26-03 (31 March 2026), "Who Participates in Repo?", the first entity-typed view of the complete market: H2 2025 daily average $12.5tn, money funds lending $2,927.5bn on net across 69 firms, hedge funds borrowing $1,804.7bn net across 189 firms, primary dealers $986.4bn net borrowers, G-SIB banks $472.4bn net borrowers. 26-04 (28 May 2026), "Affiliate Repo": $2.1tn — 16.6% of the whole US market — is intragroup, predominantly G-SIBs in NCCBR, with materially lower haircuts than third-party trades. Deduct that 16.6% before you treat the $12.5tn as arm's-length market activity.

==============================================================
SERIES 4 — SECURITIES LENDING CASH-COLLATERAL REINVESTMENT, THE GENUINE NEAR-MONEY LEG
==============================================================

TIER A, GLOBAL STOCK, FREE, WEEKLY. ISLA publishes a chart titled "Global Collateral On-Loan (Cash vs Non-Cash)" at https://www.islagroup.org/securities-lending-market-data/. The values are not in a downloadable file; they are embedded in the page's Chart.js configuration. Fetch the page HTML, locate the string chartdata_{id} = '{…}';, JSON-parse it, and read data.datasets where label is "Cash Collateral" and "Non-Cash Collateral", against data.labels for the dates. Units are EUR bn, weekly, on a rolling 26-week window — so you must harvest it repeatedly to build history. As at the week ending 26 March 2026: cash EUR 1,240bn, non-cash EUR 2,514bn, cash share 33.0%. Six months earlier (2 October 2025): EUR 1,173bn and EUR 2,150bn. Non-cash grew 16.9% over the half-year, cash 5.7%.

TIER B, US REGISTERED FUNDS, FREE, DERIVABLE TO THE DOLLAR. This is the only place a public analyst can compute reinvested cash collateral bottom-up. Procedure, which I ran end to end:
  1. Download https://www.sec.gov/files/dera/data/form-n-port-data-sets/{YYYY}q{Q}_nport.zip (~400–450MB; latest available is 2026Q2).
  2. Extract SECURITIES_LENDING.tsv, SUBMISSION.tsv, and — streaming, do not decompress the whole thing — columns 1 and 2 of FUND_REPORTED_HOLDING.tsv (ACCESSION_NUMBER, HOLDING_ID).
  3. Build HOLDING_ID → ACCESSION_NUMBER, then ACCESSION_NUMBER → REPORT_DATE from SUBMISSION.tsv.
  4. Sum SECURITIES_LENDING.CASH_COLLATERAL_AMOUNT and .LOAN_VALUE grouped by REPORT_DATE.
  5. Use only calendar-quarter-end report dates. Coverage inside a quarterly ZIP is wildly uneven: 2026Q2 holds 6,894 reports dated 31 Mar 2026 but only 417 dated 31 Jan 2026.
Result: at 31 March 2026, reinvested cash collateral $109.3bn against $149.5bn of securities on loan (73% of loans cash-collateralised on the funds' own books). At 31 March 2025, $96.8bn against $115.9bn. Year on year, cash collateral +12.9% and securities on loan +29.0% — the on-loan book is growing roughly twice as fast as the cash-collateral book, i.e. the mix is shifting to non-cash, which mirrors what ISLA shows globally. NON_CASH_COLLATERAL_VALUE is empty throughout, because non-cash collateral is held by the agent and is not a fund asset; N-PORT measures the cash channel only. Money market funds are out of scope entirely.

TIER C, WHO MANAGES THE CASH. Form N-CEN Item C.6.d names the cash collateral manager and its LEI whenever that entity differs from the lending agent, and C.6.c names the lending agent and whether it indemnifies against borrower default. C.6.f gives the monthly average on-loan value and C.6.g the net income. Parse the N-CEN XML off EDGAR and you have the agent-lender and reinvestment-manager concentration map for the entire US fund industry, annually, free. No dollar amount of collateral, only identities.

TIER D, US INSURERS. NAIC Annual Statement Schedule DL, Parts 1 and 2, "Securities Lending Collateral Assets", introduced in 2010, under SSAP No. 103 which splits collateral into on-balance-sheet ("may be sold or repledged") and off-balance-sheet ("may not be sold or repledged"). Schedule DL is a CUSIP-level listing of exactly what the cash was reinvested in, split into bonds, preferred stock and common stock. Aggregates are free at https://content.naic.org/insurance-topics/securities-lending: $41.9bn at year-end 2025 (0.4% of $9.6tn total cash and invested assets) against $34.1bn at year-end 2024, +22.9%; life $31.7bn, P/C $6.8bn, health $3.4bn, title nil. Filing-level Schedule DL needs an NAIC data licence.

TIER E, THE VEHICLES. FSOC's 2025 Annual Report, as summarised by NAIC, sizes the pools that absorb this cash at year-end 2024: private liquidity funds ~$358bn AUM, OCC/Fed-regulated bank-sponsored short-term investment funds ~$350bn, ultrashort bond funds ~$390bn. FSOC itself flags data gaps in these vehicles.

TIER F, THE FLOW. S&P Global Market Intelligence Securities Finance reports full-year 2025 global securities finance revenues of $14.906bn (+27% y/y), of which cash reinvestment revenues $2.293bn (+13%), on average on-loan balances of $3.100tn (+16%) against average lendable of $44.038tn (+15%), utilisation 5.5%. That is a FLOW and a vendor panel; do not confuse it with the stock.

WHY THIS LEG IS THE NEAR-MONEY ONE. Set out the balance sheet explicitly when you write it up. Before: pension fund holds bond X. After: pension fund holds bond X (still in NAV, still bearing its price and credit risk) plus a reinvested cash asset of 1.02–1.05 x X, against a liability to return cash. The borrower holds X and owes it back. The gross stock of short-term claims in the non-bank sector has risen by the full cash collateral amount, EUR 1,240bn globally, and it sits in exactly the instruments that behave as money substitutes. No deposit was created. Net worth is unchanged. It is a gross-up of near-money claims, not net new purchasing power — say both halves of that sentence.

==============================================================
SERIES 5 — SPONSORED REPO, AND WHAT IT DOES TO EVERY STATISTIC ABOVE
==============================================================

BUILD. Two mnemonics, free, daily:
    GET https://data.financialresearch.gov/hf/v1/series/dataset?dataset=ficc
returning FICC-SPONSORED_REPO_VOL and FICC-SPONSORED_REVREPO_VOL, 1,578 observations each from 2 April 2020, measured from the sponsored member's perspective and combining Sponsored GC (tri-party settled) with Sponsored DVP. Cross-check against the FR 2004C CBSP and TRISP venue components from Series 1, which give the same phenomenon from the sponsoring dealer's side.

WHAT IT SHOWS. Calendar-year means: sponsored repo (hedge funds borrowing) $142.2bn in 2022 → $264.4bn 2023 → $511.6bn 2024 → $911.2bn 2025 → $1,081.4bn in 2026 to date, a 7.6x rise in four years. Sponsored reverse repo (money funds lending) $191.0bn → $415.8bn → $728.2bn → $1,178.8bn → $1,397.5bn.

WHY IT BREAKS THE STATISTICS. Four separate effects, and you must handle all four.
  1. SEGMENT MIGRATION. A trade that was NCCBR becomes FICC-cleared. It leaves the segment with the deepest data (NCCBR, transaction-level with CUSIPs) and enters the segment with the shallowest public data (FICC, three aggregate mnemonics). Measured NCCBR shrinks and measured cleared grows with no change in economic activity.
  2. COLLATERAL IMMOBILISATION. The Sponsored GC leg settles in tri-party, where received collateral cannot be rehypothecated. Growth in Sponsored GC therefore MECHANICALLY LOWERS the reuse rate without any behavioural change. This is the single most important confound in any reuse time series post-2023.
  3. BALANCE-SHEET NETTING. Both legs novate to FICC and net at the sponsor, so a growing sponsored book shows up as flat or falling gross repo on the sponsor's own balance sheet — including in the Z.1 broker-dealer figures, which are reported net of ASC 210-20 offsetting.
  4. DOUBLE COUNTING IN OUTSTANDING VOLUME. FICC DVP outstanding volume counts both novated legs. Comparing it to a one-sided exposure measure overstates by roughly a factor of two.

THE REGULATORY CLOCK. The SEC's Treasury clearing mandate under Rule 17ad-22(e)(18)(iv)(A) and (B) has compliance dates of 31 December 2026 for eligible cash market transactions and 30 June 2027 for eligible repo transactions, after a one-year extension. FICC and, since December 2025, CME Securities Clearing Inc are the approved CCPs. Every reuse and multiplier series you build will have a structural break across those two dates. Build the break in now: run the multiplier separately for the DVP-plus-uncleared-bilateral subset (reuse-eligible) and the tri-party-plus-GCF-plus-Sponsored-GC subset (reuse-ineligible), using the venue components from Series 1. That decomposition is the only way to distinguish a genuine change in reuse behaviour from a change in where trades settle.

==============================================================
THE ONE SERIES THAT CANNOT BE BUILT
==============================================================
A published, ongoing, quantitative series for the stock of reused collateral in the United States, at any frequency and any lag. It does not exist. The FSB agreed the metrics on 25 January 2017 with national transmission to begin in January 2020; no global re-use series has ever been published, and the FSB's own February 2026 repo report still lists collateral reuse metrics in its DATA GAPS annex. Report that as a finding, not as a hole in your work.

## Double-counting risk

Seven overlaps. The first three are internal to this channel; the rest cross into other channels.

1. THE FOOTNOTE SUM DOUBLE-COUNTS ACROSS DEALERS BY CONSTRUCTION. If Goldman receives collateral and repledges it to Morgan Stanley, it appears in both firms' "collateral received permitted to be repledged" line. The $6,907bn US-six total is a count of CLAIMS ALONG CHAINS, not of securities. It is the correct numerator for a chain-length measure and a meaningless number if presented as a stock of assets. This is also precisely why Singh divides it by primary sources rather than reporting it as a level.

2. THE COLLATERAL MULTIPLIER AND THE REPO OUTSTANDING TOTAL MEASURE THE SAME SECURITIES TWICE ON PURPOSE. c = SO/(SO−SI) counts the same Treasury once for each time it is delivered. The OFR's $12.5tn is the cash value of outstanding commitments. Multiplying them, or adding a "reused collateral" estimate to a repo outstanding figure, counts the same economic event two or three times.

3. FICC DVP OUTSTANDING VOLUME COUNTS BOTH NOVATED LEGS. $3,387.6bn on 19 August 2026 in the OFR release is not comparable to the $4.4tn one-sided centrally cleared exposure in the OFR's own Q3 2025 sizing blog, which is built to avoid exactly this. Sponsored repo compounds it: the sponsor faces FICC on both sides of what is economically one client trade.

4. AFFILIATE REPO IS 16.6% OF THE US MARKET AND IS NOT ARM'S LENGTH. OFR Brief 26-04: $2.1tn of the $12.5tn H2 2025 total is between two entities of the same parent, predominantly G-SIBs in NCCBR, at systematically lower haircuts. It is internal liquidity management. It appears in FR 2004C, in the OFR aggregates and in the footnote figures. Any statement about market-wide funding should net it out, and any statement about a bank's collateral chain should not.

5. THE SECURITIES LENDING CASH COLLATERAL POOL REAPPEARS AS REPO. The EUR 1,240bn of cash collateral is reinvested substantially in repo, money market funds and short-term paper. That reinvestment shows up again on the other side of the repo statistics as a cash lender, and in money market fund AUM. Adding the securities-lending cash collateral to a repo outstanding figure counts the same money twice; the pool is a SOURCE OF FUNDS INTO repo, not a separate stock alongside it.

6. FR 2004C SECURITIES OUT AND THE FICC SPONSORED SERIES OVERLAP ENTIRELY. The CBSP and TRISP components of the dealer's securities-out figure ARE the sponsoring side of the same trades that the FICC-SPONSORED_* mnemonics report from the sponsored member's side. On 12 Aug 2026 the dealer-side sponsored figures were $338bn out and $477bn in; the sponsored-member side was around $1.05tn and $1.41tn. They are the same trades viewed from opposite ends, with the difference reflecting Treasuries-only versus all collateral and dealer coverage. Never add them.

7. CROSS-CHANNEL. This channel overlaps with (a) the hedge fund leverage channel — the $1,804.7bn hedge funds borrow net in repo is the same money as their Treasury basis positions; (b) money market fund and ON RRP channels — money funds' $2,927.5bn of net repo lending is the cash side of the same trades; (c) the FX swap channel — cross-border NCCBR of $1,160.8bn, a quarter of which is foreign-currency denominated, is partly the collateralised counterpart of dollar funding also captured in FX swap notionals; (d) any bank money creation channel — where a commercial bank is the cash lender in a reverse repo, the deposit it creates is already inside M2/M3 and belongs there, not here.

MEASUREMENT-BASIS INCOMPATIBILITIES, TO BE STATED EXPLICITLY EVERY TIME. Z.1 broker-dealer repo of $3,040.7bn is NET of US GAAP balance-sheet offsetting under ASC 210-20. The OFR's $12.5tn is GROSS cash value of outstanding commitments. ICMA's EUR 13,651.1bn is GROSS of both legs of participating firms' own books. FR 2004C is amounts of COLLATERAL by asset class. The footnote figures are FAIR VALUE of securities. The FSB's $16tn is deliberately ONE-SIDED. Six different bases. None of them is convertible into another without adjustments nobody publishes.

## What cannot be measured

Ten specific things. None of these can be closed with more effort; they are absences in the data, not gaps in the analysis.

1. THERE IS NO PUBLISHED COLLATERAL REUSE SERIES FOR THE UNITED STATES. Not weekly, not monthly, not annually. The 65% figure that circulates everywhere comes from OFR Brief 24-07: nine voluntary dealers, three days in June 2022, from a pilot collection. It has never been updated. The production NCCBR collection carries Security_Identifier and could in principle support a monthly CUSIP-matched reuse estimate; OFR has not published one.

2. THE OFR SFT-2 REPORT HAS NO REUSE FIELD. All 32 data elements are enumerated in Appendix A of the reporting instructions and in Table 1 to 12 CFR § 1610.11(c)(3). There is no field for collateral provenance, no rehypothecation flag, no indication whether posted collateral was itself received. Reuse in NCCBR is inferable only by OFR, only within NCCBR, and only by security-identifier matching within a single reporter's book.

3. NO CROSS-SEGMENT COLLATERAL LINKAGE EXISTS. The tri-party (BNY), cleared (OFR/FICC) and NCCBR collections were built separately and are not designed to be joined at the security level. The one time it was done — OFR 24-07 — required a bespoke merge of a pilot dataset with two production collections and a prorating assumption to allocate collateral across segments.

4. THE FSB'S AGREED GLOBAL REUSE METRICS HAVE NEVER PRODUCED PUBLISHED DATA. "Non-cash Collateral Re-Use: Measure and Metrics" was finalised 25 January 2017, with national authorities to transmit aggregated data to the FSB from January 2020. Nine years later, the FSB's own "Vulnerabilities in Government Bond-backed Repo Markets" (4 February 2026) still lists collateral reuse ratio, reuse rate and reuse share in its data-GAPS annex as metrics members should be collecting.

5. THE US SECURITIES LENDING TRANSPARENCY REGIME DOES NOT EXIST AND WILL NOT UNTIL 2029. SEC Rule 10c-1a took effect 2 January 2024; the SEC approved FINRA's Rule 6500 Series creating SLATE on 2 January 2025; on 3 December 2025 the SEC granted temporary exemptive relief extending the reporting date to 28 September 2028 and the public dissemination date to 29 March 2029. Until then, every US securities lending number in circulation is vendor data from S&P Global, DataLend or FIS, aggregated by a trade association, with no published reconciliation methodology.

6. INTRADAY AND TOKENISED COLLATERAL IS INVISIBLE TO EVERY SOURCE HERE. Singh's argument in Central Banking (12 January 2026) is that tokenised collateral settles within the same business day and therefore never appears in the end-of-day snapshot on which the 10-K and 10-Q footnotes are built. He quotes JPMorgan's own statement that intraday digitally pledged collateral settling instantaneously would not be reflected in the end-of-day figures reported in its 10-Q, and puts the intraday netting benefit at over $200bn per major bank. Every measure in this channel — the footnote, FR 2004C, the OFR releases, Z.1 — is an end-of-period stock and none of them sees this. As tokenised collateral platforms scale, the measured multiplier will fall while the economic multiplier rises.

7. FR 2004 EXCLUDES DERIVATIVES COLLATERAL AND THE UK BROKER-DEALER SUBSIDIARIES OF US BANK HOLDING COMPANIES. Michl and Park flag both. The second is the more damaging: London is where the right of reuse is least constrained, so the US-visible multiplier is a lower bound on the group-wide figure by an unknown amount.

8. FR 2052a IS CONFIDENTIAL. The Federal Reserve's own internal collateral multiplier (Infante and Saravay) is built from it. The public FR 2004C exercise is an explicit second-best. There is no route to the first-best.

9. TWO OF THE SIX LARGEST US DEALERS DEGRADE THE FOOTNOTE. Citigroup discloses collateral received ($1,064bn at FY2025) but declines to quantify the portion repledged, saying only "a substantial portion". Bank of America rounds to $0.1tn. Jefferies gives the source amount and says only that a substantial portion was repledged. So the reuse-rate numerator is unavailable for roughly $1.1tn of source collateral.

10. THE FIRST-CLASS QUANTITIES ARE OFTEN PUBLISHED ONLY AS TEXT INSIDE PDFs. NCCBR aggregates, affiliate repo, cross-border repo and the reuse benchmark appear only inside OFR briefs and blog posts, never as retrievable series. There is no API for any of them, no revision history, and no guarantee any of them will be repeated. Anything you build on them is a hand-copied constant, not a series, and should be labelled as one.

Two smaller ones worth stating. The NY Fed suppresses the FR 2004C all-maturity totals in a majority of recent weeks (33 of 53 in 2025 on the repo leg); this is recoverable from the components but only because the suppression rule is applied at the aggregate rather than the component level, which is an accident of the disclosure policy and could change. And the Fed's Z.1 repo book does not close: total federal funds and security repo liabilities of $6,757.1bn against assets of $8,341.1bn at 2026Q1, a discrepancy of −$1,584.0bn, which the release simply records as line FL902050005.

---

# Offshore dollar creation and the FX swap / forward / currency swap channel (eurodollar banking + off-balance-sheet forward dollar obligations)

**Verdict: `contested`**

## What it is

Two mechanically distinct things, routinely conflated, that must be measured separately.

(1) EURODOLLAR BANKING. A bank office located outside the United States books a USD-denominated loan and credits a USD-denominated deposit. Balance sheet before: nothing. After: bank has a USD claim, borrower has a USD deposit. This is ordinary double-entry deposit creation, but the issuing office sits outside the Fed's reporting perimeter. The Fed's H.6 M2 definition is M1 (currency + demand deposits + other liquid deposits) plus small time deposits plus retail MMF less IRA/Keogh — it contains no offshore component of any kind; M3, which did include eurodollars, was discontinued in March 2006. So this dollar deposit exists in no national monetary aggregate. It is captured, as a stock, only in the BIS locational banking statistics (LBS).

(2) FX SWAPS / OUTRIGHT FORWARDS / CURRENCY SWAPS. Balance-sheet mechanics of an FX swap: a Japanese lifer delivers ¥100 spot and receives $X spot, with a contracted reversal at a fixed forward rate. On the lifer's balance sheet the yen asset is replaced by a dollar asset; total assets unchanged; the forward obligation to repay $X appears nowhere on the balance sheet — only the (near-zero at inception) mark-to-market does. On the dealer's balance sheet, if the dealer credits a dollar deposit it has issued, the dealer's balance sheet grows by the spot leg on both sides. The notional in an FX derivative is, unlike an interest rate swap, an actual principal payment obligation — BIS states this explicitly (Avdjiev, McGuire, von Peter, BIS QR Dec 2025, p.65: in most FX derivatives the notional is the principal exchanged, "and thus an actual payment obligation").

An outright forward is different again and this distinction matters more now than in 2022: no principal moves at all until maturity. It gives a non-US investor a currency-hedged dollar-asset exposure with zero funding outlay. That is leverage, not funding, and it is the fastest-growing part of the market.

Currency swaps are a third instrument: multi-year, coupons exchanged throughout, principal at maturity. BIS aggregates them with FX swaps and forwards into the headline; Comotto's objection (below) is precisely about that aggregation.

## Is it money creation?

Decompose it. Summing the two would be wrong and inflating either would be wrong.

EURODOLLAR LEG — YES, this is money creation, and it is the part that answers the operator's puzzle. An offshore bank office extends a USD loan and simultaneously issues a USD deposit. Before: no claim exists. After: a new dollar-denominated bank liability circulates as means of payment. No pre-existing saving was intermediated. This deposit is in no published monetary aggregate anywhere — not US M2 (H.6 definition excludes it entirely), not euro-area M3 (which is euro-denominated and resident-based), not Japanese M3. It is measurable only as a stock in LBS and as a break-adjusted flow in LBS flow series. The current flow is large and accelerating: break-and-FX-adjusted cross-border USD claims of banks located outside the United States grew +$1.90tn in the four quarters to 2026-Q1, an 11.9% year-on-year rate — the fastest since the March-2020 spike and, before that, since pre-GFC. This is a real, primary-source, break-adjusted flow of new offshore dollar credit.

FX SWAP LEG — LARGELY NOT MONEY CREATION. Balance-sheet honesty requires conceding this. In an FX swap the borrower of dollars surrenders an exactly offsetting amount of purchasing power in the other currency. Richard Comotto (ICMA Centre, 22 Sep 2017, writing against the 2017 predecessor paper) makes the argument cleanly: in a repo the cash borrower keeps the risk and return on the collateral security AND receives cash, so purchasing power rises; in an FX swap the party "loses the purchasing power of the currency they sell", so leverage does not rise. He asks, in effect, where the debt is. That objection is correct at the level of a single closed pair. BIS's own current framing concedes most of the ground: the Dec 2025 BIS Quarterly primer describes FX swaps and forwards as instruments that "make money fungible across currencies" and reports that other-financial-institution positions "largely track their international portfolio allocation". That is a description of portfolio financing and hedging, not of net credit creation. The Dec 2025 Triennial analysis (Huang, Krohn, Sushko) is blunter still: the April 2025 surge was hedging demand — spot +42%, forwards +51%, options +108% versus 2022 — while FX swaps, the funding instrument, grew only 6% and interbank FX swap activity stagnated, with "no signs of dollar funding strains".

BUT THREE QUALIFICATIONS THAT KEEP THIS CHANNEL LIVE:
(a) The dollar leg has to come from somewhere. If the dealer creates it as a new offshore deposit rather than drawing existing reserves or MMF funding, the swap is the proximate cause of eurodollar creation. The LBS shows offshore dollar liabilities rising; the data cannot attribute that rise to swaps specifically, so do not claim it does.
(b) The currency surrendered may itself be borrowed. A yen-funded position that repos JGBs to raise the yen leg, then swaps to dollars, has created purchasing power — the creation happened in the yen repo, not the swap, but the swap is what redenominates it into dollars for deployment into dollar assets.
(c) Outright forwards require no principal at all. A non-US pension fund can hold a US asset, hedge the currency, and post only variation margin. That is dollar-asset demand generated with no saving and no funding, and it is exactly the leg that has grown fastest. It creates purchasing power for dollar assets without creating money. Neither M1–M3 nor debt statistics record it.

VERDICT: the headline "$113.5tn of dollar obligations" is NOT $113.5tn of new money. Most of it is intermediation, hedging and rollover. The genuinely creative parts are (i) offshore deposit creation, measurable at ~$1.9tn/yr and accelerating, and (ii) unfunded hedged-carry leverage in forwards, whose size is bounded but not measurable. Report those two; do not report the $113.5tn as money.

## Size

All BIS OTCD figures are STOCKS (notional amounts outstanding), net-net basis (adjusted for inter-dealer double-counting), reference date end-December 2025 (period code 2025-S2), data vintage 15 May 2026. All figures pulled live from the BIS SDMX API, not from memory.

STOCK — FX derivatives, end-Dec 2025:
- FX risk category, all instruments incl. options: $148.65tn (was $155.2tn at end-June 2025; the fall is real, see growth field)
- FX swaps + outright forwards + currency swaps ("forwards and swaps", instrument code C), all currencies: $129.30tn
- Same, with US dollar on one leg: $113.50tn (87.8% of the above). THIS IS THE UPDATED "$80tn-plus" HEADLINE. Borio–McCauley–McGuire's figure at end-June 2022 was $85.5tn on the same key; it is now $113.5tn.
  - of which outright forwards + FX swaps (cannot be separated — see gaps): $74.02tn USD leg / $83.30tn all currencies
  - of which currency swaps: $39.47tn USD leg / $46.00tn all currencies
- FX options, USD leg: $15.89tn. Excluded from the $113.5tn. Do not add.
- Maturity (all currencies, forwards and swaps): ≤1 year $98.1tn (75.9%); >1y and ≤5y $22.3tn; >5y $8.9tn.
- Counterparty (USD leg, forwards and swaps): reporting dealers $41.7tn; other financial institutions $60.2tn (of which CCPs $7.7tn); non-financial customers $11.6tn.

DERIVED STOCK — off-balance-sheet dollar debt of non-banks outside the US, end-Dec 2025: $35.9tn.
This is the BMM Graph 2.B estimator, reproduced exactly against their published number and then rolled forward. At end-June 2022 the identical arithmetic gives $26.05tn against their published $26tn. At end-Dec 2025 it gives $35.90tn.

STOCK — on-balance-sheet US dollar credit to non-bank borrowers outside the United States (BIS Global Liquidity Indicators), 2026-Q1, vintage 31 Jul 2026: $14.74tn (bank loans $6.68tn + debt securities $8.06tn). BMM's "$13tn" was this same series at 2022-Q2, where it reads $13.30tn.
So the off-balance-sheet / on-balance-sheet ratio has widened from 1.96x (mid-2022) to 2.52x (end-2025). That widening is the single most important number in this channel — and roughly a third of it is a coverage artefact, see below.

STOCK — eurodollar system (LBS, residence basis, foreign-currency view), 2026-Q1, vintage 31 Jul 2026:
- Cross-border USD liabilities of banks located outside the United States: $14.11tn (2000-Q4: $4.15tn; 2007-Q4: $10.10tn; 2024-Q4: $12.80tn)
- Cross-border USD claims of banks located outside the United States: $17.86tn (2024-Q4: $15.91tn)
- By parent nationality (LBS/N, includes offices inside the US): total cross-border USD liabilities of all reporting banks $19.52tn, of which US-headquartered $4.46tn, so non-US-headquartered $15.06tn; of the $19.52tn, $6.21tn is to related offices (intragroup).

STOCK — non-US banks' off-balance-sheet dollar obligations: BMM published $39tn at end-June 2022. I could NOT reproduce it and I will not roll it forward. See build_recipe step 6 and what_cannot_be_measured. The reproducible upper component, (a) = one half of global USD-leg forwards and swaps with all counterparties, is $56.75tn at end-Dec 2025. The two published adjustments that take it down to the headline are not specified precisely enough to replicate, and the arithmetic does not close against the OCC's published FX aggregate.

FLOW — turnover, April 2025, net-net daily average, Triennial Survey (final data, vintage 15 Jun 2026):
- Total FX turnover $9.5tn/day (revised down from the $9.6tn preliminary release of 30 Sep 2025)
- FX swaps $4.0tn/day (42% share, down from 51% in 2022); outright forwards $1.75tn (19%); spot $3.0tn (31%); options 7%; currency swaps 2%
- FX swap turnover by maturity: ≤7 days $2,725bn/day (68%); overnight $1,197bn/day (30%)
- USD on one side of 89.2% of all trades
- Non-market-facing trades (back-to-back plus compression) $1.2tn/day = 13% of total

FLOW — eurodollar credit creation, break-and-FX-adjusted (the only honest flow measure here), LBS: cross-border USD claims of banks outside the US, four quarters to 2026-Q1: +$1.90tn (quarterly: 2025-Q2 +$388bn, Q3 +$418bn, Q4 +$372bn, 2026-Q1 +$722bn).

STOCK IS NOT FLOW IS NOT TURNOVER — the discipline check. FX swaps plus outright forwards turn over $5.76tn/day. Annualised at 252 business days that is ~$1,453tn/year against an $83.3tn stock: a rollover ratio of 17.4x, i.e. an implied average residual life of roughly three weeks. Essentially none of that $1,453tn is new credit; it is the same short-dated stock being rolled. Any narrative built on FX turnover growth is a narrative about rollover frequency.

CONTEXT — the Fed is not currently backstopping anything: central bank liquidity swaps outstanding on the H.4.1 stand at $123 million, i.e. the standing weekly test operations only. No dollar funding stress is being observed.

**Growth.** Read these against a serious coverage break; the headline growth rate is materially overstated.

USD-leg forwards and swaps (stock, end-period): 2022-S1 $85.5tn → 2022-S2 $83.1 → 2023-S1 $90.5 → 2023-S2 $91.0 → 2024-S1 $99.1 → 2024-S2 $98.3 → 2025-S1 $113.9 → 2025-S2 $113.5tn. That is +32.7% over three and a half years, but +15.9% of it lands in the single half-year 2024-S2 → 2025-S1.

THE COVERAGE BREAK. The BIS semiannual OTCD collection covers 65 dealers in 12 jurisdictions. Every three years the Triennial Survey adds 30-plus jurisdictions and 1,000-plus dealers, and the BIS interpolates that extra segment between surveys. When the survey lands, the interpolation is replaced by observation and the series jumps. BIS states (commentary of 8 Dec 2025) that non-regular reporters accounted for 13% of FX derivatives notional in June 2025, and 11% of total notional against 9% in 2022. The sawtooth is visible and testable in the data:
  2018-S2 → 2019-S1 (2019 survey): +9.0%, then −6.2% in the next half-year
  2021-S2 → 2022-S1 (2022 survey): +5.6%, then −2.8%
  2024-S2 → 2025-S1 (2025 survey): +15.9%, then −0.4%
The 2025 jump is nearly double the two prior survey-year jumps and did not give back. The BIS publishes no decomposition of that jump into coverage and organic growth, and there is no reporting-jurisdiction dimension in the FX outstandings to let you build one (DER_REP_CTY takes only the value 5J, verified). So: state the +15.9% as "coverage-affected, magnitude of the break not published". Anyone who quotes 2025-S1 growth without that caveat is quoting a reporting change.

Cleaner underlying rate: from 2022-S1 to 2024-S2 (two comparable inter-survey stretches) USD-leg forwards and swaps grew from $85.5tn to $98.3tn, +15.0% over two and a half years, ≈5.7% p.a.

Derived off-balance-sheet non-bank dollar debt: $26.05tn (2022-S1) → $31.20tn (2024-S2) → $35.90tn (2025-S2). Same coverage caveat applies to the last step.

On-balance-sheet USD credit to non-banks outside the US (GLI, no coverage break): $13.30tn (2022-Q2) → $14.74tn (2026-Q1), +10.8% over 3.75 years, ~2.8% p.a., currently running +7.3% y/y. Note the shape: flat-to-falling 2022-2024, reaccelerating hard from 2025-Q2.
