  Rung 1, DIRECTLY MEASURED CREDIT: FINRA margin debt, $1,417,225mn, Jul 2026, monthly, gross debit balances.
  Rung 2, MEASURED CREDIT OUTSIDE THE FINRA PERIMETER: named-firm securities-based lending floor, $211.7bn across three firms at 30 Jun 2026, quarterly, bank loan carrying amounts.
  Rung 3, SURVEY-MEASURED BORROWING: Form PF qualifying hedge fund prime broker borrowing, $3,221bn at 2026-Q1, quarterly, self-reported borrowings, overlapping Rung 1.
  Rung 4, NOTIONAL EXPOSURE, NOT CREDIT: Form PF equity derivative notional $3,430bn at 2026-Q1 and BIS equity forwards-and-swaps notional $6,297bn at end-2025.
  Rung 5, UNMEASURED: internalisation, family offices, non-US prime brokerage of non-US funds, the collateral chain beyond first reuse.
The honest headline is a RATIO, not a total: roughly 41% of hedge fund equity exposure is derivative and therefore outside every credit aggregate; at one large firm the bank securities-based lending book ($116.4bn) now exceeds the broker margin book ($100.3bn); and the fastest-growing single number in the channel is bank lending to broker-dealers, +53.5% year-on-year to $541.8bn, which is the leg that actually creates deposits.

## Double-counting risk

This channel overlaps other channels and itself in five specific ways, and every one of them will produce a wrong number if summed.

INTERNAL — FINRA MARGIN DEBT vs Z.1 MARGIN ACCOUNTS vs FORM PF PRIME BROKER BORROWING. All three measure overlapping slices of the same credit on different bases. Z.1 L.216 line 35 ($662.1bn, 2025:Q4) is households and dealers only, and deliberately excludes hedge fund PB borrowing which Z.1 carries separately as FL624123035 ($774.0bn). Form PF's FPF-BORROW_PRIMEBROKER_SUM ($3,221bn, 2026-Q1) contains hedge fund debits that are ALSO inside FINRA's $1,417bn, plus borrowing against bonds and other collateral, plus borrowing from non-US prime brokers. FINRA + Z.1 + Form PF triple-counts the institutional core. Present them as a ladder with labels, never as a total.

INTERNAL — MORGAN STANLEY'S TWO DISCLOSURES. The firm's SBL loans ($116.4bn) and its margin loan receivables ($100.3bn) are genuinely disjoint at the firm level and may be added together for that firm. But the $100.3bn is ALREADY inside FINRA's $1,417bn, so adding both to the FINRA aggregate double-counts. Only the SBL leg is additive to FINRA. The same asymmetry applies to Schwab: Pledged Asset Lines ($33.4bn) are additive; receivables from brokerage clients ($122.8bn) are not.

CROSS-CHANNEL — REPO AND COLLATERAL REUSE. Morgan Stanley's $1,062,240mn of repledged collateral, the dealer repo book (Z.1 L.130 lines 3 and 20, $1,989.6bn assets / $2,827.8bn liabilities), and the securities lending market are three views of one collateral pool. Margin lending is FUNDED by repledging the same securities into repo and stock loan. Adding margin debt to repo outstandings counts the same securities twice — once as collateral for the customer loan, once as collateral for the dealer's funding of that loan. Whoever is covering repo and collateral velocity must be told which side of the line this channel sits on: margin debt is the ASSET the repo funds, not an independent addend.

CROSS-CHANNEL — BANK LENDING TO NONBANK FINANCIAL INSTITUTIONS. Z.1 L.130 line 23, bank loans to broker-dealers at $541.8bn, is the FUNDING of the margin and prime brokerage book, not additional credit to end-borrowers. It also appears inside the H.8 and Call Report "loans to nondepository financial institutions" aggregate that a bank-credit channel researcher will be building. Counting both the margin receivable and the bank loan that funds it counts the same dollar twice at two points on the same intermediation chain. State clearly that this line's value is DIAGNOSTIC — it identifies the deposit-creating leg — not additive.

MEASUREMENT-BASIS — NOTIONAL vs CREDIT. Form PF equity derivative notional ($3,430bn) and BIS equity forwards-and-swaps notional ($6,297bn) are notionals on gross positions; FINRA margin debt and SBL balances are credit outstanding; Form PF gross notional exposure sums absolute notionals across asset classes with no netting at all. These three quantities are not commensurable and no arithmetic combination of them means anything. The BIS figure is also on a net-net basis for inter-dealer positions between reporters, while the Form PF figure is gross from the fund's side — so even the two notionals are not on the same basis as each other.

FINALLY — VALUATION IS NOT MONEY, AND IT IS THE TEMPTATION SPECIFIC TO THIS CHANNEL. The mechanism here really is that rising prices expand a borrowing base. But the credit created is the loan, not the price increase. Do not express any part of this channel as a fraction of market capitalisation, and do not describe an increase in the collateral value of pledged portfolios as credit creation. Only the drawn balance is credit; undrawn capacity against a higher mark is not.

## What cannot be measured

1. INTERNALISATION. When a dealer writes a synthetic long to one client and a synthetic short to another and buys no shares, the netted amount is reported to nobody, in any jurisdiction, on any form. This is the largest single hole in the channel and it is not a data-collection lag — no regulator collects it. Everything published measures gross client positions or dealer balance sheets; nothing measures the offset between them. Any estimate of "how much financing the dealer actually provided" is therefore unfounded, and I will not produce one.

2. TOTAL SECURITIES-BASED LENDING IN THE UNITED STATES. No aggregate series exists. The Call Report's purpose-credit line (Schedule RC-C Part I item 9.b.(1)) captures only Regulation U purpose credit; non-purpose SBL — which is most of it, structured that way specifically to escape Reg U — is booked in undifferentiated "all other loans" and consumer categories. The only measurements are firm-by-firm 10-Q disclosures on unharmonised definitions. A named-firm floor is constructible; a national total is not.

3. TOTAL RETURN SWAP OUTSTANDING BY UNDERLYING, CLIENT TYPE OR FINANCING TERMS. Regulation SBSR real-time dissemination exists through security-based swap data repositories, but notional is capped for large trades and there is no public aggregate of outstanding stock by single-name underlying. The BIS semiannual survey gives a global notional for equity forwards and swaps combined, five months late, mixing synthetic prime with index and corporate hedging, from a reporting population of about seventy dealers. Nobody publishes initial margin levels on single-name equity swaps, so the credit embedded in that notional is unknown.

4. FOCUS PART II AND THE RESERVE COMPUTATION. Monthly and quarterly FOCUS filings, including Exhibit A debit item 10 (customer debit balances) firm by firm, are non-public under Rule 17a-5(e)(3). Only the annual audited Statement of Financial Condition is public, and mostly as PDF. So the firm-level composition of the FINRA aggregate is knowable only once a year and only with OCR.

5. FAMILY OFFICES. Exempt from Advisers Act registration under Rule 202(a)(11)(G)-1 and therefore file no Form PF, no 13F unless they cross thresholds on cash equity, and nothing at all on swaps. Archegos was a family office. Five years after it blew up, an identically structured position built today would be equally invisible. This is not an oversight in the data; it is a deliberate exemption.

6. LEVERAGE IMPLIED BY THE FINRA SERIES. Debit balances in Reg T accounts (50% initial margin) and in portfolio-margin accounts (FINRA 4210(g), risk-based, routinely 6:1 or better) are added together with no flag. The same headline number is consistent with very different amounts of underlying exposure, and no split is published.

7. NON-US PRIME BROKERAGE OF US EQUITIES BY NON-US FUNDS. Booked in London, Hong Kong or Tokyo, outside FINRA, outside Form PF unless the adviser is US-registered, and outside Z.1's domestic sectors. The BIS locational and consolidated banking statistics do not disaggregate equity finance.

8. THE COLLATERAL CHAIN BEYOND FIRST REUSE. The ASC 860-30-50 disclosure gives collateral received and collateral repledged at each dealer, but there is no way to net across dealers or to count how many times a single security supports a claim. Morgan Stanley's 76.8% reuse rate is a firm-level number; the system-wide multiplier is not published.

9. THE USE OF SBL PROCEEDS. Non-purpose loans are contractually not used to buy securities, but no filing records what they ARE used for. Whether the money bought a house, paid a tax bill, funded a private-market capital call or financed capex is precisely the question that would resolve the operator's puzzle, and it is nowhere on any form.

10. CORPORATE BORROWING BASE EXPANSION VIA EQUITY VALUATION. No series exists at any level of aggregation. Insider and affiliate pledging is disclosed qualitatively in DEF 14A footnotes under Reg S-K Item 403(b) with no loan amounts. Variable prepaid forwards appear in individual 8-Ks and 13D amendments. Holdco margin loans against strategic stakes are visible only when a 13D Item 6 discloses them. A count of disclosures is constructible from EDGAR full-text search; a dollar total is not.

11. SECURITIES LENDING, UNTIL SEPTEMBER 2026 AT THE EARLIEST. FINRA SLATE reporting was pushed from January 2026 to late September 2026, with public dissemination not until March 2027. There is no comprehensive US securities loan dataset today. Anything published before then covering the stock-loan leg is a commercial vendor estimate, not a measurement.

12. VINTAGE HISTORY OF THE FINRA SERIES. FINRA overwrites one file at one URL. There is no archive of prior vintages, so past revisions are unrecoverable unless you snapshotted the file yourself. Note in this context that the published series shows June 2026 at $1,502,072mn and July 2026 at $1,417,225mn, an $84,847mn single-month fall; that may be a genuine deleveraging or a restatement, and with no vintage archive there is currently no way to tell which.

---

# Stablecoins, tokenised money market funds and tokenised deposits

**Verdict: `creates_near_money`**

## What it is

Three structurally different instruments that get lumped together and must not be.

(1) FIAT-BACKED PAYMENT STABLECOIN. A bearer-transferable, non-interest-bearing, par-redeemable liability of a non-bank issuer, matched by a segregated reserve of cash, bank deposits, short T-bills, overnight repo/reverse repo and government MMF shares. Balance-sheet mechanics on issuance: holder debits its bank deposit and credits the issuer's bank deposit; issuer books a deposit asset and a token liability. Aggregate bank deposits are unchanged, ownership shifts. Then the issuer reallocates: if it buys a T-bill outright, the deposit passes to the bill seller and the bill leaves private hands; if it does overnight reverse repo, it lends the cash to a dealer and takes Treasury collateral, so the bills stay on dealer balance sheets. The two legs have opposite collateral-channel consequences and current disclosure conflates them. Under GENIUS (PL 119-27) §4(a)(1)(A) the permitted reserve set is exhaustively enumerated: (i) coin/currency/Federal Reserve Bank balances; (ii) demand deposits or insured shares at IDIs; (iii) Treasury bills/notes/bonds with remaining maturity ≤93 days or issued at ≤93 days; (iv) money received under repo where the ISSUER is the securities seller, overnight, backed by ≤93-day bills; (v) reverse repo, issuer as purchaser, overnight, Treasury-collateralised, tri-party OR SEC-registered-CCP-cleared OR bilateral with a creditworthy counterparty; (vi) shares of a registered government MMF invested solely in (i)–(v); (vii) other liquid federal assets by regulator approval; (viii) any of (i)–(iii) or (vi)–(vii) in tokenised form. §4(a)(1)(C) requires monthly website publication of outstanding token count plus reserve amount, composition, AVERAGE TENOR and GEOGRAPHIC LOCATION OF CUSTODY per category. §4(a)(2) bans pledge/rehypothecation/reuse except for margin on (iv)/(v), standard custody, and selling bills into repo of ≤93 days that is either CCP-cleared or pre-approved. §4(a)(11) forbids paying the holder any interest or yield. §17 declares payment stablecoins not securities/commodities and issuers not investment companies. §20 effective date: earlier of 2027-01-18 or 120 days after final implementing regulations; as of 2026-08-21 only NPRMs exist (OCC in FR 2026-03-02; FDIC Board approved 2026-04-07, FIL-11-2026, FR 2026-04-10), so the operative date is currently 2027-01-18. §3(b)(1) makes it unlawful for a digital asset service provider to offer a non-permitted payment stablecoin in the US from 2028-07-18. §18 carves out foreign issuers under comparable regimes.

(2) TOKENISED MMF. An actual fund share recorded on a distributed ledger. Two sub-species with completely different data regimes: 1940-Act registered 2a-7 funds (Franklin OnChain U.S. Government Money Fund / FOBXX-BENJI; WisdomTree Treasury Money Market Digital Fund / WTGXX) which file monthly Form N-MFP3 with CUSIP-level holdings; and offshore Reg D / Cayman-BVI vehicles (BlackRock BUIDL, Circle USYC, Ondo USDY/OUSG) which file at most a Form D and disclose nothing structural. These pay yield, which is precisely what GENIUS §4(a)(11) forbids stablecoins to do — so the legislative change mechanically pushes yield-seeking dollar balances out of stablecoins and into this category.

(3) TOKENISED DEPOSIT. A bank deposit liability with a ledger front-end (JPM Kinexys JPMD on Base and Canton; Citi Token Services). Legally and accounting-wise an ordinary deposit; already inside H.8, the Call Report and M1/M2. It is a settlement-rail change, not a new instrument.

## Is it money creation?

Answer in three parts, because the honest answer differs by segment.

PART 1 — FIAT-BACKED STABLECOINS (~$282.5bn, 91% of the stock): NO balance-sheet expansion, but YES near-money creation by asset transformation. Trace it. Before: household holds a $1 bank deposit; a dealer holds a $1 T-bill. After the household mints and the issuer buys the bill outright: household holds a $1 token; the dealer holds a $1 deposit; the issuer holds the bill against the token. Count the monetary claims. Before there was one deposit and one bill. After there is one deposit AND one token — two spendable dollar claims — and the bill has been absorbed into the issuer. Nobody's balance sheet grew, no loan was made, no saving rate moved. What happened is that a safe-but-not-spendable asset (a T-bill) was converted into a spendable one. The stock of MONEY-NESS rose while the stock of ASSETS did not. This is exactly the MMF mechanism Gorton/Pozsar describe and exactly the ZIRP-era pattern the operator is pattern-matching: purchasing power appears without a change in saving or a sale of other assets, because the transformation is in the liquidity attribute, not the quantity. It is invisible to M1–M3 because the issuer is not a depository institution and the token is not a deposit (GENIUS §2(22)(B) explicitly says a payment stablecoin is not a deposit and not a security).

But quantify the limb honestly, because it is smaller than the narrative: the transformation only occurs on the OUTRIGHT-BILL leg. On the reverse-repo leg the issuer lends cash to a dealer and takes collateral; the bill never leaves the dealer, no safe asset is monetised, and the issuer is simply a cash lender in the tri-party market — functionally identical to a government MMF, and already visible there. As of the 2026-07-31 N-MFP3, the Circle Reserve Fund — which holds 84% of all USDC reserves — was 88.0% overnight tri-party Treasury repo and only 12.0% outright Treasuries. So for the second-largest issuer, roughly seven-eighths of the marginal dollar is intermediation into dealer funding, not bill monetisation. The direct outright-bill position behind $73.3bn of USDC was about $7.18bn in the fund plus $150.8m held directly at 30 June 2026.

PART 2 — CRYPTO-COLLATERALISED STABLECOINS ($25.46bn, 8.2%): this IS credit creation in the loans-create-deposits sense. Sky USDS ($6.71bn), DAI ($4.76bn), Ethena USDe ($4.07bn), USDD, GHO, Falcon USDf and similar are minted as an advance against posted collateral. A borrower pledges ETH or a delta-hedged basis position and walks away with newly created dollar claims that did not previously exist as anyone's liability. This is the only segment in this channel that does what a bank does. It is $25bn. It is also partly recursive — BIS Papers 170 notes DAI and FRAX were partly backed by USDC and de-pegged when USDC did in March 2023 — so part of that $25bn is a second claim on the same USDC.

PART 3 — TOKENISED DEPOSITS AND TOKENISED MMFs: no new channel. A tokenised deposit created against a new bank loan is ordinary commercial-bank money creation, fully inside M2 and the Call Report; a tokenised deposit created by converting an existing deposit is a ledger migration. A tokenised MMF share is an MMF share — already counted in N-MFP and the OFR MMF Monitor.

FED STAFF POSITION: Jessie Jiaxu Wang, FEDS Notes, 2025-12-17, treats stablecoins as redistributing rather than creating, and models the credit consequence: if issuers hold reserves as bank deposits, aggregate deposits are unchanged but shift to volatile uninsured wholesale balances that attract higher LCR/NSFR treatment; if issuers hold Treasuries or MMFs instead, deposits leave the banking system. Her scenarios: $200bn shift with 50% recycled → $65–141bn loan contraction; $500bn with 20% recycled → $190–408bn; $1trn with direct Fed access → $600bn–$1.26trn. Note the sign: the deposit-displacement channel is contractionary for bank credit, the opposite of a funding boom.

BIS AR 2026 Chapter III frames the same point as failing the ELASTICITY test — stablecoins are constrained by a cash-in-advance issuance model and cannot expand supply to meet payment demand.

WHERE THE REAL UNMEASURED ELASTICITY IS: not issuance, but reuse. See the Singh point in what_cannot_be_measured and the collateral discussion below. A BUIDL or USYC share posted as derivatives margin is a T-bill fund share doing double duty as collateral while the underlying bills sit undisturbed in the fund. That economises on cash without creating money, and it is the mechanism most likely to be doing the work the operator is looking for.

## Size

STOCKS (all figures retrieved 2026-08-21, my own pulls unless noted):

The daily-series endpoint /stablecoincharts/all gives $309.24bn for the 2026-08-21 point. The ~$0.6bn gap between the two endpoints is a Llama artefact, not a revision — use one endpoint consistently.
- USD-pegged: $308.26bn = 99.49% of total. Non-USD in aggregate: $1.57bn (EUR $0.775bn, RUB $0.476bn, JPY $0.155bn, CHF $0.046bn, GBP $0.029bn, everything else rounding). BIS Papers 170 (May 2026) says ~98%; BIS AR 2026 Ch III says 99.4% of fiat-backed by market valuation. My pull is consistent with the BIS AR figure.
- By mechanism: fiat-backed USD $282.52bn; crypto-backed USD $25.46bn; algorithmic USD $0.27bn.
- Concentration: USDT $182.99bn, USDC $72.96bn, top two = 82.6% of the total. Next: Sky USDS $6.71bn, DAI $4.76bn, Ethena USDe $4.07bn, World Liberty USD1 $4.00bn, Global Dollar USDG $3.38bn, PayPal PYUSD $2.81bn, Ripple RLUSD $1.90bn.
- Peak of the series: $322.43bn on 2026-05-17. The stock is 4.1% below peak.

Reserve split at 2026-06-30: Circle Reserve Fund $61,917m (84% of reserves, avg yield 3.57%), bank cash $11,428m (avg yield 2.98%), plus $150.8m of directly held Treasuries. TOKENISED DEPOSITS: NOT PUBLISHED. No outstanding stock exists in any public source for JPMD, Citi Token Services or any bank-issued deposit token. J.P. Morgan's own Kinexys/JPM Coin pages publish no volume or balance figures at all. Circulating "$2–5bn daily" and "$1.5trn cumulative" numbers are press attributions to executive statements, are flows not stocks, and cannot be reconciled to any filing.

FLOWS:

- Prior 12 months, 2024-08-20 → 2025-08-20: +$107.74bn ($168.75bn → $276.49bn).
- Last three months: -$13.19bn from the 2026-05-17 peak.
- Circle reserve income Q2 2026 $667.7m; distribution and transaction costs $412.5m (i.e. ~62% of reserve income is paid away to distribution partners — the "narrow bank" earns a thin net spread).
- USDC on-chain transaction volume Q2 2026: $14.8trn, +151% y/y (Circle 10-Q). This is TURNOVER of a $73bn stock, not flow of new money.

CONTEXT FOR THE COLLATERAL QUESTION (Treasury MSPD Table 1, FiscalData API, primary):
- Bills held by the public outstanding: 2026-07-31 $6,988.2bn; 2026-06-30 $6,690.1bn; 2025-07-31 $5,995.0bn. Net bill issuance over the 12 months to 2026-07-31: +$993bn.
- Total stablecoin sector Treasury exposure, generously assembled (Tether Q1 2026 ~$141bn on the last date it was disclosed, including repo and indirect MMF exposure, PLUS Circle's ~$73bn of reserves): ~$214bn, ≈3.1% of bills outstanding. Circle's OUTRIGHT bill position alone at 2026-07-31 was $7.18bn = 0.10% of bills outstanding.
- Sector growth of +$32.75bn over the last 12 months absorbed at most 3.3% of the $993bn of net bill supply, and in practice far less because the marginal USDC dollar went into repo, not bills.

**Growth.** Decelerating hard, and currently negative. Stock of all stablecoins: +11.8% year-on-year (2025-08-20 $276.49bn → 2026-08-21 $309.24bn, +$32.75bn), against +63.8% in the preceding twelve months (2024-08-20 $168.75bn → 2025-08-20 $276.49bn). The series peaked at $322.43bn on 2026-05-17 and has fallen 4.1% (-$13.19bn) in the three months since. Base and period stated: DeFi Llama daily total circulating, all peg currencies, single-day observations not averages.

Issuer level, from filings: USDC in circulation +19% y/y to $73.3bn at 2026-06-30 (Circle 10-Q) — but USDC reserves FELL 2.5% over H1 2026 ($75.07bn at 2025-12-31 → $73.16bn at 2026-06-30), and the Circle Reserve Fund shrank from $66.47bn (2026-03-31) to $60.72bn (2026-07-31), -8.6% in four months. USDT roughly flat-to-down: ~$189bn early May 2026 → $182.99bn at 2026-08-21 on the Llama series, with $184.6bn issued per the Q2 attestation.

Tokenised treasuries/MMFs: +1.65% over 30 days to $16.15bn at 2026-08-21 (rwa.xyz). The two registered 2a-7 tokenised funds actually shrank over the same period: FOBXX $824.6m (2026-04-30) → $720.9m (2026-07-31), -12.6%; WTGXX $876.9m → $765.5m, -12.7%.

So the "fastest-growing category" framing is stale as of August 2026. The category grew explosively through 2024–mid-2025, and has been flat to contracting since May 2026. Any narrative that has stablecoin issuance funding a 2026 capex boom has to survive the fact that the stock is $13bn smaller than it was in May.

## Series

| Series | Publisher | Freq | Cost | Gives | Limitations |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stablecoin circulating supply, total and by asset | DeFi Llama | Daily | free | Outstanding STOCK by token, by chain, by peg currency, by backing mechanism (fiat-backed / crypto-backed / algorithmic), back to 2017-11-29. The only free daily universe-level series that exists. | Not a regulated source and has no revision log — series values change silently on methodology updates, so archive your own pulls with a timestamp. Its 'stablecoin' universe includes tokenised funds that are not stablecoins: at 2026-08-21 it counted USYC $2.922bn, BUIDL $2.722bn, USDY $2.151bn, YLDS $0.672bn, USD0 $0.551bn and rwaUSDi $0.433bn = $9.451bn of fund shares inside the stablecoin total. The snapshot endpoint and the daily-chart endpoint disagree by ~$0.6bn on the same date. Chain-level values can double-count bridged/wrapped representations. Circulating is sourced from on-chain contract supply, which is what the issuer has minted, not what the issuer has reserves against — reconcile to filings. |
| Form N-MFP3 — Circle Reserve Fund | SEC EDGAR | Monthly, month-end as-of | free | The single best primary series in this whole channel. Series S000077205, LEI 549300X6KEJFVQHDAG85, fund CUSIP 09261A870 (USDXX). Gives net assets, and every position at CUSIP level: issuer, title, investment category, final legal maturity, yield, value, % of net assets, daily/weekly liquid asset flags. Lets you compute the exact overnight-repo vs outright-bill split behind 84% of USDC reserves, and name every repo counterparty and size. | Month-end as-of dates only, so repo shares are subject to the usual month-end dealer-balance-sheet distortion — but since every observation is month-end the series is internally comparable. Covers only the fund, not the ~16% of USDC reserves held as bank cash. Filer CIK 0000844779 is the whole BlackRock Funds trust, so you must filter on nameOfSeries = 'Circle Reserve Fund'; BlackRock files multiple N-MFP3s on the same day for different series. |
| Form N-MFP3 — tokenised registered money market funds | SEC EDGAR | Monthly, month-end as-of | free | Exact net assets and CUSIP-level portfolios for the only two meaningful tokenised MMFs that are 1940-Act registered. 2026-07-31: FOBXX $720.9m (56.7% UST / 36.3% agency / 7.1% repo); WTGXX $765.5m (65.1% UST / 34.9% repo). | Captures only ~$1.49bn of a ~$16bn tokenised-treasury complex. N-MFP reports the FUND, not the tokenised share class — for FOBXX the whole fund is tokenised, but for other complexes you cannot separate the on-chain share class from the conventional one. Says nothing about which chains the tokens live on or who holds them. |
| Circle Internet Group 10-Q / 10-K | SEC EDGAR | Quarterly | free | Audited-basis USDC in circulation; balance-sheet line 'Cash and cash equivalents segregated for the benefit of stablecoin holders'; the reserve asset-class table with fair value and average yield split between bank Cash and Circle Reserve Fund; the % of reserves in the fund; reserve income and distribution costs; on-chain transaction volume; FDIC-insured amount vs uninsured cash. This is the only issuer in the channel subject to Exchange Act reporting and PCAOB audit. | Quarterly, so it lags the weekly reality by up to three months. Covers USDC and EURC only — roughly a quarter of the sector. Does not give CUSIPs (you get those from the fund's N-MFP). Reserve totals and 'USDC in circulation' differ slightly by timing/rounding — do not treat them as identical. |
| Circle weekly reserve disclosure and monthly attestation | Circle Internet Group / Deloitte | Weekly reserve disclosure; monthly third-party attestation | free | The highest-frequency reserve series any issuer publishes: reserve holdings broken into Deposits at Systemically Important Institutions, Other Bank Deposits, Overnight Reverse Treasury Repo, and <3-Month Treasuries — plus weekly MINT AND BURN FLOWS, which is the only genuine gross-flow series in this channel. Monthly attestation under AICPA standards asserts reserves ≥ USDC outstanding. | Category-level, not CUSIP-level (go to N-MFP3 for that). An attestation is not an audit — it asserts a point-in-time sufficiency, not the absence of intraperiod window-dressing. Weekly snapshots can miss intra-week peaks. No history archive is guaranteed; snapshot and store it yourself. |
| Tether quarterly attestation and reserves report | Tether / BDO | Quarterly | free | Total consolidated assets, total liabilities, excess reserves, gold tonnage, qualitative reserve description. Q2 2026 (as of 2026-06-30): assets $187,751,426,411; liabilities $183,641,897,215; excess reserves $4,109,529,196; USDT issued ~$184.6bn; >146 tons gold; secured lending down $2.38bn. | Disclosure QUALITY REGRESSED between Q1 and Q2 2026. Q1 gave a dollar figure for US Treasury and short-term exposure (~$141bn); Q2 gives none, describing Treasuries only as a majority of reserves. There is no split between direct bills, repo, and MMF shares; no maturity ladder; no CUSIPs; no counterparties. Reserves are consolidated across USDT, EURT and Tether Gold, so the USDT-specific backing cannot be isolated. It is an attestation, not an audit. Excess reserves halved quarter-on-quarter ($8.23bn → $4.11bn) with no reconciliation. Treat every Tether Treasury number as an upper-bound estimate of unknown composition. |
| BlackRock Circle Reserve Fund (USDXX) daily fund page | BlackRock | Daily | free | Daily NAV, total assets, holdings and yields for the fund holding 84% of USDC reserves. Circle's own 10-Q points readers here. Fills the gap between monthly N-MFP filings. | No downloadable history — you must scrape and store daily or you lose the series. Presentation format is not guaranteed stable. $2bn minimum-investment structure means Circle can and does move money in and out in large discrete blocks, so daily changes are lumpy and are not a clean proxy for USDC mint/burn. |
| Form D / Form D-A — offshore tokenised funds | SEC EDGAR | Annual amendment, irregular | free | Confirms existence, jurisdiction, exemption, number of investors and totalAmountSold. BUIDL at 2026-07-23: totalAmountSold $5,135,523,412, 28 investors, BVI, 'Other Investment Fund', offering amount 'Indefinite'. | TRAP: totalAmountSold is CUMULATIVE GROSS SUBSCRIPTIONS SINCE INCEPTION, not outstanding. BUIDL's actual NAV is ~$2.7bn against $5.14bn 'sold'. Using Form D as a stock series overstates by ~90%. No portfolio, no maturity, no counterparty data whatsoever. Filed at the fund's discretion on amendment, so the as-of date is the signature date, not a period end. |
| Tokenised treasuries dashboard | rwa.xyz | Daily | free | The only universe-level view of tokenised Treasury/MMF products: 87 assets, $16.15bn 'Distributed Value', 65,660 holders, avg 7-day APY 3.40% at 2026-08-21. Product-level sizes, chains, issuers. | Aggregator, not a primary source, and the project's primary-source rule means it should be a cross-check only. Its 'Distributed Value' vs 'Represented Value' distinction is undocumented and the two differ by orders of magnitude. Overlaps DeFi Llama's stablecoin universe by ~$9.45bn — never sum the two. Coverage of private/permissioned issuances is unverifiable. Historical series sits behind a paid API; the free view is a point-in-time snapshot. |
| Monthly Statement of the Public Debt, Table 1 | US Treasury Bureau of the Fiscal Service | Monthly, month-end | free | The denominator for every collateral-channel claim. Bills held by the public: 2026-07-31 $6,988,226m; 2026-06-30 $6,690,117m; 2025-07-31 $5,995,000m approx. Net bill issuance over the 12 months to 2026-07-31: +$993bn. | Par/outstanding amounts, not market value. Includes bills held by the Fed's SOMA, so for a free-float denominator you must net SOMA bill holdings off using the NY Fed SOMA holdings file. Monthly only, so it cannot be matched to daily stablecoin data without interpolation. |
| FDIC Forms PS-01, PS-01a, PS-02 (proposed) | FDIC | PS-01 and PS-01a weekly; PS-02 quarterly | confidential/regulatory | When live, this ends the measurement problem for FDIC-supervised issuers. PS-01 (issuers with ≥$1bn outstanding OR ≥$100m average daily transaction volume) carries Schedule D cash at depository institutions and Federal Reserve Banks, Schedule E US Treasury securities at CUSIP with fair value, maturity and coupon, Schedule F reverse repurchase agreements with collateral details, Schedule G money market mutual funds, Schedule H other instruments. PS-01a is an abbreviated weekly (Schedule C, aggregate reserve assets by type) for smaller issuers. PS-02 is quarterly, all FDIC-supervised issuers, reserve assets at fair value on Schedule B. | PROPOSED ONLY as of 2026-08-21. No data has been collected, no first as-of date is set in the notice, and whether any of it is published rather than held confidentially is undetermined. It covers only FDIC-supervised permitted payment stablecoin issuers — not OCC-chartered ones, not state-qualified issuers under the $10bn threshold, and not foreign issuers under §18. On current form Tether would fall outside it. Do not build a series plan that assumes this exists before 2027. |
| Ahmed & Aldasoro, 'Stablecoins and safe asset prices', BIS Working Papers No 1270 | Bank for International Settlements | One-off (published May 2025, revised June 2026) | free | The only identified estimate of the price impact of stablecoin flows on bills. Daily data Jan 2021–Mar 2026, local projections with a granular instrumental variable exploiting idiosyncratic token-blockchain variation. A $3.5bn inflow lowers the 3-month bill yield 0.71bp on impact, ~4bp at 10 days, peaking around 5bp at 13 days, with limited spillover to longer tenors. Effects strengthen in Treasury market stress. Notes ~$35bn of bill purchases by stablecoins in 2025. | Revised — cite the June 2026 vintage, and note that the earlier Cleveland Fed conference version (Aug 2025) carries different point estimates. The estimated elasticity is small enough that the 'price-insensitive buyer' framing is quantitatively marginal at current sector size. |
| Aldasoro, Frost & Ito, BIS Papers No 170; and BIS Annual Economic Report 2026 Chapter III | Bank for International Settlements | One-off, May/June 2026 | free | BIS Papers 170: the transaction-volume correction that kills the velocity narrative — adjusted stablecoin transaction value is about 1% of unadjusted (source Visa), and only 0.4–0.9% of the adjusted total is retail-sized (<$250). Also the reserve-composition-by-issuer chart (Graph 4, sourced to Kosse et al 2023 and company websites) and the eurodollar comparison: ~$14trn of offshore dollar liabilities booked by banks outside the US (BIS Locational Banking Statistics) against ~$0.3trn of stablecoins. AR 2026 Ch III: ~$320bn at end-May 2026, ~$28trn of 2025 transaction volume (<3 business weeks of large US wholesale settlement), 99.4% USD, and the singleness/elasticity/integrity framework. | Both are analytical publications, not maintained statistical series — there is no BIS stablecoin table you can refresh. The reserve-composition chart is built from company websites, inheriting every disclosure gap described above. The Visa adjustment methodology is proprietary and not reproducible from public data. |
| Wang, 'Banks in the Age of Stablecoins', FEDS Notes | Federal Reserve Board | One-off, 2025-12-17 | free | The Fed staff balance-sheet treatment and the deposit-displacement scenarios ($200bn/50% recycled → $65–141bn loan contraction; $500bn/20% → $190–408bn; $1trn with Fed master account → $600bn–$1.26trn), built on money multipliers of 0.6–1.26 including Kundu, Park & Vats (2025) $1 deposit → $1.26 lending. | Scenario arithmetic, not estimation on stablecoin data. Reserve composition figure is built from issuer transparency reports. Predates the 2026 NPRMs and the 2026 contraction in the stablecoin stock. |
| Singh, 'Collateral velocity is disappearing behind a digital curtain' | Central Banking / Risk.net (Manmohan Singh) | One-off, 2026-01-13 | paid | The reuse-rate argument. Velocity = total pledged collateral received divided by primary collateral sourced via reverse repo, securities lending and derivatives. Reuse rate roughly constant at ~2.0 for a decade; pledged collateral stock ~$13trn globally; JP Morgan and Barclays each around $1.8trn. His warning: tokenised platforms open repo in the morning and close it in the afternoon, so the activity never appears in end-of-day 10-Q figures. | Paywalled. The underlying reuse series is hand-collected by Singh from ~20 banks' quarterly and annual reports over 17 years and cross-checked with investor relations — it is not a published dataset you can subscribe to, and it cannot be automated. The ~2.0 reuse rate is a global aggregate with no stablecoin-specific decomposition. |
| GENIUS Act, Public Law 119-27 | US Government Publishing Office | Static, enacted 2025-07-18 | free | The statutory reserve taxonomy that will define every future data item: §4(a)(1)(A)(i)–(viii) permitted assets with the 93-day cap and the overnight constraint on both repo legs; §4(a)(1)(C) monthly website publication of outstanding count, reserve amount and composition, average tenor and geographic location of custody; §4(a)(2) rehypothecation ban with the CCP-cleared-or-pre-approved repo carve-out; §4(a)(3) monthly registered-public-accounting-firm examination plus CEO/CFO certification with 18 USC 1350(c) criminal exposure; §4(a)(4) capital, liquidity, diversification and deposit-concentration standards to be written by regulators; §4(a)(11) prohibition on paying holders interest or yield; §17 stablecoins are not securities or commodities and issuers are not investment companies; §18 foreign-issuer reciprocity exception; §20 effective on the earlier of 2027-01-18 or 120 days after final regulations; §3(b)(1) DASP prohibition from 2028-07-18. | Congress.gov blocks automated fetch (403) — use govinfo. The statute defines what must be disclosed on issuers' own websites, not what any agency will publish in aggregate. Implementing rules are at NPRM stage only (OCC FR 2026-03-02; FDIC approved 2026-04-07, FIL-11-2026, FR 2026-04-10; Treasury AML NPRM FR 2026-04-10 doc 2026-06963), so the operative disclosure regime today is voluntary issuer practice. |

## Build recipe

Build four separate series. Never one number. Everything below I have actually run against the live sources on 2026-08-21; the code paths work.

=== SERIES A: STABLECOIN OUTSTANDING (daily stock, universe level) ===

A1. Pull `GET https://stablecoins.llama.fi/stablecoincharts/all`. Each element has `date` (unix) and `totalCirculatingUSD`, a dict keyed by peg type. Sum the numeric values per date. That is your headline daily stock. Store every pull with a retrieval timestamp — Llama does not version.
A2. Pull `GET https://stablecoins.llama.fi/stablecoins?includePrices=true` for the cross-section. For each element of `peggedAssets`, sum `circulating` across peg types to get the token's outstanding, and keep `pegType` and `pegMechanism`.
A3. IMMEDIATELY partition the cross-section into three books, because they are three different instruments:
   - BOOK 1, fiat-backed USD payment stablecoins: pegMechanism == 'fiat-backed' AND pegType == 'peggedUSD', MINUS the tokenised-fund contaminants in A4. At 2026-08-21 that is $282.52bn gross.
   - BOOK 2, crypto-backed and algorithmic: $25.46bn + $0.27bn. This is the credit-creating book. Keep it separate and never merge it into Book 1.
   - BOOK 3, non-USD: $1.57bn. Report it, don't dwell on it.
A4. Remove the tokenised funds Llama miscategorises as stablecoins. Hard-code an exclusion list and re-check it monthly: at 2026-08-21 the contaminants are USYC ($2.922bn), BUIDL ($2.722bn), USDY ($2.151bn), YLDS ($0.672bn), USD0 ($0.551bn), rwaUSDi ($0.433bn) — $9.451bn total. The test for inclusion in the exclusion list is: does the instrument pay a yield or represent a fund share? If yes it belongs in Series C, not here.
A5. Reconcile the top two to filings, monthly. Llama USDC vs Circle 10-Q "USDC in circulation"; Llama USDT vs the Tether attestation "USD₮ issued". At Q2 2026 Llama's $72.96bn (21 Aug) against Circle's $73.3bn (30 Jun) and Llama's $182.99bn against Tether's ~$184.6bn (30 Jun) — the gaps are date, not method. If a gap opens beyond ~1% on a matched date, the Llama contract-supply reading has broken and you should switch that token to the filing.

=== SERIES B: WHAT THE RESERVES ACTUALLY ARE (the part everyone gets wrong) ===

This is where the analytical value is. Do NOT use issuer marketing pages.

B1. USDC — build a monthly repo-vs-bill decomposition, exact, from EDGAR:
   (a) `GET https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000844779.json` with a real User-Agent. Filter `filings.recent.form` startswith 'N-MFP'. BlackRock files many N-MFP3s per day for different series, so you must open each.
   (b) For each accession, `GET https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/844779/{accession with dashes stripped}/primary_doc.xml`.
   (c) Keep only filings where `<nameOfSeries>` is 'Circle Reserve Fund' (series S000077205, LEI 549300X6KEJFVQHDAG85).
   (d) Read `<reportDate>` and `<netAssetOfSeries>`.
   (e) Iterate `<scheduleOfPortfolioSecuritiesInfo>` blocks. Within each: `<investmentCategory>`, `<includingValueOfAnySponsorSupport>` (the position value — not `<principalAmountToNearestCent>`, which is absent in these filings), `<nameOfIssuer>`, `<finalLegalInvestmentMaturityDate>`, `<CUSIPMember>`, `<yieldOfTheSecurityAsOfReportingDate>`, `<percentageOfMoneyMarketFundNetAssets>`.
   (f) Bucket on whether `investmentCategory` contains 'Repurchase'. That gives you the split. Running this back through 2026 gives: Jan 68.1% repo, Feb 68.6%, Mar 62.1%, Apr 71.1%, May 67.2%, Jun 86.0%, Jul 88.0%. The regime shift in June 2026 is the finding.
   (g) The `nameOfIssuer` field on the repo lines gives you the counterparty list and size, which is a dealer-funding series in its own right: at 2026-07-31, Citi $9.67bn, FICC-WFBK $9.35bn, Barclays $9.00bn, GS $8.50bn, BNP $7.10bn, RBC $4.60bn, Mizuho $1.80bn, JPMS $1.80bn, DB $0.50bn, Nomura $0.25bn, Wells $0.15bn, CACIB $0.005bn.
   (h) Gross up from fund to issuer: from the Circle 10-Q, the fund is 84% of USDC reserves at 2026-06-30, the rest bank cash. So USDC's outright bill position ≈ (fund NAV × outright %) + directly-held Treasuries ($150.8m at 2026-06-30) ≈ $7.33bn in July 2026 against $73bn of tokens. Publish that ratio. It is the single most surprising number in this channel.
   (i) Cross-check the between-months path with Circle's weekly transparency disclosure (categories: Deposits at Systemically Important Institutions / Other Bank Deposits / Overnight Reverse Treasury Repo / <3-Month Treasuries) and scrape BlackRock's USDXX daily page for NAV.
B2. USDT — you cannot do B1. State the gap explicitly. The best available is the quarterly BDO attestation: total assets, total liabilities, excess reserves, gold tonnage. Record that Q1 2026 disclosed ~$141bn of "US Treasury and short-term exposure" and Q2 2026 (as of 2026-06-30) withdrew that line, giving only assets $187,751,426,411, liabilities $183,641,897,215, excess reserves $4,109,529,196. Carry Tether forward as a RANGE with a disclosure-quality flag, not a point estimate. Do not impute a repo/bill split from Circle's — Tether has never used the Circle structure and holds gold, bitcoin, secured loans and corporate bonds that Circle cannot.
B3. Everyone else (~$54bn of Book 1 outside USDT/USDC): no standard disclosure exists. PYUSD (Paxos) and USDG publish attestations of varying quality; USD1, RLUSD, USDGO do not publish anything comparable. Treat as unmeasured and say so.
B4. When Forms PS-01/PS-01a/PS-02 (OMB 3064-0225) go live, B1 becomes weekly and universal for FDIC-supervised issuers: Schedule E is CUSIP-level Treasuries with fair value, maturity and coupon; Schedule F is reverse repo with collateral detail; Schedules D/G/H are bank cash, MMF shares and other. Build the ingestion for those schedule names now; do not assume any data before 2027.

=== SERIES C: TOKENISED MMFs / TOKENISED TREASURIES ===

C1. Registered subset, exact, from N-MFP3 using the identical parser as B1: CIK 0001786958 filter nameOfSeries 'Franklin OnChain U.S. Government Money Fund'; CIK 0001859001 filter 'WisdomTree Treasury Money Market Digital Fund'. Gives net assets and full CUSIP portfolios. 2026-07-31: $720.9m and $765.5m.
C2. Offshore subset (BUIDL, USYC, OUSG, USDY, JTRSY): no portfolio disclosure. Take outstanding from on-chain token supply — Llama's peggedAssets entries, or read the token contract's totalSupply directly per chain, which is the more defensible route. Do NOT use Form D totalAmountSold: BUIDL's Form D/A of 2026-07-27 reports $5,135,523,412 cumulative gross subscriptions against a ~$2.7bn NAV.
C3. Universe cross-check only: rwa.xyz $16.15bn, 87 products, 65,660 holders at 2026-08-21. Flag it as an aggregator; do not put it in a table next to N-MFP numbers without a footnote.
C4. Compute and report the ratio C1/(C1+C2) — the share of the tokenised-fund complex with statutory portfolio disclosure. At 2026-08-21 that is roughly $1.49bn of ~$16bn, about 9%.
C5. Interpret C against GENIUS §4(a)(11): because permitted payment stablecoin issuers may not pay holders yield, the yield-seeking dollar balance has to sit in Series C rather than Series A. Series C growth relative to Series A is therefore a direct read on the regulatory-arbitrage margin the statute created, not an independent demand signal.

=== SERIES D: TOKENISED DEPOSITS ===

D1. There is no series. Do not construct one. J.P. Morgan publishes no JPMD outstanding balance, no volume figure on its own Kinexys/JPM Coin pages, and no line item in its 10-Q. Deposit tokens are not separately identified on the FFIEC 031/041 Call Report, the FR Y-9C, or the H.8 release. Citi Token Services publishes nothing comparable.
D2. The best honest statement is: tokenised deposits are ordinary deposits already inside H.8 line "Deposits" and Call Report Schedule RC, and their outstanding amount is unobservable at instrument level. Circulating "$2–5bn a day" and "$1.5trn cumulative" figures are press renderings of executive remarks, are FLOWS, and cannot be tied to any filing. Cite them only as claims with attribution, never in a table of measured quantities.
D3. If the operator needs an upper bound, the ceiling is the sponsoring banks' total deposits — which tells you the series is uninformative, which is the finding.

=== SERIES E: THE COLLATERAL-CHANNEL TEST ===

E1. Denominator: `GET https://api.fiscaldata.treasury.gov/services/api/fiscal_service/v1/debt/mspd/mspd_table_1?filter=security_class_desc:eq:Bills&sort=-record_date&fields=record_date,debt_held_public_mil_amt`. Values are $ millions, par, month-end. 2026-07-31 $6,988,226m.
E2. For a free-float denominator, subtract SOMA bill holdings (NY Fed SOMA holdings file, weekly) from E1. Do not skip this — SOMA is a large, genuinely price-insensitive holder and leaving it in flatters the stablecoin share.
E3. Numerator, done properly: use ONLY the outright-bill leg from B1(h) plus whatever Tether's attestation supports, and label the Tether component as an unverified upper bound. At 2026-07-31 Circle's verifiable outright bill position is $7.18bn = 0.10% of bills outstanding. A generous whole-sector Treasury exposure of ~$214bn (Tether Q1 ~$141bn including repo and indirect, plus Circle's full $73bn of reserves) is ~3.1% of bills.
E4. The marginal-buyer test, which is the question actually asked: change in stablecoin stock over 12 months divided by net bill issuance over the same 12 months. To 2026-08-21: +$32.75bn against +$993bn of net bill issuance = 3.3%, and that assumes 100% of the increment went into outright bills, which B1 shows it did not. The sector is not the marginal buyer of bills at present. It was a materially larger marginal buyer in the year to August 2025 (+$107.74bn), which is when the narrative was formed.
E5. Price impact: apply the BIS WP 1270 (June 2026 revision) elasticity — a $3.5bn inflow moves the 3-month bill 0.71bp on impact, ~4bp at 10 days, ~5bp peak at 13 days, front-end only. Scaling that to the 12-month +$32.75bn implies a cumulative effect in the low tens of basis points at the very front end, and none further out. Cite the revised vintage; the Aug 2025 conference version carries different coefficients.

=== SERIES F: THE REUSE / VELOCITY OVERLAY (Singh) ===

F1. Singh's metric is total pledged collateral received divided by primary collateral sourced via reverse repo, securities lending and derivatives; his estimate is ~2.0, roughly constant for a decade, on a global pledged stock of ~$13trn, with JP Morgan and Barclays around $1.8trn each.
F2. To rebuild the numerator yourself, go to the US GAAP collateral footnote under ASC 860-30-50 in each dealer's 10-Q: "fair value of securities received as collateral that the entity is permitted to sell or repledge" and "fair value of the portion that has been sold or repledged". IFRS filers give the equivalent under IFRS 7.15/7.42D. This is a manual, per-bank, per-quarter extraction across roughly the top 20 dealers — Singh does it by hand and cross-checks with investor relations, and there is no vendor product that reproduces it.
F3. His January 2026 point, and the one that matters for this project: tokenised platforms open a repo in the morning and close it in the afternoon, so the position is flat at the reporting instant and never enters the 10-Q footnote at all. The same blindness affects end-of-day tri-party statistics. Intraday reuse therefore raises true velocity while lowering measured velocity. There is no fix from public data — the only remedy would be intraday snapshots from the platform operator (Kinexys, Canton, Broadridge DLR), which are not published.
F4. Related and measurable-in-principle: tokenised MMF shares posted as derivatives margin (BUIDL and USYC are accepted at several venues) let one T-bill do duty as both a fund asset and margin collateral. No venue publishes accepted-collateral composition by instrument, so this is currently unquantifiable; the place to look if it ever is published is CFTC-regulated DCO risk disclosures and exchange collateral schedules.

=== SERIES G: TRANSACTION VALUE — HOW NOT TO USE IT ===

G1. Circle's 10-Q reports $14.8trn of USDC on-chain volume in Q2 2026, +151% y/y, against a $73bn stock. BIS AR 2026 Ch III reports ~$28trn of stablecoin transaction volume in 2025.
G2. Both are unadjusted. BIS Papers 170, using Visa's adjustment, finds adjusted transaction value is ABOUT 1% OF UNADJUSTED once high-frequency trading, intra-exchange volume and bots are stripped, and that only 0.4–0.9% of the adjusted total is retail-sized (<$250). BIS AR 2026 puts 2025's ~$28trn at less than three business weeks of settlement in the largest US wholesale payment systems.
G3. Therefore: never present on-chain volume as evidence of monetary activity, and never divide it by outstanding to get a velocity number. The Visa adjustment is proprietary and not reproducible, so the honest treatment is to report the unadjusted figure with the 1% caveat attached, or omit it.

## Double-counting risk

Seven specific overlaps. All of them are live, and several of them appear in published commentary.

1. DEFI LLAMA'S STABLECOIN TOTAL ALREADY CONTAINS TOKENISED FUNDS. At 2026-08-21 its $309.83bn includes USYC $2.922bn, BUIDL $2.722bn, USDY $2.151bn, YLDS $0.672bn, USD0 $0.551bn and rwaUSDi $0.433bn — $9.451bn of fund shares. Those same instruments make up more than half of rwa.xyz's $16.15bn tokenised-treasury total. Adding Llama's stablecoin figure to rwa.xyz's tokenised-treasury figure double-counts about $9.5bn.

2. THE CIRCLE RESERVE FUND'S REVERSE REPO IS THE REPO CHANNEL'S NUMBER. $52.72bn of the fund's $59.90bn portfolio at 2026-07-31 is overnight tri-party Treasury repo with named dealers. That cash sits inside tri-party volumes, FICC-sponsored volumes and the dealers' own funding statistics. Whoever on this project is mapping repo and collateral reuse will count it. Stablecoin outstanding and repo outstanding must never be added.

3. THE CIRCLE RESERVE FUND IS ALSO AN MMF. Its $60.72bn of net assets at 2026-07-31 is inside SEC N-MFP totals and inside the OFR Money Market Fund Monitor's government-fund AUM. Adding stablecoins to MMF AUM double-counts it. Same for FOBXX ($720.9m) and WTGXX ($765.5m) — these are conventional 2a-7 funds that happen to have a ledger front-end, and they have always been in the MMF numbers.

4. CRYPTO-BACKED STABLECOINS ARE PARTLY BACKED BY OTHER STABLECOINS. BIS Papers 170 notes DAI and FRAX were partly backed by USDC and de-pegged when USDC did in March 2023. A meaningful fraction of the $25.46bn crypto-backed book is a second claim on USDC already counted in the $282.52bn fiat-backed book. There is no public series decomposing the collateral, so the overlap can be identified but not sized.

5. TOKENISED DEPOSITS ARE ALREADY IN M2. JPMD and Citi Token Services balances are bank deposit liabilities sitting inside H.8, Call Report Schedule RC and M1/M2. Adding a tokenised-deposit estimate to a stablecoin figure and calling the sum "new digital money" double-counts conventional bank money. (Fortunately no such estimate is published — see what_cannot_be_measured item 1.)

6. TETHER'S RESERVES ARE NOT ALL TREASURIES AND ITS TREASURY EXPOSURE IS NOT ALL DIRECT. The Q1 2026 ~$141bn was "US Treasury and short-term exposure", a composite spanning direct bills, repo and indirect exposure via MMFs. Treating it as a direct bill position double-counts against repo statistics and against MMF AUM simultaneously. Its $187.75bn of assets also include gold (>146 tons), bitcoin, secured loans and corporate bonds, none of which are Treasury demand.

7. CROSS-CHAIN AND WRAPPED REPRESENTATIONS. A token bridged from Ethereum to another chain can be counted on both if the aggregator's methodology slips. This is why chain-level Llama data should never be summed independently of the token-level total, and why the token contract's canonical supply is the safer read for any single instrument.

AND THE ONE OVERLAP THAT IS NOT DOUBLE-COUNTING BUT LOOKS LIKE IT: a stablecoin liability and its Treasury-bill asset are two sides of the same position, and the whole analytical point of this channel is that BOTH are money-like. That is not an error to net out — it is the near-money creation. The rule is: count the liability in the money series, count the asset in the collateral series, and never add the two together into a "size of the channel" figure.

## What cannot be measured

Stated plainly, in descending order of how much it hurts.

1. TOKENISED DEPOSIT OUTSTANDING DOES NOT EXIST AS A NUMBER. Nowhere. Not in JPMorgan's 10-Q, not in the FR Y-9C, not in FFIEC 031/041, not in H.8, not in any Kinexys or Citi publication. Every figure in circulation is a press rendering of an executive remark, is a flow, and is unreconcilable. This is the single largest hole in the channel and it is the segment most likely to matter to a bank structurer.

2. TETHER'S RESERVE COMPOSITION IS UNKNOWN AND GOT WORSE IN 2026. The Q2 2026 attestation (as of 2026-06-30) withdrew the dollar figure for Treasury exposure that Q1 had given. There is no direct-bill vs repo vs MMF split, no maturity ladder, no counterparty list, no CUSIPs. Reserves are consolidated across USDT, EURT and Tether Gold so USDT-specific backing cannot be isolated. Excess reserves halved from $8.23bn to $4.11bn in one quarter with no reconciliation. Sixty percent of the sector is therefore backed by assets of unknown composition, which means the sector-level "stablecoins hold $X of Treasuries" statement — including the ones in BIS and Fed work — rests on issuer self-description.

3. NO US REGULATORY SERIES EXISTS YET. GENIUS takes effect no earlier than 2027-01-18; implementing rules are at NPRM stage; FDIC Forms PS-01/PS-01a/PS-02 (OMB 3064-0225) are proposed with no first as-of date and no statement that the data will be published rather than held confidentially. And their scope excludes OCC-chartered issuers, state issuers under the $10bn threshold, and §18 foreign issuers — which on current form means Tether. Anyone planning a series should assume nothing before 2027 and possibly nothing public ever for the largest issuer.

4. NO EU AGGREGATE. MiCA Article 22 requires EMT issuers to report quarterly to their competent authority; those reports are not published and no EU-wide aggregate of e-money-token outstanding exists. The EBA maintains a register of authorised ART/EMT issuers — a list of names, not amounts. So the second-largest regulated bloc has a comprehensive rulebook and no public number. (Consistent with BIS Papers 170's Graph 12 point that implementation of FSB standards is uneven — Stage 5 frameworks exist without Stage 5 statistics.)

5. NO HOLDER SECTOR, NO RESIDENCY, NO PURPOSE. You cannot tell whether a stablecoin balance belongs to a US resident, an Argentine saver, a hedge fund's margin buffer or a market-making bot. TIC does not identify stablecoin issuers as a holder sector, and issuers' bill holdings are attributed by custodial location, which for Tether means a US custodian and therefore likely misattribution in TIC. BIS Papers 170 resorts to Chainalysis corridor proxies for cross-border flows and Visa adjustments for retail share precisely because nothing better exists.

6. NO Z.1 LINE. The Financial Accounts of the United States (latest 2026:Q1, released June 2026) has no stablecoin instrument category and no stablecoin issuer sector, so issuers' asset holdings fall into rest-of-world or other-financial-business by domicile and the channel is invisible in the flow of funds. Verify against the current release before relying on this.

7. YOU CANNOT DECOMPOSE STABLECOIN GROWTH INTO ITS SOURCES. Given a +$32.75bn twelve-month increase, no public data tells you how much is (a) new dollars leaving bank deposits, (b) dollars recycling within crypto from sold tokens, (c) EM dollar substitution, or (d) leveraged positions in the perpetual futures complex. Circle's weekly mint/burn is the only gross-flow series in the channel and it covers one issuer. Without this decomposition the money-creation question cannot be answered empirically at sector level — only mechanically, as above.

8. INTRADAY TOKENISED REPO IS INVISIBLE BY CONSTRUCTION (Singh's point, and the one that most directly serves the operator's puzzle). A repo opened in the morning and closed in the afternoon on a tokenised platform is flat at every reporting instant, so it never enters ASC 860-30-50 collateral footnotes, never enters end-of-day tri-party statistics, and never enters the reuse-rate numerator. Tokenisation therefore raises true collateral velocity while lowering measured velocity. The gap is unbounded from public data. Nothing published today would let you detect it, and Singh's own ~2.0 reuse rate is hand-collected from twenty banks' quarterly reports and is not a purchasable dataset.

9. TOKENISED MMF SHARES AS MARGIN. BUIDL and USYC are accepted as derivatives collateral at several venues. No venue publishes its accepted-collateral composition by instrument, so the amount of tokenised-fund collateral doing double duty as margin — a T-bill working twice — is unquantifiable. This is a real collateral-multiplication channel with a zero-line data source.

10. THE OFFSHORE TOKENISED FUND COMPLEX HAS NO PORTFOLIO DISCLOSURE. About 91% of the ~$16bn tokenised-treasury complex (BUIDL, USYC, USDY, OUSG, JTRSY) sits in Cayman/BVI/Reg D vehicles that file at most a Form D. You get an outstanding supply from the chain and nothing else — no maturity, no counterparty, no repo share.

11. TRANSACTION VELOCITY IS NOT RECOVERABLE. Unadjusted on-chain volume overstates by roughly a hundredfold (BIS Papers 170, from Visa's adjustment), and the adjustment methodology is proprietary. There is no public, reproducible way to compute a defensible stablecoin velocity.

12. VINTAGE HAZARD. DeFi Llama has no revision log and silently restates history on methodology changes; BIS WP 1270 was revised in June 2026 with different coefficients from its August 2025 conference version; Tether's Q1 and Q2 2026 attestations are not comparable line-for-line. Timestamp every pull and cite the revision vintage or the numbers will not reproduce.

---

# Residual non-bank credit channels: non-bank mortgage (origination, servicing, warehouse and MSR financing), non-bank consumer credit incl. BNPL, captive/vendor and equipment finance, trade credit and reverse factoring, CRE debt funds, residential transition lending, and litigation finance

**Verdict: `contested`**

## What it is

Eight structurally distinct mechanisms, deliberately kept separate because they are measured on different bases and consolidate against overlapping collateral.

(1) NON-BANK MORTGAGE. An independent mortgage company (NMC, SIC 6162) originates a loan funded by drawing a WAREHOUSE LINE from a bank. Balance sheet: bank asset = loan to NMC; bank liability = new deposit; NMC asset = the mortgage; NMC liability = warehouse draw. The mortgage is then sold into a Ginnie/Enterprise pool, the MBS buyer's deposit is extinguished, the warehouse line pays down. The NMC retains the MSR — a capitalised intangible representing a strip of future servicing fees — and borrows against it via MSR lines (advance rate ~65%) and MSR-backed notes. The NMC also must advance P&I to bondholders on delinquent loans, financed from the same lines. So the standing balance-sheet footprint is bank credit to a non-bank, revolving.

(2) NON-BANK CONSUMER CREDIT / BNPL. Finance companies and fintech lenders hold consumer receivables funded by corporate bonds, CP, warehouse lines and ABS. BNPL specifically: the provider pays the merchant at shipment less a merchant discount and holds a ~6-week receivable on the consumer. Funding is overwhelmingly bank-provided warehouse plus forward-flow sales.

(3) CAPTIVE / VENDOR FINANCE. A manufacturer's finance subsidiary (Ford Motor Credit, Toyota Motor Credit, GM Financial, Caterpillar Financial, Dell Financial Services, HPE Financial Services, Cisco Capital) lends to the customer to buy the parent's product. The parent books revenue at delivery; the cash returns over the lease/loan term. The captive funds itself in the term debt and ABS markets. Structurally the vendor is financing its own revenue recognition.

(4) TRADE CREDIT (plain). Supplier ships and books a receivable; buyer books a payable. No financial institution is involved and no deposit is created. This is the largest single item in this brief and sits entirely outside every credit aggregate.

(5) REVERSE FACTORING / SUPPLY-CHAIN FINANCE. The buyer confirms an invoice on a funder's platform; the funder (bank or SPV) pays the supplier early, creating a deposit for the supplier; the buyer subsequently pays the funder. The buyer's obligation is legally novated to a financial creditor but is typically still presented as a trade payable, not debt. Combined with extended payment terms this both creates bank money and understates buyer leverage. This is the mechanism behind Carillion, NMC Health and the Greensill collapse, and is why FASB ASU 2022-04 (ASC 405-50) and the 2023 IAS 7/IFRS 7 amendments forced disclosure.

(6) CRE DEBT FUNDS. Closed-end or evergreen vehicles making whole loans, mezzanine and preferred equity against commercial property, funded by drawn LP commitments plus fund-level leverage (repo/warehouse from banks, CRE CLOs, subscription and NAV lines).

(7) RESIDENTIAL TRANSITION LOANS (fix-and-flip / bridge). Short-duration, business-purpose, non-QM loans to residential investors, originated by non-banks, warehouse-funded, then term-financed in 144A RTL securitisations.

(8) LITIGATION FINANCE. Non-recourse capital provided against a contingent legal claim, funded by permanent equity and private funds; the claim is not a receivable until judgment.

## Is it money creation?

Answer differs by mechanism. Most of this is intermediation. Two parts are genuine money creation and one part is neither — it is money economisation, which is probably the piece that actually answers the operator's puzzle.

GENUINE MONEY CREATION (bank loan → new deposit):
• Warehouse lending to non-bank mortgage originators, BNPL lenders, RTL lenders and consumer fintechs. The creating institution is the warehouse BANK, not the non-bank. When the line is drawn, a deposit is created; when the loan is sold into a pool and the line pays down, that deposit is extinguished and the position converts to intermediation of the MBS buyer's pre-existing savings. The standing drawn balance is therefore a permanent revolving money-creation footprint. This is directly observable: Z.1 S125s2.1.s line 19, FL613168005, "Depository institution loans n.e.c." owed by finance companies, $289.8bn at 2026:Q1 (2023: $235.3bn). That series is the best single proxy for drawn warehouse capacity in the whole system.
• MSR lines and servicing-advance facilities. A bank lends against an intangible; a deposit is created. Note the purpose though: proceeds mostly fund P&I advances to bondholders on delinquent loans. That is liquidity substitution, not new purchasing power reaching the real economy.
• Reverse factoring where the funder is a bank. The supplier receives a deposit that did not previously exist, against a claim the bank now holds on the buyer. Purchasing power is genuinely created and it is booked as "accounts payable."

INTERMEDIATION, NOT CREATION:
• Finance company and captive lending funded by corporate bonds and CP. Z.1 S125s2.1.s shows $1,207.3bn of debt securities funding $1,728.8bn of loans at 2026:Q1. A bondholder's pre-existing deposit is transferred to the borrower. No net new claim on the banking system.
• ABS issuance. The ABS buyer surrenders a deposit; the originator receives it. S125s1.3.s total assets $1,882.8bn are entirely intermediated.
• CRE debt funds and private credit funds funded by drawn LP capital. The FSB says this explicitly: private credit funds are excluded from the narrow measure because they are closed-end, low-levered and do not rely on short-term wholesale funding. (Fund-level NAV and subscription lines ARE bank credit and DO create money — but those belong to another researcher's channel, so I am not sizing them here.)
• Litigation finance. Permanent equity against a contingent claim. No money is created; a previously unmarketable asset is made tradeable, which is a liquidity innovation, not a monetary one.

NEITHER — MONEY ECONOMISATION (this is the important one):
• Plain inter-firm trade credit creates a claim without creating a deposit. The supplier's receivable and the buyer's payable net to zero across the corporate sector, and no bank balance sheet expands. But the transaction settles without money changing hands at the point of sale. A given stock of M2 therefore supports a larger volume of real transactions — the effect appears as higher velocity, not higher money. Z.1 F81.t shows net change in nonfinancial corporate trade payables running at $895.5bn SAAR in 2026:Q1, against $330.3bn in 2025:Q4, $407.8bn for full-year 2025 and $81.7bn for full-year 2024. Total net change in trade payables across all sectors was $1,160.6bn SAAR in 2026:Q1. If you are looking for capex being funded without a visible rise in savings or money, an inter-firm credit channel expanding at roughly a trillion dollars annualised is a materially larger candidate than anything in the fashionable private-credit coverage — and it is invisible in M1–M3 by construction, because it never touches a bank.

Caveats on that last number, stated plainly: it is one quarter at a seasonally-adjusted annual rate, it is volatile (the four preceding quarters were $691.0bn, $318.8bn, $291.2bn, $330.3bn SAAR), Q1 carries annual-report reclassification artefacts, and the Z.1 trade credit table carries a -$889.3bn discrepancy between total payables and total receivables at 2026:Q1 — about 9% of the stock. Do not present the single quarter as a trend. Do present the level: NFC trade payables $4,523.2bn at 2026:Q1 versus $4,063.5bn at 2025:Q1, +11.3% y/y, against nominal GDP growth well below that.

## Size

All figures state STOCK or FLOW explicitly. Do not sum across the bullets — see double_counting_risk.

US FLOW OF FUNDS, 2026:Q1, Z.1 released 11 June 2026 (all STOCKS, $bn, NSA, end-period):
• Finance companies (S125s2.1.s, ex-L.128) total financial assets 2,929.4. Loans 1,728.8 = consumer credit 705.0 + mortgages 317.5 + other loans and advances to business 706.4. Funded by debt securities 1,207.3 (open market paper 57.7, corporate bonds 1,149.6) and depository institution loans n.e.c. 289.8. Memo: consumer operating leases NOT in financial assets, 219.8 (FL613066303).
  — Trend is DOWN, not up: total loans 1,712.8 (2023) → 1,767.0 (2024) → 1,744.7 (2025) → 1,728.8 (2026:Q1). The boom is not in the sector the Z.1 measures.
• Issuers of ABS (S125s1.3.s, ex-L.127) total assets 1,882.8, versus 1,687.4 at 2025:Q1 = +11.6% y/y. Mortgages 1,136.6 (1-4 family 545.8, multifamily 74.4, commercial 516.5); other loans and advances 361.9 (includes domestically-issued CLO collateral); trade credit 59.2; consumer credit only 14.9; funding agreements with life insurers 291.0. Liabilities: CP 239.0, corporate bonds net 1,621.8, repo 22.0.
• Total mortgages (F4.5.s, ex-L.217) 21,677.7. Ginnie Mae pools 2,908.1 (FL413065015) vs 2,730.2 at 2025:Q1 = +6.5% y/y, the fastest-growing pool line. Commercial mortgages (F4.5c.s) 3,996.2, of which finance companies only 24.8 and mREITs 165.6 — there is NO debt-fund line.
• Home equity held by ABS issuers (FL673065123) 38.3, versus 6.2 at end-2023. Six-fold in three years off a tiny base; fastest-growing line in the entire mortgage table.
• Consumer credit (F4.3.s, ex-L.222) household liability 5,073.0. Finance companies 705.0, ABS issuers 14.9, federal government 1,604.2. By type: credit cards 1,276.3, auto 1,560.1, student 1,865.8, other 370.9.
• Trade credit (F81.s, ex-L.230): total payables STOCK 8,933.6; nonfinancial corporate payables 4,523.2 (2025:Q1 4,063.5, +11.3% y/y). Total receivables 9,822.8; NFC receivables 6,044.5; rest-of-world receivables 827.1. Discrepancy -889.3.
• Trade credit FLOW (F81.t, SAAR): total net change in payables 2026:Q1 = 1,160.6; NFC alone = 895.5. Annual: 2023 257.0, 2024 81.7, 2025 407.8 (NFC).
• Household-sector residual holdings of loans to nonfinancial corporate business (FL153069803) 131.0, versus 85.3 at end-2023. This is where the Z.1 parks private credit and debt funds it cannot see.
• Margin loans to households (FL663067003) 622.2, versus 397.9 at end-2023, +56% in three years.

US MONTHLY, G.19 Consumer Credit, June 2026 data, released 7 Aug 2026 (STOCK, NSA, $bn): total 5,119.0; depositories 2,082.9; finance companies 699.5; credit unions 720.6; federal government 1,605.1; nonprofit/educational 11.0. Revolving 1,305.6, nonrevolving 3,813.4.

SUPPLIER FINANCE / REVERSE FACTORING — measured directly from SEC XBRL on 21 Aug 2026 (STOCK):
• us-gaap:SupplierFinanceProgramObligation, union of frames CY2025Q1I–CY2026Q1I, latest value per CIK: 122 unique registrants, $74.9bn. Single-frame CY2026Q1I: 109 filers, $68.3bn. CY2025Q4I: 85 filers, $53.9bn.
• Largest: HP Inc 9.10 (FYE 2026-04-30), Walmart 5.90, Procter & Gamble 5.70, NextEra Energy 4.20 + 2.10 (two co-registrant CIKs — dedupe), AT&T 4.08, Mondelez 3.30, Genuine Parts 3.18, Dell Technologies 3.10, Target 2.80, Ingram Micro 2.61, Advance Auto Parts 2.50, Boeing 1.83, GM 1.30, Cigna 1.30, Coca-Cola 1.26, Arrow Electronics 1.20, Kimberly-Clark 1.00.
• FLOW, CY2025 annual: SupplierFinanceProgramObligationIncrease 26 filers, $48.9bn (P&G 17.13, Nike 11.50, General Mills 4.13, Novelis 3.25, VF 3.05, HPE 1.90); DecreaseSettlement 27 filers, $48.4bn. Only ~26 of 122 filers tag the rollforward, so the flow is drastically under-captured relative to the stock.
• This $74.9bn is a SUBSET of the $4,523.2bn Z.1 NFC trade payables, not an addition to it.

CAPTIVE AND VENDOR FINANCE — SEC XBRL us-gaap:NotesReceivableNet, frame CY2026Q1I (STOCK): 233 filers, $2,061.9bn in aggregate, but the tag is not a captive filter. Named captives: Ford Motor Credit 117.05, Toyota Motor Credit 101.00, GM Financial 87.86, Caterpillar Financial 32.64. TECHNOLOGY VENDOR FINANCING specifically: Dell Technologies 13.95 (2026-05-01), HPE 9.21 (2026-04-30), Cisco 6.58 (2026-04-25) = $29.74bn on-balance-sheet across the three. That is the measurable floor; vendor-arranged third-party financing and purchase/supply commitments are not in it.

MORTGAGE SERVICING RIGHTS — SEC XBRL, frame CY2026Q1I (STOCK, carrying value of an intangible, NOT credit): us-gaap:ServicingAssetAtFairValueAmount 96 filers $81.4bn (Rocket 19.38, Rithm 10.86, JPMorgan 9.09, Blackstone REIT 4.71, Annaly 4.12, Truist 4.11, PNC 3.82, PennyMac Mortgage Investment Trust 3.62); us-gaap:ServicingAsset (amortisation method) 53 filers $37.8bn (PennyMac Financial 10.15, Wells Fargo 6.22, UWM 4.59, Onity 3.03, loanDepot 1.70).

NON-BANK MORTGAGE FUNDING — FSOC Report on Nonbank Mortgage Servicing (2024), figures as of Q4 2023 unless noted:
• Warehouse: ~235 warehouse lenders, aggregate BORROWING LIMITS ~$285bn (a limit, not a drawn stock). Average NMC had lines from ~9.5 creditors. ~55% of lines uncommitted, weighted by facility limit, from the MBFRF subset whose facilities totalled $186bn. Advance rate 90–95%; Moody's assumes 93%.
• MSR lines: ~35 lenders, aggregate credit LIMITS ~$30bn. Advance rate ~65% per Moody's.
• Market share: 2022 NMCs originated 64% of purchase mortgages (HMDA) and held MSRs on 54% of balances (Inside Mortgage Finance, Q4 2022), versus 39% and 4% in 2008. Independent NMCs were sellers for 66% of Enterprise-securitised mortgages and issuers for 84% of Ginnie Mae mortgages in 2022, versus 10% and 14% pre-crisis. Non-banks service ~70% of the total Agency market.
• Leverage: of 550+ NMCs filing the MBFRF, only 37% had secured debt below 30% of gross tangible assets at Q3 2023; 35% exceeded 60%.

GLOBAL — FSB Global Monitoring Report on Nonbank Financial Intermediation 2025, published 16 Dec 2025, data as of YEAR-END 2024 (STOCKS, USD trn): total global financial assets 503.7; NBFI 256.8 (51.0%, +9.4%); banks 191.3 (+4.7%); central banks 34.1 (-4.2%); public financial institutions 21.5 (+3.1%). Narrow measure 76.3 (+12.7%, 15.4% of total). By economic function: EF1 58.1 (+15.1%), EF2 6.1 (+5.5%), EF3 4.9 (+5.8%), EF4 0.142 (+2.5%), EF5 5.3, unallocated 1.7 (+4.5%). EF2 is precisely this channel — finance companies, leasing and factoring. US contributed ~one third of global EF2 growth; US+India+UK hold 68.2% of EF2 assets. Germany -31%, Hong Kong -22.7%, Indonesia -12.6%. New 2025 granularity: of identified EF2 exposures, household non-mortgage loans 42.3%, NFC loans 40.4%, mortgages 16.9%, financial corporations 0.5%; loans average 66.7% of finance-company assets. Box 2: total non-bank exposure to real estate $4.4trn. NOTE A DEFECT: the FSB text says EF5 "grew 4.3% in 2024" while the Graph 1-6 table records 6.6%. Cite the table, flag the inconsistency.

BNPL — CFPB report, 13 Jan 2025, data year 2022 (FLOW, gross merchandise value, NOT a stock). Scaled to all six firms (Affirm, Afterpay, Klarna, PayPal, Sezzle, Zip): 401.9m applications, 277.3m originated loans, $33.8bn GMV. Matched-credit-record sample: 66.1m applications, 45.6m originations, $5.6bn GMV (Table 1). Underlying dataset ~145m applications 2017–2022. Approval rates 73% (2020), 78% (2021), 79% (2022); deep-subprime borrowers 45% of originations and subprime a further 16% across 2021–22. THE MOST RECENT AUTHORITATIVE US BNPL DATA YEAR IS 2022. There is no published outstanding stock. I will not construct one from GMV and an assumed tenor.

TRADE FINANCE — BIS CGFS Paper 50, 31 Jan 2014, data year 2011 (FLOW): US$6.5–8trn of bank-intermediated trade finance provided during 2011, of which ~US$2.8trn letters of credit (SWIFT MT700). ICC Trade Register: US$1,958bn, 21 banks, ~11% of global trade. Berne Union: trade credit insurance covered nearly US$1.7trn. BIS has not refreshed this estimate in twelve years and it predates the entire reverse-factoring build-out.

LITIGATION FINANCE — no official series exists anywhere. The only continuous primary disclosure is Burford Capital Ltd (CIK 0001714174), a US-listed 10-K filer: total assets $6,641m at 2025-12-31 (10-K), $4,270m at 2026-03-31 and $4,272m at 2026-06-30 (10-Qs); assets at fair value $5,864m → $3,353m → $3,357m. The ~$2.4bn Q1 2026 contraction is observed but I did not read the 10-Q and make no claim about its cause — read Note 1 and the consolidation disclosures before using it.

CRE DEBT FUNDS, RESIDENTIAL TRANSITION LENDING, MSR-BACKED NOTES OUTSTANDING: no published series. See what_cannot_be_measured.

**Growth.** Each with period and base stated. Note the divergence: the sectors the official accounts measure are flat, and the ones they do not measure are the ones moving.

MEASURED AND FLAT OR SHRINKING:
• US finance company loans (Z.1 FL614035005): $1,712.8bn (2023) → $1,767.0bn (2024) → $1,744.7bn (2025) → $1,728.8bn (2026:Q1). -2.2% from the 2024 peak. Consumer credit within it fell from $739.4bn (2024) to $705.0bn (2026:Q1), -4.7%.
• US consumer credit, all holders (Z.1 F4.3.s): $5,099.4bn (2025:Q4) → $5,073.0bn (2026:Q1), a fall. Y/y from $4,963.3bn at 2025:Q1 = +2.2%.
• Total household and nonprofit debt: +2.6% SAAR in 2026:Q1 per the Z.1 release commentary.

MEASURED AND GROWING FAST:
• Trade credit, the headline. Nonfinancial corporate trade payables STOCK: $4,063.5bn (2025:Q1) → $4,523.2bn (2026:Q1) = +11.3% y/y. FLOW (FA103170005, SAAR): FY2023 $257.0bn, FY2024 $81.7bn, FY2025 $407.8bn, 2026:Q1 $895.5bn. The 2024-to-2025 step is a 5x increase on an annual basis; the 2026:Q1 single-quarter SAAR is 2.2x the 2025 annual rate. Treat the quarter as a signal to investigate, not as a trend — the four preceding quarterly SAARs were $691.0bn, $318.8bn, $291.2bn, $330.3bn, which is very high variance.
• ABS issuers total assets: $1,687.4bn (2025:Q1) → $1,882.8bn (2026:Q1) = +11.6% y/y. Within it, commercial mortgages +13.0% y/y ($457.0bn → $516.5bn).
• Securitised home equity at ABS issuers (FL673065123): $6.2bn (2023) → $16.2bn (2024) → $32.7bn (2025) → $38.3bn (2026:Q1). Six-fold in three years off a small base, and +98% y/y from $19.3bn at 2025:Q1. Fastest-growing line in the whole Z.1 mortgage block.
• Ginnie Mae pools (FL413065015), the non-bank-issued segment: $2,730.2bn (2025:Q1) → $2,908.1bn (2026:Q1) = +6.5% y/y, versus +3.9% for US-chartered depositories' 1-4 family holdings.
• Depository loans to finance companies (FL613168005, the drawn-warehouse proxy): $235.3bn (2023) → $289.8bn (2026:Q1) = +23.2% over the period; +11.5% y/y from $260.0bn at 2025:Q1.
• Household margin borrowing (FL663067003): $397.9bn (2023) → $622.2bn (2026:Q1) = +56.4% in three years; +30.5% y/y from $476.9bn at 2025:Q1.
• Supplier finance program obligations, SEC XBRL: the level series cannot yet support a clean growth rate because filer counts are still rising with disclosure adoption (85 filers/$53.9bn at CY2025Q4I; 109 filers/$68.3bn at CY2026Q1I). The right statement is: adoption is still expanding, so the measured level is rising faster than the underlying exposure. Build a balanced panel before quoting growth.

GLOBAL, YEAR-END 2024, FSB:
• NBFI sector +9.4%, roughly double the banking sector's +4.7%. NBFI now 51.0% of total global financial assets.
• Narrow measure +12.7% to $76.3trn.
• EF2, this channel: +5.5% to $6.1trn — about half its 2018-23 annualised rate of 10.1%. So the finance-company channel is decelerating globally, consistent with the flat US Z.1 picture.
• EF5, securitisation vehicles: to $5.3trn. The FSB's own text says +4.3% and its Graph 1-6 table says +6.6%; cite the table and flag the inconsistency.
• EF1 +15.1% to $58.1trn, roughly double its five-year rate, and the only double-digit function.

## Series

| Series | Publisher | Freq | Cost | Gives | Limitations |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Z.1 table S125s2.1.s — Finance companies, stocks outstanding (formerly L.128) | Federal Reserve Board | Quarterly | free | Full balance sheet of US finance companies incl. retail captive finance companies and mortgage companies. Key series FL613066005 (consumer credit), FL613065000 (mortgages), FL613069505 (other loans to business), FL613168005 (depository institution loans n.e.c. — best proxy for DRAWN warehouse capacity), FL613066303 (consumer operating leases, memo, excluded from financial assets). | TABLE RENUMBERED from L.128 effective the 11 June 2026 release; series IDs unchanged. The whole sector is benchmarked to the FR 3033s Survey of Finance Companies, which is QUINQUENNIAL, and interpolated monthly by the FR 2248 Domestic Finance Company Report.
